# Testing and CI

The test suite and CI are designed for speed from the first commit. The rule that shapes everything here: **the blocking pull-request checks finish in under 5 minutes per OS on GitHub-hosted runners.** Anything slower runs in a tier that does not block.

## Test tiers

| Tier | What | Where it runs | Blocking? | Time budget |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **0. Static** | Formatting, lints, licence and source checks, generated-file freshness, repo rules (no home-directory paths, dependency list in sync, crate dependency rules). | Every pull request. | Yes | Under 2 min on a warm cache. |
| **1. Unit** | Pure logic inside each crate: parsers, decoders, mappers, policy evaluation, text layout, view functions. Property tests. Snapshot tests. | Every pull request, both OSes. | Yes | The whole tier in under 20 s of test time above the start of its processes on Linux, 25 s on Windows ("Budgets are enforced", below). |
| **2. Integration** | Several crates together, in-process, with the scripted provider, fake tools or real tools on a temporary workspace, the fake clock and the virtual terminal. The agent loop, sessions, context, permissions, the RPC conformance scenarios, the TUI against the virtual terminal. | Every pull request, both OSes. | Yes | Under 40 s of test time above the start of its processes. |
| **3. System** | The real binary as a process: headless runs against the scripted SSE server, the TUI under a pseudo-terminal, process-tree and sandbox behaviour, extension scripts and MCP fake servers. | Every pull request, both OSes. | Yes | Under 60 s of test time above the start of its processes on Linux, 90 s on Windows. |
| **4. Performance** | The budgets in [performance-budgets.md](performance-budgets.md). | Main branch after merge, nightly, and on request. | No | 10 min. |
| **5. Live** | Smoke tests against real providers. Fixture recording. One headless run of the real binary against Anthropic, and one against DeepSeek, since step 13b. | By hand: `cargo xtask live` and `cargo xtask record`, since step 14. No workflow runs them yet. Never on pull requests. | No | |
| **6. Soak** | Fuzzing of decoders and parsers, long scripted sessions for memory growth, release builds for every target. | Nightly. | No | |

Tiers 1 to 3 are separated by where the test lives and by nextest filters, not by cargo features:

- unit tests are `#[cfg(test)]` modules in the crate;
- integration tests are one test binary per crate, `tests/it/main.rs`, with modules inside it;
- system tests live in `cineris-cli/tests/system/` and in `cineris-testkit`-driven binaries, and their names start with `sys_`.

**One integration binary per crate.** Every extra test binary is another link step, and linking is the slow part on Windows. Doc tests are turned off (`doctest = false`); examples that must compile are ordinary tests.

**Only a `sys_` or `perf_` test starts a process** ([CONTRIBUTING.md](../CONTRIBUTING.md), "Test tiers"), and since the twelfth tooling step the test hooks enforce it. Every place in the workspace that starts a process marks the start with `cineris_base::hook::started`: the harness's git, the sandbox's process tree (every command of `cineris-tools`' runner), a pseudo-terminal's program, the test kit's children and the system tests' own starts. With the `test-hooks` feature, which every test build has (the test kit turns it on), and under nextest, the mark looks at the test's name (`NEXTEST_TEST_NAME`). When its last segment starts with neither `sys_` nor `perf_`, it writes this to the test's standard error and aborts the test's process:

```text
cineris test hooks: the test `verify::a_check` started `powershell`, and only a test named `sys_…` or `perf_…` may start a process (CONTRIBUTING.md, "Test tiers"; docs/testing-and-ci.md). Rename the test `sys_…`, which puts it in the system tier with that tier's 60 s limit, or give it a launcher, a script or a scripted runner that starts nothing. The test was stopped here so that it does not pass by luck under tier 2's 10 s limit.
```

- **Why an abort and not an error:** a refused start returned as an error is a failed command, which a test of a turn can take in its stride and pass. The test the rule was written for did exactly that: it started Windows PowerShell under tier 2's limit and passed, until a slow runner made it time out ([steps/flaky-test-fixes/answered.md](steps/flaky-test-fixes/answered.md)).
- **A version probe is refused where the harness decides to ask** (`hook::starting`, in `cineris-core`'s assembly of a shell), on the thread that decides: the probe itself runs on a thread of its own, which a test may end before or after it starts, and a refusal there would stop the test or not by that race.
- **What it found when it was turned on:** nothing, on Linux and on Windows ([steps/tooling-12/answered.md](steps/tooling-12/answered.md)). A start that does not go through a mark (a test's own `Command::new`) is not seen; the bare-program rule and review are what keep those few.
- **Outside nextest nothing is refused:** `cargo test` runs many tests in one process and cannot say whose start it is.
- **What it costs:** one read of an environment variable for each process a test build starts, and nothing in the product, where the mark is an empty function. It is tested end to end by `cineris-base`'s `hook::refusal_tests::sys_a_test_not_named_sys_is_stopped_at_its_first_start`.

**A system test that starts more than about 20 processes is Unix-only, or batches them.** A process start costs far more on a hosted Windows runner than on Linux: one test that ran git 70 times took 6.2 s there. Such a test runs only on Unix (`#[cfg(unix)]`, with what Windows loses said where the test is), or it starts fewer processes: one git with many paths, one shell that runs a script, a table of cases in one child.

- **A tool's test that Windows still needs is run there by the nightly.** The git-heavy system tests of `xtask`'s tools that run locally or on Linux CI are ignored on Windows by the blocking job (`#[cfg_attr(windows, ignore = "… the nightly runs it on Windows")]`) and stay blocking on Linux: `combine`'s, and since the CI-budgets fix the owner guard's, `rebase`'s, `branch`'s, `mutate --changed`'s, `git`'s and `rename`'s, 14.4 s of Windows' system tier on an 8 ms runner ([steps/ci-budgets/answered.md](steps/ci-budgets/answered.md)). The nightly's Windows job runs `combine`'s by their prefix and the others with `cargo xtask git-tests-on-windows`, which runs each by its exact name and fails unless each one ran and passed (a name that matches nothing would otherwise pass having run nothing). The names are `GIT_TESTS_ON_WINDOWS` in `xtask/src/nonblocking.rs`, and a unit test holds them to the tests that carry the reason, path for path.

**No real waiting in tests.** This is rule 12 of the [build plan](build-plan.md).

- Timeouts, retries, backoff, cooldowns, debouncing and anything else that depends on time passing are tested with time that is paused or simulated: the async runtime's test clock, which jumps to the next timer as soon as every task is idle, or the fake clock for code that takes time from the clock trait in `cineris-base`. A test of a 30-second timeout takes microseconds. The fake clock is defined in `cineris-base`, beside the trait, because that crate tests itself with it and cannot depend on the test kit; the test kit re-exports it. Its `sleep` returns at once, moves its time forward and records what was asked, so blocking code that waits and retries (the replace of a locked file) is tested for how long it would have waited.
- Production code never reads the system clock or sleeps directly. It goes through the runtime's timers or the clock trait, which is what makes the rule possible to follow.
- **Async code that also needs the date waits on a timer trait.** The retry layer of `cineris-llm` waits between attempts and also says when the next attempt is and when a cooldown ends, so it needs waits and dates from one clock. It takes its waits from a `Timer` trait and its dates from the clock trait. The harness gives it the runtime's timer. A test gives it the test kit's `FakeTimer`, made from the same `FakeClock`, in one of two kinds: **automatic**, where a wait finishes when it is first polled and moves the clock by its length, so code that waits and goes on runs straight through and the test reads back what it waited for; and **manual**, where a wait finishes only when the test moves the clock past its end, for a time limit that must not pass until the test says so. Neither needs an async runtime: the test kit's `block_on` runs such code on the test's own thread.
- **Two sides of a test take turns through a gate, not a pause.** Where things must happen in an order (the scripted server has seen the client hang up; a scripted response may go on), one side opens a `Gate` and the other waits for it. A blocking wait on a gate takes a bound, so that a mistake is a failed test and not a hung one. Where the library itself must stop at a place (between a read and a write), the library marks a point and the test holds it ([A point the library stops at](#a-point-the-library-stops-at)).
- **Wait for a gate. Do not assert that it is open,** unless your own thread is the one that opens it. Another thread opens its gate a moment after the thing the gate marks: the scripted server writes a piece and then marks it, so the piece can reach the test first. An assertion that the gate is already open passes almost every time and fails under load, which is how step 5 put a test on the main branch that the nightly run then failed. Asserting that a gate is still **closed** is sound only for a gate the test itself opens.
- **Never rely on closing a socket from one thread to wake a read that is waiting in another.** Linux wakes it. Windows does not promise to, and a test that joins the reading thread then hangs there, some of the time. A thread that reads a socket and must be stoppable gives its read a short timeout and asks, each time it runs out, whether it has been told to stop. That is how the scripted server stops (`crates/cineris-testkit/src/server.rs`): the interval bounds how long stopping can take, and no test waits on it.
- A test never calls `std::thread::sleep`. Clippy's disallowed-methods list bans it everywhere, tests included.
- Tier 3 cannot pause time, because the code under test is another process. It waits on a condition with a deadline (output seen, file present, process gone), never on a fixed delay, and the helper for that is in `cineris-testkit`.
- **A process test's children are the test binary itself, run again** (`cineris_testkit::children`, since step 11). A test binary has one entry test, which passes at once under the test runner, and becomes a child when a test starts the binary again with a child's specification: one that stays until it is killed, one that starts a grandchild, one that the polite interrupt does not stop, one that exits with a code, one that prints its arguments back; and since step 28 one that says a word on its standard error, a grandchild started without a standard output, and one that writes a given number of numbered bytes to each of its two output streams (`streams_arguments`, `stream_bytes`). Each child connects to the test's `Roll` on the loopback interface and keeps the connection until it dies. So "the process is up" is a connection that arrives, and "the process is gone" is that connection closed by the system: neither is a guess at how long something takes. No helper program is built, so there is no second link step, and no test runs `sleep`.
- **A test in a process of its own** (`cineris_testkit::rerun`, since the third tooling step). How the harness finds a program depends on its process's `PATH` and working directory, which a test cannot change: the tests of one binary share them. Such a test runs itself again as a child, with `Rerun::this_test().path([…]).cwd(dir).env(name, value).run()`; in the child, `rerun::is_child()` holds, the test does the search the harness does and reports with `rerun::say(key, value)`, and the parent reads it back with `said(key)`. A rerun whose name matches no test, which would pass having run nothing, fails. Step 13a's tests of a relative `PATH` entry used to make a path relative to the test's own working directory by hand; two of them now run in a child whose working directory holds the planted program, and a third, of the session store's git, turned a break that could only survive into one a test catches.
- **A call that should end by itself is given a bound.** The shell tool's tests run such a call with nobody to end a wait for it. If the runner then waits for something that is not there (a grace for a process the command did not leave), the wait never ends on a hand-driven timer, and the test used to be stopped a minute later with nothing to read. The bench now lets every wait go after its limit and fails the test with the waits the runner was in.
- **The process runner waits on a timer trait,** like the retry layer. Its tests give it a timer whose waits end when the test ends them, by their length: the time limit of a call, the grace between two stages of stopping, the grace for what a command left running. A test of a two-minute time limit takes as long as the processes take to die. A call that does not end by itself runs on the test's thread while a director on a second thread waits for children and ends waits, and a director that fails lets every wait go, so that a failed assertion is a failed test and not a hung one.

**Budgets are enforced.** This is rule 13 of the build plan.

| Tier | A test is reported as slow at | A test is stopped, and fails, at | The tier's tests together |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1. Unit | 2 s | 10 s | 20 s on Linux, 25 s on Windows (since the owner's decision of 2026-11-20, in the small fixes; [steps/small-fixes/answered.md](steps/small-fixes/answered.md)) |
| 2. Integration | 2 s | 10 s | 40 s |
| 3. System | 10 s | 60 s | 60 s on Linux, 90 s on Windows |

- The limits on one test are nextest's `slow-timeout` and `terminate-after`, set per tier in `.config/nextest.toml`. They hold in every profile, locally as in CI, except `properties`, the nightly run with fifty times the property cases, whose limits are scaled for it ("Property tests", below).
- The tier totals are the sum of the tests' own times, not the wall time of the run. `cargo xtask ci test` reads them from nextest's JUnit report after the run, prints them with the share of the budget used, and writes them to the CI job's summary page.
- **Each tier is measured under its own load** (the owner's decision of 2026-11-15). The blocking test job is two runs of nextest: tiers 1 and 2 first (`-E 'not test(/(^|::)sys_/)'`), and then the system tests alone, three at a time (the `ci` profile's `system` group). One run held the system tests two at a time beside the rest from the start, so the integration tier was measured under the processes, pseudo-terminals and children of tier 3: on one Windows runner `main`'s integration tier took 27.4 s in the job and 21.2 s alone. An experiment of the fifth tooling step (`try` run 43665893748, five rounds of each layout on one runner of each OS) gave Windows' integration tier 17.8 s split against 22.5 s, 21% less, and its unit tier 8.6 s against 10.3 s, with the system tier unchanged (54.5 s against 53.9 s) and the job 1.7 s longer; with four system tests at a time they slowed each other (12.5 s more of the system tier). The budgets were not changed.
  - **The two runs leave one report.** The first's JUnit report is set aside and merged with the second's (`xtask/src/junit.rs`, `merge`), at `target/nextest/ci/junit.xml`, where one run left its own, so the tier budgets, `ci --tiers`, `ci-times` and the kept artifact read one report as before. The log shows two steps, `run tiers 1 and 2` and `run the system tests`, each with its time; both run when the first fails.
  - **`ci --tiers` and `ab` run the job as it is,** in two. A filterset given to them (`-- -E 'package(cineris-tui)'`) narrows each run, joined with `and` to the run's own: nextest takes a test that matches any of its filtersets, so given beside it, it would run the other tier's tests too. `ab` runs each tree as that tree's own job does: a tree whose `.config/nextest.toml` has no `system` group is from before the split, and is run in one, so that an A/B of a change to the layout compares the two jobs.
  - **On Windows the system tests ran two at a time** (the owner's decision, amended on 2026-11-15 in the sixth tooling step; [steps/tooling-6/answered.md](steps/tooling-6/answered.md)). Most of Windows' system tests start PowerShell, whose start cost 290 ms alone and 440 ms with three at once on one runner (`try` run 43713246255). The same tree read its system tier at 57.6 s with three at a time and 46.6 s with two, five rounds of each on one runner, with tiers 1 and 2 unchanged; the system run's wall time went from 20.1 to 24.2 s (`try` 43715198613). With the step's test changes (93.2 s on `main` against 80.4 s, on one runner, `try` 43715195861) that brings the tier near 65 s on a 10 ms runner, where a 12 ms runner had failed the budget. In `.config/nextest.toml` it is the group `system-windows`, chosen by an override for `cfg(windows)` before the `ci` profile's own; Linux stays at three, and the line `system = { max-threads = 3 }`, which `xtask` reads to know a tree's job is two runs, stays as it is. **Since the ninth tooling step they run in two lanes of one test each** ([steps/tooling-9/answered.md](steps/tooling-9/answered.md)): the system tests of `cineris-core`, `cineris-tools` and `cineris-policy`, which start PowerShell for most of their tests, one at a time in the group `system-windows-shells`, and the rest one at a time in `system-windows`, beside them. Two tests still run at once, and two PowerShell starts meet less often (`cineris-cli`'s tests, in the other lane, start Windows PowerShell too). On one runner, five rounds of each, the tier read 57.8 s at the median where two at a time read 64.4 s (−10%), its rounds 57 to 62 s where the other's were 58 to 74, and the run's wall time 30.4 s where it was 32.2 s (`try` 43888750300).
  - **On Windows tiers 1 and 2 run two at a time** (three from the owner's decision of 2026-11-16, in the seventh tooling step; [steps/tooling-7/answered.md](steps/tooling-7/answered.md)). With four test processes at once on a 4-core runner, the tests slowed each other: on one 6 ms runner three at a time took `main`'s integration tier from 23.4 to 19.0 s and its unit tier from 11.3 to 7.9 s, for 1.5 s more of the run's wall time (`try` 43778700279, three rounds of each). Two at a time took off more (38.3 to 23.6 s on an 8 ms runner) for 8 s more of the wall time, and was not taken then: the job's time is what people wait for. **Two at a time since the owner's decision of 2026-11-16, which reverses the seventh tooling step's three** (proposed by the ninth tooling step, [steps/tooling-9/answered.md](steps/tooling-9/answered.md), and shipped by the tenth), measured again: on one 6 ms runner two at a time took the integration tier from 28.1 to 22.2 s (−21%) and the unit tier from 13.8 to 9.5 s, for 5.8 s more of the run's wall time (`try` 43884288025), where the integration tier had been over its budget on 9 to 10 ms runners. In `.config/nextest.toml` it is the group `tiers-windows`, chosen in the `ci` profile by an override for `cfg(windows)` and every test that is not a system test.
  - **On Linux tiers 1 and 2 run two at a time too** (the owner's decision of 2026-11-19, in the CI-budgets fix; [steps/ci-budgets/answered.md](steps/ci-budgets/answered.md)). With every core of the 4-core runner busy, the unit tier read 16 to 29 s on `main` from one run to the next, the same tree, and its CPU-heavy tests (the TUI's sweeps and properties) slowed each other most. On one 4 ms runner, three rounds of each layout, two at a time took the unit tier from 15.9 to 10.6 s (−33%) and the integration tier from 21.1 to 14.9 s (−29%), with the system tier unchanged, for about 6 s more of the two runs' wall time (26.5 to 32.4 s, in a Linux job of 130 to 160 s; `try` 44634574569). In `.config/nextest.toml` it is the group `tiers-linux`, chosen in the `ci` profile by an override for `cfg(target_os = "linux")`; other systems run as many as they have cores. **What it changes is how the tier is measured, not what its tests cost:** the 680 unit tests added since wave 12 cost what they cost, and the tier now reads them with less of each other's load in it. **It holds on every Linux machine,** a local run's as a runner's, since the override cannot tell them apart: a local `cargo xtask ci`, `ci --tiers`, `ab` or `combine` runs tiers 1 and 2 two at a time too, which lengthens a local run on a machine of many cores. `ci --tiers --like-runner`'s factors for Linux were measured with four at a time on both sides, before this, and want measuring again.
  - **On Windows a test that is not about which shell runs its command uses Windows PowerShell** (since the ninth tooling step; [steps/tooling-9/answered.md](steps/tooling-9/answered.md)). Counted on a runner, PowerShell 7 was 105 of the system tier's starts and about half of its time: a start costs 0.44 s there, Windows PowerShell 0.31 s, git 0.05 s and `cmd.exe` 0.02 s (fitted to each test's starts and time, `try` 43883759862). And the binary asks PowerShell 7 its version on the side the first time it runs on a machine, which every system test's machine is: one more start a test, where Windows PowerShell is asked nothing. So the core's rig (`World::options`), `cineris-tools`' `default_shell()` and `cineris-cli`'s `Machine::user_config` give Windows PowerShell on Windows. Each shell's own tests (`crates/cineris-tools/tests/it/shell.rs`: PowerShell 7, Windows PowerShell and `cmd.exe`, each case of them) are unchanged, and the shell the harness picks itself, PowerShell 7 on a runner, stays covered end to end by two tests: `cineris-core`'s `shell::sys_a_command_is_given_the_environment_the_command_analysis_relies_on` (`crate::default_shell()`) and `cineris-cli`'s `tui::sys_a_turn_is_interrupted_mid_stream_and_mid_tool` (`Machine::with_product_shell`), and by `tools::sys_the_shells_version_is_kept_for_the_runs_after_the_first`, which names it. On one 7 ms runner the change took the system tier from 73.5 to 59.7 s, with the trust tests' refusals (`try` 43886546354).
  - **`cargo xtask test --profile ci` is one run,** with the system tests three at a time beside the rest (two at a time in two lanes on Windows). It has the job's settings, not its order: `cargo xtask ci test` is the job.
- **A tier is held to its budget above the start of its tests' processes** (the owner's decision of 2026-11-13). nextest runs each test in a process of its own, and starting one costs about 5 ms on a hosted Linux runner and 6 to 12 ms on a Windows one, by the runner's speed. For a thousand unit tests that was 6 to 12 s of the unit tier's 20 before any test did anything, so the raw total told which runner a run drew more than how its tests did: one tree gave 17.0 s on a fast runner and 22.7 s on a slow one.
  - **The start cost is measured by each run:** the time that 1% of its unit and integration tests were quicker than. The quickest tests of a run do next to nothing, so their time is the start of a process. Not the single quickest, which one lucky test moves by a millisecond and with it the whole tier: in one job that ran one tree twice with each of two settings, the unit tier's figure moved by 1.1 s between the two runs of one setting with the quickest test as the floor, where the 1st percentile and the raw totals did not move. System tests are left out of the measure: they start processes of their own. A dedicated empty test in each binary was weighed against it: it would be one sample where the percentile stands on about eighteen, and a test every binary must keep.
  - **A run of a few crates, or of a filter's tests, measures its own start,** and its quickest tests may do real work: the report says so when it has fewer than 500 unit and integration tests to measure on. CI runs the whole workspace.
  - **Each test's time above that cost is added up,** a test that took less counting as nothing, and the warning at the budget and the failure at one and a half times apply to that sum. The same on both OSes: on Linux it takes about 5 s off a unit tier of 13.
  - **A start cost above a ceiling is a warning** (since the seventh tooling step): above 8 ms a test on Linux and 15 ms on Windows, more than the slowest runner of each starts a test in, the report says so, as an annotation on the pull request in CI. A cost every test process shares (a larger test binary that loads more slowly, a global initialiser, a heavier harness) rises with the start and is taken off every test with it, so the budgets cannot see it; the warning is where it shows. A run of fewer than 500 tests says nothing: its quickest tests may do real work.
  - **The report prints both totals** and the cost it took off: `process start: 11 ms a test (the 1st percentile of the run's 1857 unit and integration tests' times)`, then for each tier its tests, its time, its time above the start, its budget and the share of it used.
  - **What it does not even out.** A slow runner is slower at the tests' work as well as at starting them. On the same tree the unit tier read 11.0 s above the start on a fast runner and 11.6 s on a slow one, within noise, where the raw totals were a third apart; the integration tier read 23.1 and 31.3 s, 28.2 and 40.6 s raw. Its tests do far more than start, and the budget is right to see that. The two runs are kept as fixtures, `xtask/fixtures/tiers/windows-fast-runner.xml` and `windows-slow-runner.xml`, and a test holds the figures.
- **A run whose disk stalled is not judged, locally** (since the eighth tooling step; `xtask/src/stall.rs`, `tiers.rs`). A tier's time is the sum of its tests' times, and when a shared machine's disk stalls every test that touches a file waits with it: in wave 11 a tier budget "failed" by a stall, and a person read the log to find that out. The two test runs of `cargo xtask ci` read `/proc/pressure/io` when they start and when they end, and leave what it said of each (how long the run was, and for how long every task waited on I/O: `full` of the kernel's file) in `target/nextest/ci/pressure.txt`, beside the report, which the build forgets with it, each line with the tiers its run measured (`tiers=1,2` for the first run, `tiers=3` for the system tests'; a re-run's line is its run's). The tier budgets step reads that file. When every task waited on I/O for **10% or more** of a run's time (a run and its re-run counted as one), it prints

  ```text
  not judged: the machine stalled. Every task waited on I/O for 41.3 s of the test runs' 120 s (34%, from /proc/pressure/io; a run is taken to have stalled at 10%), so the times of the tiers they ran (1, 2, 3) are the disk's as much as the tests', and those tiers are not held to their budgets; any other is. Run the tests again when the machine is quiet. CI's runners are always judged.
  ```

  above the same tables (each tier's time, by crate, its slowest tests), with no mark, warning or failure for the tiers of the run that stalled: those it neither passes nor fails. The tiers of a run that did not stall are judged as ever, so a stall of the system tests' run leaves tiers 1 and 2 held to their budgets (found by the step's review: summed over both runs, a stall of one excused the other's tiers). (The step list at the end of the run says `ok` for a step whose every tier was excused, since a step has two outcomes; the line above is what says it was not judged.) **On CI the check is what it always was,** whatever the pressure: when `GITHUB_ACTIONS` is set the file is not looked at. A hosted runner's disk is its own, and a verdict that moved with a reading nobody can check there would be a way round the budgets. **Where the pressure cannot be read** (Windows, a kernel without `/proc/pressure/io`, or no file written) the run is judged as it was before. **The threshold, 10%,** is the one `mutate` uses for a busy disk (`full avg60` of 10%, from which it starts with one job), taken over the runs' own time: a run that spent a tenth of its time with every task waiting on the disk lost 2 s of a tier's 20 to it. It was chosen, not fitted to a distribution of quiet runs, which nobody has measured: the stalled runs of wave 11 read 54% ([the reference machine](measurements/README.md#machines)'s disk in the sixth step's measurement, in "What a runner would say, from a local run") and 66% (a review's two `mutate` jobs on a busy machine), and a machine at rest reads 0%. A lower figure would take every busy afternoon of the reference machine for a stall; a higher one would judge a run that lost a fifth of its time. It is one constant to move when local runs say it is wrong. A re-run after a stall ("Running the tests many times over") is part of the runs: a run that stalled enough to be re-run is, in all likelihood, over the threshold, and is not judged. `ci --tiers`, a measurement, and `ci --tiers --from`, which reads a report from elsewhere, are not changed: they judge as before. What it cannot tell: a stall short enough, or shared with a build that stopped, to be under the threshold for the whole of two runs of five minutes, which slows tests a little and is judged.
- **The report lists each tier's slowest tests,** five of them in CI, with the share of the tier's time they are. A CI log shows no time for a test under two seconds, and a tier's total is made of hundreds of those: this is where to look when a tier grows. "A CI run's test times", below, has how to get the rest.
- **A tier over its budget is a warning; a tier over one and a half times its budget is a failure.** Both are annotations on the pull request. The gap is there because hosted runners vary by tens of percent from one run to the next, and more on Windows (the same build took 60 to 82 s on four Windows runners in step 2). A failure at exactly the budget would turn a tier that is near its limit into red builds at random, which teaches people to re-run instead of to fix. The warning is the signal to act; the failure is for a tier that is over on any runner. The limits on a single test stay hard failures: they are several times what a healthy test takes.
- **A tier over its budget says what its runner was** (since the sixth tooling step): the run's process start, and whether that is a usual runner or a slow one, with what a slow one implies (`runner: 12 ms runner, a slow runner: tiers likely about twice what a usual runner (6 to 10 ms a test) reads, with the same tests`). The figures are the fifth tooling step's: a 12 ms Windows runner read the system tier at 138.8 s where 10 ms runners read 77 to 90 s, every tier about twice, and a slow Linux runner 1.8 times a usual one's. A run of too few tests to measure its start says so instead. The line is printed only when a tier is over.
- A test over its slow mark needs a reason or moves to tier 6.

**Property tests** use no property-testing crate. The pattern was set in step 4 (`crates/cineris-base/src/cases.rs`), and steps 6, 10 and 12 follow it. Since step 5 the helper every crate uses is `cineris_testkit::cases`. `cineris-base` keeps its own private copy for its unit tests, because it cannot depend on the test kit, which depends on it; a change to one is a change to both. `cineris-tools` had a third copy while step 6 was built beside the test kit; step 7 removed it, and the crate's unit tests use the shared one.

- A property is a function from a generated input to "fine" or "what was wrong". A generator is a function from a random-number generator to an input. `check(name, cases, generate, property)` runs the property on that many inputs.
- **Every run is the same.** Each case draws from the seeded generator of `cineris-base` with a seed made from the property's name and the case number. There is no randomness between runs or machines, so a property test cannot be flaky.
- **A failure prints the property, the seed, the case number and the input.** `CINERIS_PROPERTY_SEED=<seed>` runs that one case again, alone.
- **`CINERIS_PROPERTY_SCALE=<n>` multiplies every property's number of cases.** Pull requests run at 1, which keeps the properties of one crate to well under a second of the unit tier's 20. The `nightly` workflow runs tiers 1 and 2 at 50. The first time this was tried it found a case the default count had not (a wrong expectation in a wrapping test), which is the point of it. At 50 a property takes fifty times as long, so the nightly run has a nextest profile of its own, `properties`, whose limits on one test are scaled for it: slow at 60 s, stopped at 300 s. The limits pull requests run with are not changed. A property of pure logic is far from either (the slowest of step 4 takes 1.8 s at 50 on the reference machine); one that does real file work for each case is not: the rewind property of `cineris-session`, a session and a workspace per case, takes 0.75 s at 1 and 32 s at 50 on the reference machine, and passed the 10 s limit in the nightly run of 2026-11-13 (issue #19).
- **There is no shrinking,** so generators prefer small inputs, and an input is printed whole. In exchange the helper is 60 lines, adds no dependency and nothing to the build.
- **Small spaces are checked exhaustively** in addition, where that is possible, in the nightly run and by a sample on a pull request (the next point): every sequence of up to six path steps from a small alphabet, every split of a byte stream into two and into three chunks.
- **An exhaustive test that takes a noticeable part of a second checks a sample on a pull request and all of it in the nightly run.** It asks `cases::exhaustive()`, which holds when `CINERIS_PROPERTY_SCALE` is above 1. Its own comment says what the sample is, and the sample must reach every kind of case: path sequences of up to five steps, the calendar's days from 1600 to 2399 with the two ends of the range, every third second cut of a stream. A property's number of cases is cut the same way when it takes a tenth of a second on a Windows runner. The nightly run multiplies whatever the number is by fifty, so a cut there is a cut of the nightly run too, by the same share: the properties cut on 2026-11-13 run 40 to 50% of the cases they did, in both.

**A comparison with the real thing is a property too.** Where the code models another program, the test asks that program. Step 12's command analysis says "this line is these commands with these arguments", and a rule is matched against that, so `crates/cineris-policy/tests/it/differential.rs` generates lines, keeps the ones the analysis models, and compares: bash and `sh` are run with each command replaced by a function that prints its arguments, and PowerShell is asked to parse each line with its own parser. They are system tests, because they start a shell, and `CINERIS_PROPERTY_SCALE` multiplies their lines like any property's cases. On its first run the comparison found that dash reads `20<file` as an argument and a redirection where bash reads a descriptor. A shell that is missing skips its half locally and fails it in CI.

What was weighed against it: proptest, which shrinks failing inputs and has a library of generators, and which would add about fifteen packages to every crate's test build, on both OSes, for the life of the project.

## Deterministic agent tests without a network

`cineris-testkit` provides three levels of fake, one for each kind of test.

| Fake | Replaces | Used for |
|---|---|---|
| **Scripted provider** | `ModelClient`. A script of model events with optional delays, errors, stalls and aborts, plus assertions on the requests it received. | Tier 2: every loop behaviour. Runs in microseconds. |
| **Scripted transport** | `Transport`. Returns recorded response bodies in chosen chunk sizes. | Tier 1: protocol adapters, from fixtures. Tier 2: the provider client and its retry layer. |
| **Scripted SSE server** | The provider's HTTP endpoint. A local listener that writes scripted chunks with controlled timing, stalls and disconnects. | The real HTTP client, timeouts, cancellation (tier 2, in-process, since step 5), and the real binary end to end (tier 3). |

All three were built in step 5, with two more pieces they need.

**The conformance scenarios** (`cineris_testkit::conformance`, since step 27) are the RPC server's: each is data (a workspace's files, the scripted SSE server's replies, and steps: send an operation, answer the next request, wait for an event of a type), and one list holds them all. `cineris-rpc`'s tests run every scenario through the core in process and through the RPC connection over its in-memory ends (tier 2), and `cineris-cli`'s through the real binary's `serve --stdio` (tier 3); what the client was sent is compared after the same normalising (ids numbered by first appearance, times taken out, runs of deltas of one item and kind joined). A step that adds a method or an event adds a scenario there, and every carrier runs it ([architecture/11-rpc-protocol.md](architecture/11-rpc-protocol.md), "As built in step 27"). Since step 28's close, one of a project's commands and extensions, from the `release` example: the trust question that lists its extension, a `turn/start` that names its command file, then `commands/list` and `extensions/status` after the turn, so that the session's extensions are settled on every carrier; its script command is listed, not run, since the scenarios start no process. Beside the comparison, `cineris-rpc`'s test checks that scenario's own facts on the in-process transcript, so that a change that broke every carrier alike does not pass for their agreement: the trust question lists `changelog`, the command file's body is the prompt, `commands/list` has both commands, and `extensions/status` says `ready`. The examples under `examples/` are data that `cineris-cli`'s, `cineris-core`'s, `cineris-ext`'s, `cineris-policy`'s and the test kit's tests read (`include_str!` in `conformance`), so a change to them is a change to those tests. `cineris_testkit::scratch::git_with_input` runs git with standard input, so that a test makes a history of many commits in one `git fast-import` (a start of git costs about 50 ms on a hosted Windows runner).

- **The scripted provider** builds a response from what a model does (text, reasoning, tool calls, usage, an end, a speed the provider served it at) and what goes wrong (an error part-way, a delay on a timer, a stall until cancelled, a wait for the test's gate, a stream that just stops). It hands over one event at a time and only when the reader asks, so the order a test sees is the order of the script.
- **The scripted SSE server** listens on the loopback interface only, on a port the system picks, with plain threads and no async runtime, so it can also serve another process. A reply is a script of steps: send these bytes, tell the test this point was reached, wait for the test, hold the connection until the client hangs up, drop it. That last-but-one step is how a test sees that a cancelled request really closed its connection. A client that hangs up while the script is still writing is seen too, by the write that fails: a process stopped by a signal in the instant after its request arrived does that. **A gate that a server thread opens only at the end of its script never opens if an earlier step fails** (a write to a client that hung up, a wait the test never lets go): a test waiting for it waits out its limit. That was the shape of step 13b's SIGINT flake. Mark the point the test needs as early as it is true, or wait for something that opens either way, as the gate of "hold the connection until the client hangs up" does.
- **A test protocol** (`TestProtocol`): a wire dialect small enough to write by hand in a test and complete enough to say everything a real one can, including the speed that served a request and a refusal that belongs to a speed tier. `cineris-llm` has no real protocol until the first provider step, and its client is tested with this one. It is not a model of any provider's API.
- **Fixture models** with every shape of speed tier: one that fails when it cannot be served and has a limit and a cache of its own, one the provider downgrades by itself, and a model with no tier.

**A request a real protocol could not build.** The scripted provider takes any request. `ScriptedProvider::built_as(protocol, provider)` (since the fourth tooling step) runs the real `Protocol::build` on each request first, for a catalog model given with `with_model`, and fails a request the protocol cannot build with the build's error, as the real client does, taking nothing from the script; its stable prefix is the protocol's, so the loop's debug-build check runs on real bytes. Step 15's review needed one to show that a summary's thinking budget was not below `max_tokens`. `cineris-agent`'s context tests use it for Anthropic and DeepSeek.

**A response is cut at the request's maximum output** (since the sixth tooling step). A real provider stops a response at `max_output`, ends it with the length stop reason and counts the output it wrote; a script that said more than the request allows used to pass the loop as if it had been written whole. `ScriptedProvider` now cuts it, and ends it as a provider does:

- **The limit** is the request's `max_output`, or the model's own most when the request sets none, and never more than the model's own most (as Anthropic's builder holds it). `test-model` writes 8,000 tokens at most.
- **Output is counted as the loop counts a request before a provider has counted it,** at three bytes to the token (`cineris_testkit::provider::BYTES_PER_OUTPUT_TOKEN`; a test of `cineris-agent` holds it to `cineris_agent::context::DEFAULT_RATIO`), over the text, the reasoning and the arguments of the tool calls. A response is cut when its output would be more than the limit's bytes, never at them.
- **What is cut:** the delta in which the limit falls is cut on a character boundary, and nothing of the script after it is played, errors, waits and an `abort` included. Every block that was open is closed with what had arrived of it, as the real decoders close an unfinished block (`BlockEnd` with the partial content); a tool call cut in its arguments has arguments that are not JSON, as a real one has. The response then ends with `StopReason::MaxOutput` and a usage whose `output` is the limit (the script's other counts are kept). The loop treats it as it treats a real one ("a response cut off in its text", "a response cut off inside a tool call", in `cineris-agent`'s `streaming.rs`).
- **It is the default.** Of the 2,812 tests of tiers 1 and 2 when it was made, two had a script longer than their request allowed (a 600 kB write in `cineris-core`'s approval test; a 20,000-byte summary in `cineris-agent`'s test of a summary too large for a new epoch), and no test cut by it passed unnoticed. Each says why it opts out with `provider.set_output_limit(false)`, which is shared by every clone, so it can be called on a world's provider after the rig is made; `ScriptedProvider::without_output_limit()` is the same at construction. A script that needs a response longer than its model may write is a script of a response that cannot happen: the opt-out is the exception to say so.

**A long text in an assertion's message** is `cineris_testkit::brief(&text)`: whole when it is short, its size and its two ends when it is long. A notice of ten kilobytes in a failure message once hid the result it was about.

**Sizing a context test by pressure.** A test of compaction needs a request at a given pressure, and step 15's bulk was counted by hand, then counted again three times as the prompt and the tools grew. `Rig::fill(Fill::…, prompt)` in `cineris-agent`'s rig (since the fourth tooling step) gives the session's first request the bulk that puts it below the threshold for a background summary (`Below`), between it and the trigger (`Preparing`), past the trigger (`Compacting`), or past the window (`PastWindow`). It measures the request without the bulk on a twin of the session (the same prompt, tools, instruction files, thresholds and model, at the same workspace path, in a world of its own with a window it cannot fill), and puts the estimate in the middle of the band, from the real limit (`cineris_agent::context::limit_of`) and thresholds, at the ratio the loop uses before a provider has counted. A band the window cannot reach is refused with the numbers. The fixture models it works from:

| Model | Window | Most output | Limit | Background summary past | Compaction past |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `test-model` (`fixtures::model()`) | 200,000 | 8,000 | 188,000 | 131,600 | 159,800 |
| `test-model` with `rig.window(6_000)` | 6,000 | 8,000 | 4,380 | 3,066 | 3,723 |
| `test-model` with `rig.window(4_000)` | 4,000 | 8,000 | 2,920 | 2,044 | 2,482 |
| `test-plain` (`fixtures::plain_model()`) | 32,000 | 4,000 | 27,360 | 19,152 | 23,256 |

The limit is the window less the room kept for the answer (the most output, capped at 32,768 and at a quarter of the window) and a fiftieth of the window; the thresholds are `context.prepare_at` (0.70) and `context.compact_at` (0.85) of the limit, in tokens. Seven of step 15's tests use it; they still catch all 40 of step 15's breaks.

**A request after the first** (since the sixth tooling step). `Rig::fill` sizes a session's first request and refuses one with history. Two more methods size a later one, on a twin made the same way:

- **`Rig::fill_next(Fill::…, prompt)`** sizes the first request of the next turn of a session that has history, and gives the next turn its bulk, as `fill` does. The twin is resumed from a copy of the items the rig's store holds, so what the session has said is in the measure; `summaries::past_the_trigger` (the second turn's request between the trigger and the limit, which counted its bulk by hand from the first request's estimate) is its first user.
- **`Rig::fill_after(Fill::…, prompt, earlier)`** sizes a request inside a turn: the one that follows `earlier`, the responses of the turn `prompt` starts that come before it (the call of a tool round, whose result is in the request). The twin runs the turn on those responses and measures the request after them. It returns what it measured and **gives the session nothing**: bulk given before the turn is in the turn's first request too, so the test gives it with `rig.bulk(filled.bulk)` at a moment of its own, while the turn runs, and the notice reaches the turn at its next request. `earlier` is what the session will be given, without a wait (a `wait` in a response changes no byte of the request, so the test's script may have one where the twin's has none: the test builds both from one function), and its tools must answer as the twin's. The test of it holds a response at a gate, gives the bulk, opens the gate, and checks the second request's estimate against the one sized.
- **A few sizes are still counted by hand, on purpose** (the eighth tooling step moved the rest onto `fill` and `fill_next`: the over-budget notices of the transition, interrupt and session tests, the second turn of the compaction whose epoch was not recorded, and the summary with a reasoning budget). A notice given in the middle of a turn that runs in one `world.run` (`rig.bulk` after a summary finished, in `context.rs`) cannot be sized by `fill_next`, which runs between turns; a request whose provider reported tokens (`counted`) has no twin; twelve notices that are each a place to cut are not one bulk; and a test that holds a window against the system prompt's room (`context.rs`, "the test holds from about 5,050 tokens") is about the prompt's size and says so. A test that passes within 5% of a threshold is not warned about by CI: which number is the threshold, and how near is too near, is the test's meaning and not its result.
- **They refuse what the twin cannot know**, with the numbers or the reason: a band the window cannot reach with that history; a session that has no history (`fill_next`) or has some (`fill`); a request the twin's turn never makes (how many it made, and which was asked for); a session whose store the rig does not hold, or that has compacted already; and a session any of whose requests was counted by its provider (a response that reported tokens), where the loop estimates from the count and the twin has none. A twin is resumed from items alone, so its estimate and the session's can differ by the few tokens of a notice: the tests allow eight.

**A directory of a test's own** is `cineris_testkit::Scratch`, made with the `scratch!` macro: a new, empty directory under the one Cargo gives the crate's integration tests, with helpers to write files and to make a git repository whose configuration is the test's own and not the machine's. It is removed when the test ends, and left in place when the test fails; in the test job's own runs on a GitHub-hosted runner, whose disk goes with the job, it is left too (`CINERIS_TESTKIT_KEEP_DIRECTORIES`, which the blocking job and `try`'s runs of the tests set, and a stress run or a break's does not; since CI budgets II, [steps/ci-budgets-2/answered.md](steps/ci-budgets-2/answered.md)). A test that needs "no repository above this directory" asks for `Scratch::outside`, in the system's temporary directory. The tests of `cineris-config`, `cineris-policy`, `cineris-session` and `cineris-agent` use it. The ones that still have their own are listed in [steps/tooling-w5/answered.md](steps/tooling-w5/answered.md).

**Hostile names** are one set, in `cineris_testkit::hostile`, that every test of how a name is shown, kept, refused or looked up takes its names from (since the seventh tooling step). A name from a workspace is text the harness did not write; each test used to keep its own few of them, and one kept a constant that was a line feed on Linux and U+2028 on Windows, so that what it checked depended on where it ran, and a bug in how a line feed is shown could stay hidden on the system where the constant was U+2028. The set has a kind for each way a name is hostile: control characters (C0, DEL, C1), bidirectional controls (the embeddings, overrides and isolates, the marks, and names that use them to spoof an extension), other format characters (`Cf`: zero-width, joiners, the byte-order mark, the soft hyphen, the tag characters), characters that render as nothing and are not `Cf`, line breaks (LF, CR, CRLF, NEL, U+2028, U+2029), escape sequences for a terminal (CSI, OSC closed by BEL and by ST, the C1 forms), long names (254, 255 and 256 bytes, 260 characters, 254 and 256 UTF-16 units, a few thousand, and runs of characters whose escape is longer than they are), the names Windows keeps for devices (with other case, an extension, trailing dots and spaces, the superscript digits), combining marks, wide and emoji characters, right-to-left letters, punctuation that a file system, a shell or a glob gives a meaning (`..`, a leading `-`, a backslash, a colon, a stream, a quote, a trailing dot, an escape written out as text) and the replacement character. An entry is a `Name`: a label a failure message can print, the `text`, the `shown` form that `cineris_base::visible` must give it (written by hand, so that the function is checked against something other than itself), and its `Kind`. `hostile::of(kind)` and `hostile::all()` give them; `hostile::character(c)` and `hostile::named(label)` pick one.

**The rule: no constant per system that changes what a test checks.** The set has no `cfg` (a unit test reads its source for one), so the same entries are there on Linux and on Windows: a name that is hostile is hostile on both. What differs is what a *file system* takes: Windows refuses `CON`, a line feed and a colon in a file name, and Linux a slash and a NUL. A test that makes files asks the system at run time: `hostile::takes_file_name(dir, name)` makes the file in a directory of its own inside `dir` and reads back whether the directory holds exactly that name, and `hostile::accepted(dir, kind)` gives the entries of a kind that it does. The test runs its case for each of those, and fails when there are none, so that it cannot check nothing and pass; a test that makes no file (a path asked for that is not there, a reason, a notice) takes every entry. A case about one system's rule (a device name is refused on Windows, an ordinary file name on Linux) is in a file for that system and takes the same entries of the set. `cineris-base`, which is below the test kit (rule 6 of [architecture/01-workspace.md](architecture/01-workspace.md)), keeps its own copies in its tests; the test kit's unit tests check the set against `cineris-base` (`visible` and `acts_unseen`) instead.

**A crate's own unit tests cannot use test-kit types that are made of that crate's types.** The test kit depends on `cineris-llm`, so in `cineris-llm`'s unit-test build there are two copies of the crate, the one under test and the one the test kit was built against, and a `ModelEvent` of one is not a `ModelEvent` of the other. So `cineris-llm` tests its pure parts (the parser, the retry rules, the catalog, cost) as unit tests, and its client, which needs the scripted transport and the fake timer, in its integration-test binary. The parts of the test kit that involve only `cineris-base` types, such as the property helper, work in unit tests too.

**Fixtures.** Recorded provider responses live in the repo under each protocol's `fixtures/` directory: the raw response stream, the request that produced it, and a note. `xtask record` captures them from a real provider by hand, scrubs credentials and identifiers, and is never run in CI. Each provider step adds fixtures for every case in its checklist ([build-plan.md](build-plan.md)).

As built in step 14, for the first protocol:

- **Where.** `crates/cineris-llm/fixtures/<protocol>/<name>/`, with four files: `request.json` (the body that was sent), `response.sse` (the response body, byte for byte), `response.json` (the status and the headers that were kept) and `note.md` (what it shows, the model, and how it was made). `.gitattributes` keeps every file under a `fixtures` directory exactly as it is, line endings included.
- **Recorded, or written by hand.** A case that the real API produces on request is recorded. A case that cannot be provoked (an overload in the middle of a response, a refusal, a server-side tool, a stop reason from a newer API) is written by hand from the provider's documented format, and its note says so. Of Anthropic's 33 fixtures, 17 are recorded. Of the 72 of `openai-chat`, 56 are recorded, from OpenRouter, DeepSeek and Venice.ai; the 16 written by hand are OpenAI's seven and the local servers' five, for which there was no key and no server, and four cases that cannot be provoked (two failures in the middle of a response, a stop reason, and a fast tier's refusal). A fixture written by hand because its server was not there says so in the first line of its note, so that a recording can replace it.
- **The requests are shared.** The recorder builds each request with the same function the offline tests use. So a fixture's `request.json` is a snapshot of a request the real API accepted, and a test fails when the builder stops writing it. A recorded conversation is rebuilt the same way: each later request from what the decoder made of the response before it, and compared, byte for byte, with what was sent.
- **Recording.** `cargo xtask record [--provider PROVIDER] [SCENARIO…]` runs an ignored test of `cineris-llm` (the `live` module of the protocol's tests), and `cargo xtask live [PROVIDER]` the smoke test beside it, and then, for `anthropic` and `deepseek`, one headless run of the real binary: an ignored system test of `cineris-cli` (`tests/system/live.rs`) that hands the key to the binary's environment and runs a one-line task with one tool call, `--output json`, with the whole built-in toolbox. The provider is `anthropic` when none is named, or `openrouter`, `deepseek` or `venice`. `cargo xtask live --headless [PROVIDER]` (since the fourth tooling step) runs every headless test of `tests/system/live.rs` against the provider (`--test NAME`, since the sixth, runs the one named: its whole name, its name in the module or the end of it, `--test compaction`; `--dry-run` prints the commands it would run and whether the key file's variable is set, and builds, reads and sends nothing), under `cargo test` and not nextest, so a long session (step 15's compaction run) is not stopped at a system test's limit; with the key file and the ledger as `live` has them, and stopping before anything is built or sent without the key file. A test holds every ignored test of that file to a command. What the live tests of every protocol share (the transport that keeps what passes through it, the key, the spend limit) is `tests/it/live.rs`. They are there and not in `xtask` because `xtask` is built before every CI job, and the HTTP and TLS stack is the largest dependency the project has. No CI job runs ignored tests.
- **The key.** Each provider has a variable that names a file holding its key and nothing else: `CINERIS_LIVE_ANTHROPIC_KEY_FILE`, `CINERIS_LIVE_OPENROUTER_KEY_FILE`, `CINERIS_LIVE_DEEPSEEK_KEY_FILE`, `CINERIS_LIVE_VENICE_KEY_FILE`. The key is never an argument and never in the environment. Without the variable both commands say so and stop: they do not pass, and they do not skip.
- **What is taken out** (`cineris_testkit::fixture::Scrubber`). No request header is kept at all. Of the response headers only a short list is kept: the content type, `retry-after`, the request id and the rate limits of the model and of the fast tier. **The size of each rate limit is made up:** every `…-limit` is replaced by a round number chosen by what the limit counts (1,000 requests, 1,000,000 tokens, 100,000 output tokens), and every `…-remaining` by the same share of it, so that "all of it", "none of it" and "one request used" still read as they did. The ids the provider makes up (`msg_…`, `req_…`, `toolu_…`) are replaced by stand-ins, the same stand-in wherever the id appears, so that a tool result still names its call. The opaque payloads of reasoning blocks are replaced the same way: they are bound to the account that produced them. Every value of such a member that is not empty is replaced, whatever it looks like, and the member is also found where a block is quoted inside a string.
- **The servers of `openai-chat` write identifiers every which way** (a prefix and a hyphen, a bare UUID, a call id with underscores in it), so a recording of one finds them by where they are: the value of every `id` and `tool_call_id` member is an identifier, and is replaced wherever it appears afterwards, in any later exchange too. Of their headers the six `x-ratelimit-…` ones and `x-request-id` are kept, with the sizes made up in the same way. A UUID is an identifier wherever it is: one provider quotes its request id inside the message of a failure, where no member names it. The value of a request-id header is one whatever it looks like. **A stand-in is never replaced again:** a recording that quotes an earlier fixture (the conversation a second provider is handed) keeps that fixture's stand-ins.
- **A fixture says nothing about the account that recorded it.** The sizes of its rate limits would show its usage tier, which is why they are made up. A test checks that every limit in every fixture is the value the scrubber writes.
- **A recording that holds the key is refused.** Everything that is about to be written, and every line that is about to be printed, is searched for the key and for any twelve characters in a row of it. If it is there, nothing is written and nothing is printed. The refusal is tested with a made-up key, in the request, in the response, in a header that is kept and in the note.
- **What a run costs.** Each call prints what it used and what that cost at the catalog's prices, and a run stops before it has spent half a dollar. **Each call is also a line in a spend ledger,** outside the repository: the user's state directory (`~/.local/state/cineris-live/spend.jsonl`, or `%LOCALAPPDATA%\cineris-live\spend.jsonl`), or the file `CINERIS_LIVE_LEDGER` names. A line holds the date, the provider, the model, the tokens, the cost and the test; it is checked for the key like everything else a live run writes. `cargo xtask live --spent [--since YYYY-MM-DD]` totals it by provider and model. Since step 15 the headless runs of the real binary are in it too: each adds a line from its `--output json` record's tokens and cost (`cineris-cli`'s `tests/system/live.rs`).
- **A dry run.** `cargo xtask record --provider PROVIDER --dry-run SCENARIO` builds the scenario's first request, prints its method, URL and body, and sends nothing. It reads no key, so it needs no key file, and it does not ask a provider's usage endpoint. It stops at the first request, because what a scenario sends after it is built from the response. Recording all of Anthropic's fixtures cost about one and a half cents. Where a response states its own cost (OpenRouter, Venice.ai), that is the figure counted, and a run ends by asking the provider's usage endpoint how far its own figure moved; only the difference is printed. Recording the `openai-chat` fixtures cost under a cent for all three providers together.

**The toolbox fixtures.** Step 13b's one live run found three refusals by Anthropic's strict mode of the real built-in toolbox, which no fixture could have shown: the fixtures declared one small tool. Since the second tooling step, the real toolbox is recorded too.

- **What the binary sends is kept.** A system test of `cineris-cli` (`tools::sys_the_toolbox_the_binary_sends_is_the_one_its_fixtures_were_recorded_with`) runs the binary against the scripted server for the cheapest catalog model of each recorded provider (Anthropic's `claude-haiku-5-5`, DeepSeek's `deepseek-flash`, OpenRouter's `z-ai/glm-5.3-flash`, Venice.ai's `deepseek-v4-flash-0731`) and reads the tools of its first request: the real toolbox, through the real request builder and the strict reduction where the model has strict tools (only Anthropic's of the four). They are kept in `crates/cineris-llm/fixtures/toolbox/<provider>.json`.
- **A request with them is recorded** from each provider, with a short system prompt and a one-line task that needs no tool: the fixture `toolbox` of `anthropic-messages`, and `<provider>-toolbox` of `openai-chat`. All four providers accepted it on 2026-11-13, for $0.0022 together.
- **Two tests in `cineris-llm` hold the builder to it** (`tests/it/toolbox.rs`): built from the kept tools, a request declares them exactly as the binary did (name, description, schema and `strict`); and its bytes are the recorded `request.json`, which the provider accepted. A change to the builder or to the strict reduction that changes what is sent fails there, until it is recorded again. The fixtures are replayed at every cut like the others.
- **A change to the toolbox is a warning,** not a failure: the system test compares the tools the binary sends now with the kept ones (all but the shell tool's description, which names the machine's shell; on Linux only, since the kept tools are a Linux machine's) and, when they differ, says so on the job's summary page. Whether a provider still accepts the new tools is known only from a recording.
- **Recording it again:** `cargo xtask record --toolbox [--provider PROVIDER]` keeps the tools the binary sends now to the provider named, or to every provider whose key file is named in the environment (the system test in its writing mode, `CINERIS_UPDATE_TOOLBOX=<providers>`), then records the toolbox scenario from each. A provider that is not recorded keeps the tools of its recording, so a change to its tools stays a warning. The command fails unless the capture test really ran. It refuses `--dry-run`: `record --provider P --dry-run toolbox` prints the request instead. Snapshots of the new streams are written with `CINERIS_UPDATE_SNAPSHOTS=1`, as for any fixture.

**Replays at every chunk boundary.** Stream decoders are tested by replaying each fixture split at every byte offset, which catches the bugs that only appear when a network read ends in the middle of a token.

- Each stream fixture is decoded whole, one byte at a time, in pieces of several fixed sizes, and cut in two. The events must be the same every time, and what they are is in a snapshot for each fixture.
- **The work is bounded, and divided between two places,** as step 4 learned it must be. Cutting a stream of `n` bytes in two at every byte decodes `n` bytes `n` times, in a build that does not optimise the JSON reader. For the 26 streams of step 14 that is 200 MB of decoding. On the step's first CI run it took 22 s of test time on Linux and about 40 s on Windows, and put the integration tier over its budget there (51.6 s of 40 s, a warning). So:
  - **on every pull request,** each stream is delivered one byte at a time, which puts a boundary at every byte of it, and in pieces of two other sizes; and it is cut in two at each of its first 64 bytes and at about fifty places spread over the rest (a hundred places and seven sizes until 2026-11-13, when the Windows integration tier was at its budget);
  - **in the nightly run,** which already multiplies the property cases and has time limits of its own, each stream is cut in two at every byte, and delivered in pieces of seven other sizes.
- **The streams of `openai-chat` are larger**: one records 104 kB for 385 tokens, because the server sends a chunk of 300 bytes for each token of reasoning. So for them the number of cuts goes down as a stream gets longer (between 8 and 50 on a pull request), a stream over 16,000 bytes is cut in two at about 4,000 places in the nightly run and not at every byte, and one test replays all of a server's streams: a process for each stream cost more than the decoding did. Measured in CI's own build profile on the build machine before the push, the sum of the protocol's test times was 2.8 s against 2.4 s for Anthropic's (3.8 s before the streams of a server shared a test).

**Golden transcripts.** Sequences of protocol messages from released versions are kept and replayed against current types, to catch accidental breaking changes to the RPC protocol. The first one, written by hand in step 3, is `crates/cineris-protocol/tests/golden/v1-session.jsonl`: every method, every event type and every kind of item, part, delta, notice and plan edit of protocol version 1. Three tests use it: each line must read into its typed form and write back as the same JSON; the same must hold after a field no version knows is added to every object; and each line must be valid against the generated schema ([architecture/11-rpc-protocol.md](architecture/11-rpc-protocol.md)). A transcript is not edited to make a test pass. The types that travel in no message have golden values in a second file beside it, and a test fails, naming the type, when a type in the schema has a value in neither. An addition to a released version gets a new golden file.

**No paid API in CI.** Pull-request jobs have no secrets. The scripted fakes and fixtures cover everything that blocks.

**Usage records from other tools.** The adapters in [architecture/18-usage.md](architecture/18-usage.md) are tested only against small, made-up example files written for the purpose: one set per format version an adapter supports, plus damaged and unknown-version examples. Real session data from any tool is never used as a fixture and never committed. The static check that rejects home-directory paths covers the fixture folders, and an adapter test asserts that no text from the input reaches the output.

### A month of statistics, made up

A test of the usage view, of the panel that shows it, of the readers of other tools' files or of a measurement of `cineris usage` needs a month of statistics that looks like a person's: working days and days off, many sessions, a spread of models, a cache that is mostly read. Real data is never a fixture (above), and a month by hand is a month of one kind. `cineris_stats::synthetic` (since the sixth tooling step, `crates/cineris-stats/src/synthetic.rs`) makes one.

- **Where it lives, and why.** In `cineris-stats`, behind its `test-hooks` feature, and not in the test kit: it makes the crate's own `Record`s and writes them through the crate's own `Recorder`, and the test kit may not depend on `cineris-stats` ([architecture/01-workspace.md](architecture/01-workspace.md), rule 6 and the table). The crate's own unit tests have it with no feature. Another crate's tests ask for it in `[dev-dependencies]`: `cineris-stats = { workspace = true, features = ["test-hooks"] }`. `cargo xtask rules` fails when anything else turns the feature on, as for `cineris-tools`.
- **Use.** `synthetic::write(&StatsDir::new(dir), &Spec::month(seed, Scale::HEAVY))` fills an empty directory with `events-2026-10.jsonl` and `aggregates.json`, and returns a `Summary`. `Spec::year(seed, scale)` is twelve months from January 2026, one file each, for "a year of generated history"; `Spec` has the first month, the number of months and `days` (only the first so many days of each month; `with_days(n)` and `with_months(n)` set them) as fields. A test that reads the directory sees exactly what a user's holds: the writer thread wrote it, and the aggregates were brought up to date by the real sync, with "today" at the end of the last month written (a year or less folds and prunes nothing).
- **The scales.** `Scale::LIGHT` (about 200 requests a month: two days in five, a session or two, short turns), `TYPICAL` (about 1,500: weekdays, two or three sessions of a dozen turns), `HEAVY` (about 5,000: nearly every day, weekends half of them, four or five long sessions a day, every provider, the fast tier, subscription billing and a judge) and `EXTREME` (some 90,000: the size step 16 measured disk use at; for a release build, not a debug test). A scale is a plain struct of ranges and percentages, so a test that needs another makes one.
- **What a month holds.** Per session: a project, a main model drawn from the catalog's own names (`claude-opus-5-5`, `gpt-6-luna`, `deepseek-v4-pro`, `openai/gpt-6-luna` through OpenRouter and the rest) with its catalog prices, billing (metered, or subscription with the cost as an estimate), a speed (the fast tier where the model offers one, with some requests falling back to standard and recording `speed_requested`), the variants and effort level. Per turn: two to four requests of the main model with the tool calls between them, in the ways a call ends (ok, failed, invalid arguments, unknown tool, denied, interrupted), the turn's own ending (completed, failed by a failed request, interrupted, limit, denied), the verification result where files were edited, the split of the wall time and each tool's share. Per request: tokens by class with the cache read on most requests and lost when the user was away more than five minutes, cache writes on the providers that report them, reasoning tokens, the cost at the model's price, time to first token, tokens a second, a few retries and a few failed requests with no tokens and no price, and a context record. The `fast` role writes a title at the start of a session and a summary when the context passes 160,000 tokens (which begins a new epoch); the `judge` role appears in the heavy scale. Nothing in it is taken from anyone's use.
- **Deterministic.** The seed is the whole of the randomness (`cineris_base::SeededRng`); nothing reads the clock, the time zone or the environment; days are UTC; and only integer arithmetic and exact divisions are used. One seed gives the same bytes on both operating systems, which a test holds by writing a month twice and comparing every file.
- **The summary is an independent count.** `Summary` is added up from each event as it is made (requests, failed requests, tool calls, turns, contexts, lines, active days and days off, usage by day and model, turns by outcome, calls by tool, wall time), by code that shares nothing with `Part::add_line`. A test compares it with what `Aggregates` and a reading of the files say, so it checks the crate's reader as much as the generator. The ordinary tests do that for a week of a light user and a day of a small mixed scale, and for two months; the measurements below do it for a whole light, typical and heavy month and a year.
- **What the blocking tests cost, and what they leave to a measurement.** The blocking tiers run seven tests of the generator, 60 ms in all on the reference machine in the `ci` build, one at a time (each is a day or two of a small month, 5 to 15 ms with the test's process start). They are: one seed gives the same bytes; another seed gives other figures; a week of a light user and a day of the mixed scale each read back as the summary says (and a light week has days off and days worked); two months are two files that add up; a two-day month of the mixed scale has its roles, speeds, billings, failed requests and cache reads and every cost is its tokens at the catalog's price; the scales' differences, from their parameters (no month made); and the models and prices against the catalog. `MIXED`, a scale of the tests' own, works every day at rates far above a real user's (failures, fast tier, subscriptions, judge), so that two days show kinds a real month shows in weeks. **No ordinary test asserts on a time.** What needs a whole month is `perf_` and ignored, as the repo's measurements are, and run by hand: `cargo xtask test -p cineris-stats --run-ignored only perf_ --no-capture` (nextest shows a passing test's output only with it), or on a runner `gh workflow run try --ref <branch> -f os=windows -f command="perf -p cineris-stats"` (or `try -f command="test -p cineris-stats --run-ignored only perf_"`): `perf_the_scales_made_and_timed` (a light, a typical and a heavy month, each read back and compared with its summary, with the table below printed), `perf_a_heavy_month_has_every_kind_priced` (every outcome, role, purpose, provider, difference and verification kind, the summaries on the `fast` role, and every cost) and `perf_a_year_is_twelve_files_and_adds_up`. They print their times and assert on none. Measured on the reference machine, `ci` build: a light month (550 lines, 0.3 MB) 14 ms, a typical one (5,500 lines, 2.9 MB) 133 ms and a heavy one (19,400 lines, 5,400 requests, 10 MB) 465 ms, and a light year (7,500 lines) 196 ms, in the process that makes it. Most of it is the real writer serialising each line and the real sync reading it back, not the generator, and reading a month again (parsing every line) costs about as much as making it. On a hosted Windows runner the heavy month took 3.25 s, which is why it is not in a blocking tier.
- **Breaks.** `mutations/tooling-6.breaks`: the seed ignored, something besides the seed deciding, the summary missing a kind of event, days off that never come, a cache never read, a fast request priced as standard, a price that drifts from the catalog (the generator's prices are held to `catalog/models.toml` by a test that reads it), and a queue too small for the events made between two waits for the writer. Each names ordinary tests: `mutate` never runs an ignored one.

### An engine, with sane defaults

A test that makes a permission engine (`cineris_policy::Engine`) gives it a `Parts`, which has a field for everything an engine is made from: eighteen of them. Each crate whose tests made one wrote them all out by hand, and the re-decide step of step 12 copied the literal into `cineris-core`'s unit tests; every field added to `Parts` meant changing every copy. Since the ninth tooling step `cineris_policy::test_support::TestParts` makes them (`crates/cineris-policy/src/test_support.rs`):

```text
let engine = TestParts::at(path("/work/app")).home(path("/users/dev")).engine();
let parts = TestParts::at(workspace).level(Level::ReadOnly).rules(rules).parts();
```

- **The defaults are the most ordinary session there is:** `supervised`, lease `l_1`, a trusted project (`trusted` and `repository_trusted`), a POSIX shell whatever the workspace's path style, an enforcing sandbox with the workspace's default scope, no rules, approvals, models or store, nothing protected, paths read as text (`Lexical`) with the home directory the parts end with, and a fake clock at `2026-11-13T10:00:00Z`. The table is on `TestParts::at`.
- **What a test is about, it says.** A setter for each field tests vary (`home`, `level`, `rules`, `approved`, `sandbox_policy`, `sandbox`, `trusted`, `repository_trusted`, `protected`, `shell`, `locate`, `store`, `clock`), `with(|parts| …)` for the rest, `parts()` for a `Parts` the test changes or starts itself (a test of a start that fails), and `engine()`, which fails the test with the engine's error. A resolver the test gives is used in place of the text one, whichever order the setters came in.
- **Only tests have it.** It is behind the `test-support` feature of `cineris-policy`, which a dev-dependency turns on: `cineris-core`'s, for the unit tests of its policy, and `cineris-policy`'s own on itself, for its integration tests. `cargo xtask rules` holds `test-support` to dev-dependencies as it does `test-hooks` (below, "Lints and formatting").
- **Moved onto it:** `cineris-core`'s `policy::tests::engine`, and `cineris-policy`'s own `parts()` (`tests/it/main.rs`), `real_engine_at` (`real_paths.rs`) and `windows_parts` (`files.rs`). Each keeps what it is about written out: the home, the protected directories, the sandbox's extra root, the shell, the resolver.
- **Breaks:** `mutations/tooling-9.breaks` (the home not given to the resolver, a given resolver ignored, a level not set, and the rule's list without `test-support`).

## Showing that the tests bite

A test that passes says little until it has been seen to fail. Two things are checked before a step is reported, with a command for each: that a test does not pass only because of the order things happened to run in, and that it fails when the rule it tests is broken.

### A seeded order of events

**Where the order came from.** The loop plans each tool call, and bounds each result, on the blocking pool (`cineris_tools::run_blocking`). Those are real threads, and they finish when the machine lets them: a call's plan arrives before or after the response's next event, and one call's plan before or after another's. The loop itself looks at what is ready in a fixed order and picks nothing at random. A test of step 12 failed three runs in ten for this reason, and a break of the code was once reported as caught by a test that had only lost that race.

**What a test of the loop does now.** `cineris-agent`'s rig (`crates/cineris-agent/tests/it/main.rs`, `World`) puts that work on the test's own thread, at moments a seeded generator decides. The session already runs on a single-threaded runtime on that thread, so with the blocking work there too, **one seed is one order of events, on every machine.** That holds for work that goes through `run_blocking`, which is all the loop has. A thread that a test or a tool starts by itself is still the machine's to schedule. And work given to `run_blocking` must not wait for another task of the same runtime (a gate, a channel): on the pool it would be let go, and on the test's only thread it never is.

- **The seam** is `cineris_tools::InlineBlocking`, behind the `test-hooks` feature of `cineris-tools`.
