While one is alive, `run_blocking` called on the thread that made it runs its work on that thread, after a number of turns of the runtime drawn from the seeded generator (none to eight, mostly few). Work whose token fires before its turn comes is not run, and the caller gets "cancelled", as it does from the pool.
- **Only tests have it.** A dev-dependency turns the feature on, and `cargo xtask rules` fails when anything else does. A build of the binary by itself (`cargo build -p cineris-cli`, the `dist` build) resolves `cineris-tools` with no feature, and the released binary has none of it. A build of the whole workspace with its tests (`cargo nextest run --workspace`) resolves it with the feature, for every crate in that build, so the binary the system tests start has the branch compiled in. It does nothing there: the switch is a thread-local that only an `InlineBlocking` sets, and only a test makes one.
- **The seed** comes from `cineris_testkit::seed::for_this_test()`. An ordinary run uses 0, every time. `CINERIS_TEST_SEED=<n>` fixes it. In a run of `cargo xtask stress`, repeat `n` uses the first seed plus `n`.
- **A failed test shows its seed.** The rig prints `test seed 17: run this test again in the same order with CINERIS_TEST_SEED=17`, and the test runner shows a test's output when it fails.
- **A test must pass under every seed.** It asserts on what the loop promises (the results are recorded in the model's order, a refused call stops the turn at that call), and where it needs one order it makes it, with a gate. It does not assert on an order the seed decides.

When the rig was seeded, every test of `cineris-agent` passed under seeds 0 to 299: none depended on an order.

**Other rigs.** A crate whose tests drive the loop or the tools on a single-threaded runtime (the core's, from step 13) takes the same two lines: `cineris-tools` with `test-hooks` in its dev-dependencies, and an `InlineBlocking::seeded(cineris_testkit::seed::for_this_test())` kept for the life of the test. A test whose order is the operating system's to decide (processes, sockets, threads of its own) cannot be seeded, and is repeated instead.

- **The core's own operations' blocking work goes through the same seam** (since the review of step 13a): putting a session together, a fork's and a rewind's work on the log, the end of a session. So a core test's seed orders it with the loop's. What the core does for the loop through its seams is still on real threads, in the machine's order: the session store's writes, recording an approval the user chose to keep, recording a round's changes, reading an image; and so are a command's process and the base state's capture.
- **A test that acts between two steps of an operation** uses `InlineBlocking::after(n)` in place of a seed: every piece of blocking work waits exactly `n` turns of the runtime. With `after(1)`, each piece is one `Pending` of the future waiting for it, so a test that polls the operation by hand knows what is under way when it acts. The core's rig has it as `World::one_turn_each`, and the tests of a client that leaves, and a shutdown that begins, while a session is being put together use it (`crates/cineris-core/tests/it/lifecycle.rs`). Before it, such a test held the runtime's one blocking thread itself (`max_blocking_threads(1)`), which can order the first piece of work and not the second.

### A point the library stops at

**Why.** A race test needs the library to stop at a precise place: between a read and the write that follows it, between a file's new content being complete and the rename that publishes it. Nothing from outside can make it stop there, so such tests relied on timing (a reader that spins until it sees the window), on a thread held by hand, on a FIFO or on a loop that tries until the race happens. Since the seventh tooling step the library marks the place, and the test holds it.

```text
library:   cineris_base::hook::point(cineris_base::atomic::BEFORE_THE_RENAME);
test:      let hold = Hold::new(cineris_base::atomic::BEFORE_THE_RENAME);
           let writer = std::thread::spawn(|| write_atomic(…));
           hold.reach();             // the writer is stopped there
           …                         // read the file, change it
           hold.let_one_go();        // or release()
           writer.join()
```

- **Where it lives.** `cineris_base::hook`, in the lowest crate every library depends on. `point(name)` is the library's side; `Hold` is the test's, behind the `test-hooks` feature of `cineris-base`. A point's name is a sentence a failure can print, and the module that marks it exports it as a constant (`atomic::BEFORE_THE_RENAME`), so that a test cannot misspell it. `cineris_testkit::Hold` is the base crate's `Hold` with what only the test kit can add: its waits are named in the timeout report, `reach()` fails with a message, and the same methods are reached through `Deref`. `cineris-base`'s own tests have no test kit (it depends on `cineris-base`), so they use `cineris_base::hook::Hold` and ask for the feature with a **dev-dependency on the crate itself**, which Cargo allows and which keeps `cargo xtask test -p cineris-base` building the tests that need it (the crate-alone run of a feature-gated test is otherwise dropped). The test kit turns the feature on for every crate that has it as a dev-dependency.
- **With the feature off a point is nothing.** `point` is an empty `const fn` marked `#[inline(always)]`: no code, no symbol, no data, not the load of a flag. Every build of the product has it off. Shown, not argued: the assembly of the whole of `cineris-base` (`cargo rustc -p cineris-base --release --lib -- --emit=asm -C codegen-units=1`) is **byte for byte the same with `write_atomic`'s point and without it** (2,670,711 bytes of assembly each), and holds neither the point's name nor a symbol of the module. Repeat it after a change of `point`.
- **With the feature on and nothing held a point is one load.** It reads a counter, the holds alive in the process, and returns when it is zero: 1.0 ns over an empty loop (1.8 ns against 0.7 ns an iteration, a release build on the second machine, a billion calls). While a test holds some *other* point, a miss takes the registry's lock and a search: 41 ns, and only in a test that holds something. A test build has the feature on for every test and for the binary the system tests start (features are unified across a build of the whole workspace's tests), so there the points do nothing, as `InlineBlocking` does ("A seeded order of events", above).
- **Only tests turn it on.** `cargo xtask rules` holds a `test-hooks` feature to dev-dependencies and the test kit, in any crate that has one (the rule reads the feature's name): a normal or build dependency that asks for it, `default` or another feature of the crate that lists it, and a feature of another crate that passes it on are refused. For `cineris-base` the rule was extended in one place: a dev-dependency of a crate on itself is no edge of the crate table ("Which crate may depend on which"), where a normal one still is refused. The checks of both are unit tests of `rules::crate_graph`.
- **A build of the whole workspace has the hooks.** The test kit is a member of the workspace and turns the feature on for `cineris-base`, and cargo builds one set of features for a build, so `cargo build` at the root, with no `-p`, builds a `cineris` that carries them, each read from a variable in its environment: among them, since CI budgets II, a divisor of the dialogs' readiness times (`hook::ready_divisor`), and since step 28's third pull request the harness's mark of its sessions' commands made to fail (`hook::mark_fails`, which only holds the harness's sessions at `supervised`, failing safe). **A switch that could weaken a guard is not a hook:** the switches of the Windows approving check's own tests, how it lists the harnesses' jobs and whether a test binary counts as Cineris's, are read only under `#[cfg(test)]` in `cineris-sandbox`, which no build but that crate's own tests compiles, so even such a `cineris` holds neither. Every build that is shipped or measured as shipped names its package (`cargo xtask size`, `startup`, `alias`, step 34's `dist`), and `cargo xtask size` refuses a binary that holds the hooks (`shipped::test_hooks_in`): build the binary to use with `cargo xtask alias` or `-p cineris-cli`.
- **What a hold does.** Every thread that arrives at the held point stops until the test lets it go or the hold's bound passes (5 s, `Hold::with_limit` for another). Past the bound the thread goes on, and the hold counts it (`timed_out`), so that a mistake is a failed test and never a hung one. **Every wait of the test's side has a bound too**: `wait_reached(limit)`, `wait_reached_times(n, limit)` and `wait_stopped(n, limit)` return whether the thing happened, and `reach()` fails the test with a message when the library does not arrive.
- **When no test holds a point it passes at once;** a point that nothing reached is a point that has no arrival, and the hold says so (`arrivals()`).
- **The n-th arrival.** `skipping(n)` lets the first `n` arrivals through and stops the next ones: the second write of two, the third read. `let_one_go()` lets one stopped thread go on (one of those stopped, in no order: whichever wakes first; or the next to arrive) and keeps the others; `release()` lets all of them go, and every later arrival pass; dropping the hold releases it, so a test that fails never leaves a thread stopped.
- **Two tests in one process.** `cargo test` runs tests as threads of one process, and a hold is the process's. Two holds of one point do not live together: the second waits, within its bound, for the first to end, and fails with a message that says why when it does not. But any thread of the process that arrives at a held point stops, whichever test it belongs to, because the library cannot know. nextest gives each test a process of its own, which is how the workspace runs its tests, and there a hold is the test's alone. A test that holds a point is written for that: it does not claim to be safe under `cargo test` beside tests that run the same library code.
- **A wrong name is found.** The points that threads reached while any hold was alive are remembered, and a test whose point never came says which they were: a name that differs by a character shows up beside the one the test gave.
- **The timeout report names it** ([When a test runs out of time](#when-a-test-runs-out-of-time)): the test's wait is `waits for the library to reach the hold of the point … made at crates/…:40:16`, and each library thread stopped at a point is `stopped at the library point …, held by a test until it lets the thread go`. A task stopped at an awaited point (below) is listed as `a task polled on <thread>`.
- **First use.** `write_atomic` has one point, `BEFORE_THE_RENAME`: the new content is complete in its temporary file and the target has not been touched. `cineris-base`'s `a_reader_sees_the_old_content_or_the_new_and_never_a_mixture` held a reader spinning on a file while a writer replaced it a hundred times, and saw the window by chance. It now stops the writer there, four times, reads the target three times each (exactly the old content, with the temporary file beside it), lets the writer go and reads the new content. Every assertion is kept (reads were made, none was neither version, the replaces happened, the last content is one of the two, nothing is left in the directory). A second test, `a_file_that_is_created_is_not_there_until_its_content_is_complete`, looks at the directory at the same stop: the name is not there yet, which a reader that looks at the end of a write cannot see, and which no test saw before (its break creates the empty file early). **A break that the spinning reader also catches was measured:** a write that truncates the target before the rename left the target empty for the whole of the content's write, and the old test failed it at once (326,359 of 326,839 reads saw neither version). The point does not find more of those; it finds the state at a place nobody could stop at, and makes the finding the same on every run, in 11 ms and 12 reads, where the old test made 100 replaces and about 300,000 reads.
- **How it is tested.** The hold's own behaviour in `cineris-base`'s unit tests (a thread stops and goes on after the release, the bound, the n-th arrival, one let go and the others kept, a second hold, a dropped hold, the points remembered); the test kit's in its own (the report names a stopped thread, a point that never came says what did, a hold is named by where it was made); breaks of each in `mutations/tooling-7.breaks`.
- **A second writer between a read and its write, where the lock is the system's** (since step 28's close). A test cannot wait for a thread blocked in the operating system's lock. It holds the first writer at a point between its read and its write (`cineris_policy::trust::BEFORE_THE_PINS_ARE_WRITTEN`), asks the lock file itself whether it is held (a second open's `try_lock`), starts the second writer, and only where the lock is not held waits for the second to stop there too: held, the second waits and keeps the first one's change; not held, one change is lost every time (`pins::a_writer_between_anothers_read_and_write_waits_and_both_keep_their_pins`; the same check without a point in `cineris-core`'s `ext::state::tests::two_writers_of_a_projects_file_keep_both_values`). A test that started its writers together and let them race caught a lock let go too early only when the race fell that way.
- **An extension call's thread left running, held, not raced** (since step 28's close). A test that needs a call's thread left running holds it at `cineris_ext::engine::A_CALL_IS_STARTED` through the call's time and the grace, and lets it go once the abandon is judged: the call's deadline is set when the thread says the call started, before the thread looks whether its runtime was stopped, so moving the clock then races that look (`extensions_hooks::an_extension_whose_runtime_cannot_start_fails_its_gates`; the other order, a call stopped before it runs, in `a_call_stopped_before_it_runs_leaves_its_extension_startable`). A test whose subject is the native loop itself has the script call `cineris.notify` just before the loop, and moves the clock once it has seen it.

**A point on an async task** (since the ninth tooling step). `point` stops the thread that arrives. A core test runs the core on the test's own current-thread runtime, and with `InlineBlocking` the core's blocking work runs there too ("A seeded order of events", above), so a point that the core's task reaches stops the one thread that could let it go, or do what the test wanted done while it was stopped: a hold there deadlocks the test until the bound. The test of a revoke made during a change of level could not hold the change; it counted the blocking work's turns instead (`World::one_turn_each`), which an await added to the change would have moved, with the test still passing and testing nothing.

```text
library:   cineris_base::hook::await_point!(cineris_core::BEFORE_THE_NEW_LEVEL_IS_IN_FORCE);
test:      let hold = Hold::new(cineris_core::BEFORE_THE_NEW_LEVEL_IS_IN_FORCE);
           let mut level = std::pin::pin!(front.op(Op::SessionSet(…)));
           hold.drive_until_reached(level.as_mut()).await;   // pending at the point
           …                                                  // revoke, on the same thread
           hold.release();
           level.await
```

- **The library's side is a macro, `await_point!(NAME)`**, written as a statement in an async fn or block. With the `test-hooks` feature it is `hook::point_async(NAME).await`: a future that, at a held point, records the stop as a thread's is recorded and is pending; the task's thread goes on with the runtime's other tasks. `release`, `let_one_go` and the hold's bound wake it, and the same `Hold` holds either kind of point (`skipping`, `arrivals`, `stopped`, `timed_out`, `seen` and the timeout report count a task as they count a thread). A task dropped while it is stopped is stopped no longer. With nothing held the first poll is the same one load as `point`'s, and ready.
- **It is a macro because an await is never nothing.** The choice was a function returning a ready future, a mode of `Hold`, or a macro. A mode of `Hold` would leave the library's side a blocking call, and only the library knows whether its place is on a task. And a function is not nothing in the product's build: compiled with `-C opt-level=3`, an `async fn` that awaits `std::future::ready(())` in the middle is 1,446 bytes of assembly longer than the same fn without it (a state of its own, and the panic for a `Ready` polled twice), and with a unit future whose `poll` is `Ready` and `#[inline(always)]`, still a state in the jump table and a future 8 bytes larger, because a value that lives across an await is kept in the future whether or not the await can be pending: the state machine is laid out before the optimiser sees that it cannot. Without the feature the macro expands to `let _: &'static str = NAME;`, which checks the name's type and is no code: no await, no state, no symbol.
- **Where no thread can bridge, a thread does.** `cineris-base` has no runtime and no timer. A pending future of the hook hands its waker to a thread of its own, which waits on the hold's condition variable until what the future waits for is so (let go, released, or dropped) or the bound passes, wakes the task and ends; a task still pending after it (another took the permit) arms another. The thread is a bound, as the blocking point's `wait_timeout` is: a passing test never waits for it in real time.
- **The test's side.** `Hold::wait_reached_times_async(n, limit)` in the base crate is `wait_reached_times` as a future, for a test whose library runs on its own thread. The test kit's `Hold::drive_until_reached(operation)` (and `drive_until_reached_times(n, operation)`) polls an operation that the test drives (a `Pin<&mut F>`, the way `poll_once` takes it), with the wait for the stop beside it, and returns with the operation pending at the point; it fails the test when the operation finishes without stopping there, or with `reach()`'s message when no stop comes within its bound.
- **First use.** `cineris-core`'s change of level (`Core::set_level`) awaits `BEFORE_THE_NEW_LEVEL_IS_IN_FORCE` after it has made its engine (with the approvals of the engine in force then) and written its lease, before it hands the engine over (`knowledge.put_in_force` or `policy.replace`). `redecide::a_revoke_made_while_the_level_changes_is_not_lost` drives the change there, revokes on the same thread, lets it go and checks that the revoke was not lost. It no longer counts turns: it runs under the seeded order like every other core test, and asserts that the engine in force still had the approval when it revoked and that the change was let go, not timed out. Its breaks (`step-12-redecide/a-new-engine-keeps-the-approvals-it-was-made-with`, `an-answer-recorded-round-the-lock`, `a-revoke-made-round-the-lock`) still name it.
- **How it is tested.** `cineris-base`'s unit tests poll the future by hand (nobody holds it: ready at once; held: pending, and its thread goes on, until the release wakes it; one of two let go; a task woken for a permit a thread took first is woken again by the release; the bound; the n-th arrival; a dropped task; the awaited wait for a stop); the test kit's drive an operation on their own thread to the point and past it, and fail when it finishes first or never stops. Breaks in `mutations/tooling-9.breaks`.

### Running the tests many times over

`cargo xtask stress -p <crate>… [--count N] [--first-seed S]` runs the crates' tests `N` times over (100 when not given), and reports each test that failed on some repeat:

```text
1 test(s), 40 time(s) each, with seeds 0 to 39.

FAILED 20 of 40 times: cineris-agent::it order::stress_fixture_expects_the_first_sessions_call_to_start_first
  seeds: 0, 1, 2, 6, 7, 8, 11, 14, 16, 17, 18, 22, and 8 more
  again: CINERIS_TEST_SEED=0 cargo xtask test -p cineris-agent -E 'test(=order::stress_fixture_expects_the_first_sessions_call_to_start_first)'
```

That is the command's own test case, a test that is wrong on purpose (below). Seed 0 fails it every time and seed 3 passes it every time.

- The repeating is nextest's (`--stress-count`, in a nextest profile of its own, `stress`). The command sets the first seed, reads which repeat of which test failed from the JUnit report, and says how to run one failure again. Anything it does not know goes to nextest: a filter, `-E`, `--run-ignored`.
- **Each failing test's message is printed** (since the fifth tooling step), read from the same report: under the test's seeds, its first failure's text, which is the panic's message for a test that panicked (the thread's name taken off, as the test is named) and, for a timeout, what the test wrote to its standard error, where the test kit's timeout report is. Six lines at most, each cut at 160 characters, with how many more there were; then the first line of up to two other failures of the test whose text differs (digits aside: a seed, a process id, a time), the count of the rest, and the report's path for all of it:

  ```text
  FAILED 2 of 3 times: jx tests::flaky
    seeds: 40, 42
    failure, under seed 40:
      | panicked at src/lib.rs:11:9:
      | assertion `left == right` failed: the repeat was even: 0
      | with a second line
      | and a third
      | and a fourth
      | and a fifth
      | … and 4 more line(s)
    all of it: target/nextest/stress/junit.xml
    again: CINERIS_TEST_SEED=40 cargo xtask test -p jx -E 'test(=tests::flaky)'
  ```

  A test that passed only on its retry shows the failure it needed the retry for. Its tests read a report nextest wrote for the purpose (`xtask/fixtures/stress/junit.xml`).
- **Timeouts that end together are taken for the machine** (since the sixth tooling step; `xtask/src/stall.rs`). A test that hangs by itself is stopped at its own limit, counted from its own start. When a shared machine's disk stalls, the test runner's timers are late too and fire together when it comes back, so a dozen tests of crates nobody changed are stopped within moments of each other whatever time each started. When three or more of a run's timeouts end (their start plus their time, from the JUnit report) within 250 ms of each other, `stress`, `ci` (each test run that fails) and `mutate` say so, with the evidence:

  ```text
  STALL: 8 tests timed out within 140 ms of each other: likely the machine, not the tests.
    cineris-session::it durability::…
    …
    I/O pressure (/proc/pressure/io) now: some task waited 40.0% of the last 10 s and 15.5% of the last 60 s; every task at once 30.0% and 10.0%.
    During this run's 312 s, some task waited on I/O for 41.3 s, and every task at once for 30.2 s.
    Run them again, alone or with fewer jobs, before reading anything into these timeouts.
  ```

  The pressure is read when the run starts and again at its end, so the stall time is the run's own. Where there is no `/proc/pressure/io` (Windows, or a kernel without it) the message says that it could not be read and why. One test's timeout, two together, or timeouts a second apart are not a stall, **and nor are tests stopped on time:** a test that hangs, by itself or on a bug it shares with others, is stopped at its own limit (10 s, 60 s for a system test, 300 s in the `properties` run, 1,800 s for a measurement; a test holds the list to `.config/nextest.toml`), and only a test stopped more than 250 ms past its limit counts. Two reviews of the step found the cases that rule is for: a break in `mutate` that hangs a crate's tests stops the first wave of them together, and stops system tests at 60 s in the same moment as a later wave of 10 s ones; taken for the machine's, such a break was never judged. What it cannot tell: a stall short enough that the runner's timers were not late.
- **`ci` runs a cluster of timeouts again, once, when the disk stalled** (since the eighth tooling step; `xtask/src/stall/rerun.rs`). In wave 11 six sessions of seven lost time to a stalled disk on a shared machine: a person had to read the output, see that it was the machine, and run the tests again by hand. Each of the two test runs of `cargo xtask ci test` (`run tiers 1 and 2`, `run the system tests`) now does that when it fails, under rules that never let a failure through. **It runs tests again only when all three hold:**
  1. the run failed;
  2. its failures hold a cluster that the check above finds: three or more timeouts, each stopped late, ending within 250 ms of each other (a lone timeout, timeouts at their own limits, or timeouts apart are never one; a test that passed on its retry is not a failure of the run, so it is not counted);
  3. the pressure says the disk stalled during the run: every task waited on I/O for **10% or more of the run's time** (`full` of `/proc/pressure/io`, its counter after the run less its value when the run started, over the run's wall time; the threshold is `xtask/src/stall.rs`' `STALLED_SHARE` and is the one of the tier check, with the reasons, above).

  **What it runs again** is the cluster's tests, once, each named by its binary and its name exactly (`-E '(binary_id(=cineris-session::it) and test(=durability::…)) or …'`, in place of the run's own filter, with the run's other arguments), and no others. The run's other timeouts, a test that failed in any other way, and a timeout that is not in the cluster are not run again and still fail the step; a test whose name a filter cannot be trusted to name exactly (characters other than letters, digits, `_ : / . -`) is not run again either. **What it leaves in the report** is the run's own report with the cases of those tests replaced by the cases the re-run wrote (`xtask/src/junit.rs`, `replace_cases`; the failure counts of each suite and of the whole are made again from the cases), so that the tier check and `ci-times` read one report, with the times the tests really took. **The step passes only when,** after that, the report holds no failed case, the re-run's command succeeded, and every test run again is in the re-run's report and passed. A test that times out again, one that fails, one that is missing from the re-run's report, and everything else that failed, each fails the step, which says which. The output says what it did:

  ```text
  STALL: 8 tests timed out within 140 ms of each other: likely the machine, not the tests.
    …
  Running the 8 tests that timed out together again, once: the disk stalled during the run (every task waited on I/O for 41.3 s of its 312 s, 13.2%; …). A test that times out again, and every other failure, still fails this step.
    cineris-session::it durability::… passed on the re-run
    …
  All 8 tests passed on the re-run, and nothing else failed: the step passes. Their times in the report are the re-run's.
  ```

  and a cluster that is not run again says why (`Not running them again: the disk did not stall during the run: every task waited on I/O for 1.2% of its time, and a run is taken to have stalled at 10%.`, or that the pressure could not be read). The job's summary page lists the tests run again and how each did. **On CI and on Windows:** the same rules hold on CI (`cargo xtask ci` is what CI runs). Ubuntu's kernels give `/proc/pressure/io`, so on the Linux test job a cluster of late timeouts during a stall of a tenth of the run is run again there too, by the same rules, and says so in the log and on the summary page; the tier budgets are judged there whatever the pressure (above). Windows never gives the file, and where the pressure cannot be read a cluster is a failure that is not run again: so on Windows nothing changes. **`CINERIS_PRESSURE_FILE`** names a file, in the kernel's form (`some avg10=… total=…` and `full …` lines), to read in place of `/proc/pressure/io`, on any system: it is how the tests give the decisions a recorded pressure and never a real stall, and how a person tries one by hand (the commands read it once when a run starts and once when it ends, so a fixed file says no time stalled; `mutate`'s one-job rule reads it too). **How it is tested:** the decisions are functions of the failures, the pressures and the reports (`stall::rerun::decide`, `verdicts`, `run`, `stall::judge`, `junit::replace_cases`), and the tests of them (`stall::rerun::tests`, `stall::tests`, `junit::tests`, `tiers::tests`) give them pressures and reports written for the purpose: a cluster with a stall is run again; one with no stall, one with no pressure to read, a lone timeout, and a cluster of tests that passed on a retry are not; a timeout that recurs, a test missing from the re-run, a re-run whose command failed and a failure of another kind beside a re-run that passed each fail the step; the report left has the re-run's cases and true counts. Nothing runs nextest or stalls a disk. What it cannot tell: that a stall short enough to leave the pressure under 10% of the run made the timeouts (the step fails, and a person runs the tests again); that the first run's other tests, which ran in the stall too, were slowed (the report keeps their times, and the tier check does not judge such a run locally, above); and the Windows side, where it never re-runs, is untested there beyond the unit tests, which run on both systems. **Not changed:** `ci --tiers`, `ab` and `stress` do not re-run after a stall: they are measurements, and a stalled one is run again by a person. (A known flake is run again once by `stress` and `ci`, by the rules of "Known flakes", below.)
- **A test that passed only on its retry counts as failed for that repeat.** nextest gives a test in a `timing_sensitive` module one retry, and reports it as passed when the retry passes. A test that needs its retry some of the time is what this command looks for.
- **For a seeded rig** each repeat is another order, and the seed brings a failure back exactly. **For process and thread tests** each repeat is another roll of the machine's own dice, and the seed means nothing: a test that fails one time in two hundred there is still a test with a race in it.
- **`--cores K --load N` squeezes the run:** the command pins itself, and with it cargo, nextest and every test process, to the first `K` cores it may use, and runs `N` busy loops beside them on the same cores. A race whose window is a few microseconds on an idle machine opens wide enough to be hit. The tests are built first, unpinned. On Linux the pin is `taskset`'s; on Windows PowerShell sets the process's affinity mask. The busy loops are threads of the command, so they end with it however it ends. The SIGINT flake of step 13b failed about once in a thousand runs on an idle machine and was found only this way, by hand.
- **Run it before reporting a step,** for `cineris-agent` and for every crate whose tests start processes or cross threads: `cargo xtask stress -p cineris-agent --count 300` takes 17 s on the reference machine. Stress first, then break the code: a test that fails by chance makes a break look caught.
- **How it is tested.** Its reading of a report, on reports written for the purpose (unit tests). And by hand on the test above, `order::stress_fixture_…` in `cineris-agent`: it expects the first of two sessions' calls to start first, which nothing promises, so it fails under about half the seeds. It is ignored in every ordinary run; `cargo xtask stress -p cineris-agent --count 40 --run-ignored only stress_fixture` runs it.

### Known flakes

A test that fails now and then for a reason not yet fixed is an open item, and since the tenth tooling step the item's file names the test: `` - Flaky: `cineris-core::it trust::sys_…` ``, its binary and its name as nextest's report gives them ([outstanding/README.md](outstanding/README.md)). The list of known flakes is these lines and nothing else (`xtask/src/flaky.rs`), so it cannot drift from the items: settling the item, or taking the line out, takes the test off the list. Wave 12's sessions stopped, diagnosed and ran again by hand each time one of them failed in code they had not touched, and a `mutate` baseline that stopped on one lost a quarter of an hour.

- **Who reads it:** `stress`, `ci` away from CI (each of its two test runs, after the stall's re-run above), and `mutate`'s run of the tests with nothing broken.
- **What they do when a listed test fails:** name it with its item (`cineris-tools::it changes::sys_… failed, and is a known flake: docs/outstanding/a-git-look-too-slow-on-a-loaded-windows-runner.md`), run it again **once**, by its binary and its name exactly and with no retry (`--retries 0`, whatever nextest's profile gives the test), and go on when that run passes. The case in the report is then the second run's, so the tier check and `ci-times` read its real time.
- **What still fails:** a listed test that fails its second run (`FAILED AGAIN`, `TIMED OUT AGAIN`, or missing from that run's report), a second run whose command failed, and every failure of a test that is not listed. **A test that is not listed is never run again.** `ci` and `stress` run a listed test again beside other failures, and still fail; `mutate`'s baseline runs nothing again when anything else failed beside it.
- **When nothing is run again, though a known flake failed:** a run of nextest (the first, or `ci`'s re-run after a stall) that ended in another way than with failed tests (exit status 100): a setup script, a build, a crash, which a report's failures do not explain and a flake that passed would hide; and in `ci`, a known flake that was in a stall's cluster and was run again there already, which is not run a third time.
- **Every second run is printed and counted:** `ci` says each verdict and puts a table on the job's summary page; `stress` says how many known flakes passed when run once more and are not counted as failures of the run; `mutate` says `N tests pass, 1 of them a known flake on its second run`.
- **`stress` runs a known flake once more under the seed it first failed with**, with the run's other arguments and inside the same `--cores`/`--load` squeeze, and puts the stress run's report back afterwards. **`stress --strict`** runs nothing again, so every failure counts: for a session that is fixing a known flake and stresses it to show the fix.
- **CI's blocking jobs run nothing again.** Under GitHub Actions (`GITHUB_ACTIONS` set, as `xtask` reads it everywhere) `ci` names a listed test that failed, with its item, on the log and the summary page, and fails as before: a flake on a pull request is still the person's to judge and to run again, and the blocking checks keep their meaning.
- **A `try` run is not a blocking job:** `stress` and `mutate` dispatched through `try` run a known flake once more as they do locally. To measure a known flake on a runner, give `stress --strict`.
- **The marks of today** (the tenth tooling step): `cineris-tools`' GitTooSlow ([a-git-look-too-slow-on-a-loaded-windows-runner](outstanding/a-git-look-too-slow-on-a-loaded-windows-runner.md)) and `cineris-index`'s memory peak ([a-memory-peak-test-near-its-limit](outstanding/a-memory-peak-test-near-its-limit.md)). The leader-close test of step 23 is not marked: its cause was fixed and its item settled. Nor are `cineris-core`'s tests of the base capture beside the turn's git any more (the second small fixes): a test that reads the session's `base` record waits for the capture to be over (`BaseCapture` in the core's test rig, at the point `cineris_session::BASE_STATE_HANDED_OVER`), before the close, which waits for it only so long, and before a turn whose git could hold the index's lock beside it.
- **How it is tested:** the decisions are functions of reports and runs written for the purpose (`flaky::tests`: a listed flake that passes, one that fails again, a failure that is not listed alone and beside a flake, CI and `GITHUB_ACTIONS` set to anything, a run that ended other than by failed tests, a flake already run again after a stall, a second run with no report, missing from it, or whose command failed, the one run's arguments; `stress::tests`: which flakes and seeds, the run's command, what is excused, and `known_flakes` with a pretend run: passed, failed again, nextest failed though the report passed, `--strict`, a stress run that ended other than by failed tests, not listed; `mutate::drive::tests`: a pretend runner's baseline with a flake that passes alone, fails again, stalls, fails beside another failure, or is not listed; `checks::tests`: a joined `-E` filter is taken out). `flaky::tests::every_known_flake_names_a_test_where_its_binary_and_module_put_it` holds each mark to a function in the file its binary and first module name. Not reached: `ci`'s call that reads `GITHUB_ACTIONS` and the exit statuses (the functions it calls are tested), and `stress`'s start of cargo for the run once more. Breaks: `mutations/tooling-10.breaks`, `flaky-*`.

### When a test runs out of time

nextest stops a test at its tier's limit and says only that it timed out. Since the second tooling step, a test that uses the test kit's waits says what it was doing first (`cineris_testkit::watch`).

- **When.** The first time a test process uses a `Gate`'s blocking wait, the scripted server, a `Roll` or `children::until`, one thread starts that does nothing until the test has used four fifths of its limit: 8 s of 10 in tiers 1 and 2, 48 s of 60 for a system test, 240 s of 300 in the `properties` profile, read from the profile nextest names (`NEXTEST_PROFILE`) and the test's name. A test unit-tests that these agree with `.config/nextest.toml`. On Linux the time is counted from the process's start; elsewhere from the first wait ([outstanding](outstanding/timeout-report-timing-on-windows.md)). If the test is still running then, the report goes to its standard error, the waits first and then the process tree, which on Windows takes PowerShell a moment, which nextest shows with the failure and keeps in the JUnit report. A test that passes never reaches it, and pays for one thread that waits and a lock when a wait starts and ends.
- **What it says.** Each wait in progress, longest first, with the thread that waits: the gate, named by where the test made it (`the gate made at crates/…/x.rs:40:16`, from `#[track_caller]`); the scripted server's connection by number, method and path, and the step of its script (reading the request, holding the response until a gate opens, waiting for a gate before step 3 of 5, holding the connection until the client hangs up); a child of a roll by name and process id; a thread of the library that a test's `Hold` has stopped at a point of its code, by the point and for how long (since the seventh tooling step, [A point the library stops at](#a-point-the-library-stops-at)). Then the process tree under the test, each process with its state and command line. On Linux, each thread's name, state and wait channel (the kernel function it sleeps in), and its kernel stack where the system lets it be read (as root). On Windows, each thread's state and wait reason, asked of PowerShell; thread stacks are not read there.
- **The test's own children** (since the twelfth tooling step), after the waits: each process the harness or the test kit started for the test (`cineris_base::hook::spawned`, marked by the sandbox's process tree, the harness's git, the pseudo-terminal and a `Roll`'s children), with its process id, how long ago it started, its command line, and what became of it: it exited and its wait returned (and after how long), it is still running, it exited and nothing has waited for it (a zombie, on Linux), or it is gone. Only the sandbox's tree, which starts every command of the runner, marks its wait (`spawned_tracked`), so only for its children does the report say that a child is gone while the wait of the code that started it has not returned: beside a thread still waiting for that end, a missed wake. Of the others it says only that they are gone. A child still running is a late exit. Linux reads `/proc`; Windows asks the same PowerShell that reads the tree, and when PowerShell gives no answer, the report says the system was not asked. A child is found by its id alone, which the system may give a new process once the child is gone (soon, on Windows).
- **Not covered.** A test that uses none of the test kit's waits gets no report, unless it calls `cineris_testkit::watch::doing("…")` or `watch::arm()` itself. Rust offers no way to read another thread's stack from inside the process, so the report says what each wait is, not where in the code each thread is.
- **The fake timer's waits are in it** (since the fifth tooling step): a wait of a manual `FakeTimer` is named while it runs, with its length and the fake time it was made at, and that it ends only when the test moves the clock. The core's rig hung on such a wait (the shell's grace for a process left running) for a minute with nothing printed; now the report at 8 s says so.
- **A question nobody answers fails a rig's test at once** (since the fifth tooling step). The core rig's `Front` waits for a turn's end in `turns()`; an approval request that arrives meanwhile, and that the test did not take up with `approval()`, is answered by the front's `Answering` (`allow_all()`, `deny_all()` or `with(…)`, set with `front.answering(…)`), and with none the test fails, naming the call, once what is already on its way has arrived. A turn that asked what the test did not expect used to wait for ever. The agent rig's `ScriptedPolicy` answered every unscripted question by allowing the call, which could hide a question that should not have been asked: it now refuses such a call, so nothing runs, and fails the test when its rig goes, naming the call, unless the test answers with `answer_for(tool, …)` or `answer_all(…)`. (A panic in the question would not do: the loop makes a question's panic an error for its call, and goes on.)
- **Outside nextest it does nothing,** and never in a child a test started from its own binary.
- **How it is tested.** Against a test that hangs on purpose (`watch::tests::hangs_on_purpose`, which does nothing unless started for this): a gate nobody opens, a scripted server holding its response, and a child process. A system test runs it with a limit of one second (`CINERIS_WATCH_LIMIT_MS`), reads the report, kills the tree and checks it names both gates, the connection and the child. It is ignored in the blocking checks, where it would cost a second of the system tier and more on Windows; the nightly workflow runs it on both OSes.

**Widening a race on purpose.** `CINERIS_TESTKIT_PAUSE_MS=<n>` makes the scripted server pause `n` milliseconds before each write and before opening each gate of its script. A race between what the server writes and what the test does next, which a loaded machine loses once in a thousand runs, then loses every time, so that a fix can be shown to hold under the same widening. It is read once per process, is off unless set, and nothing in the product reads it. A test in the nightly run checks that four writes take at least four pauses.

### Where a test's time goes

nextest says how long a test took. `CINERIS_TESTKIT_PROFILE=1` makes a test that uses the test kit say where the time went (`cineris_testkit::profile`, since the fourth tooling step):

```text
CINERIS_TESTKIT_PROFILE=1 cargo xtask test -p cineris-cli -E 'test(=commands::sys_x)' --no-capture
```

```text
time profile of `sys_trust` (CINERIS_TESTKIT_PROFILE): 218.9 ms from the process's start
         at        took  what
     0.0 ms      0.0 ms  process start, before the test kit was first used
     0.0 ms      0.7 ms  phase: set-up
     2.8 ms     27.7 ms  phase: the binary runs to its end
    28.2 ms      0.1 ms    wait: the scripted server's connection 1: reading the request [scripted-server-1]
    …
               211.2 ms  in phases and tear-down; 1.0 ms in the test kit's waits, on any thread; 7.7 ms in none of them (the test's own work)
```

- **What it shows:** the process's start before the test kit was first used (on Linux, from the system's record of when the process started, to its clock tick of 10 ms); the phases a rig marks (`set-up` in the agent, core and binary rigs, a core started, a session opened, a turn, the binary run to its end, tear-down from the rig's `Drop`) and any a test names with `profile::phase("…")`; and every wait of the test kit's own (a gate, each step of the scripted server's script, a roll's child, `until`), the same waits the timeout report names, with the thread that waited when it is not the test's.
- **When it is written:** when the test's [`Scratch`](../crates/cineris-testkit/src/scratch.rs) goes, which every rig holds, so a rig's tests need no change. nextest shows a passing test's output with `--no-capture`.
- **It costs nothing when it is off.** The variable is read once; after that each mark is one load of a flag. No clock is read, nothing is allocated and no lock is taken. A unit test holds that.
- **On a hosted runner** (since the fifth tooling step), where a passing test's output is not kept: `CINERIS_TESTKIT_PROFILE_DIR` names a directory that each test's report is also written to, a file a test, named for its binary, its name and its process. `try`'s input `-f testkit=profile` sets both variables and uploads the directory with the run's log: `gh workflow run try --ref <branch> -f os=windows -f command=test -f testkit=profile -f filter='kind(test) & !test(/(^|::)sys_/)'` is tier 2 profiled on a Windows runner.
- **Shown in the fourth tooling step** on the slowest test of each rig ([steps/tooling-4/answered.md](steps/tooling-4/answered.md)).

### Where a measurement's time goes

The profile is opt-in and is about the test kit's waits. A `perf_` test (a measurement, in its own file, in a release build: [performance-budgets.md](performance-budgets.md)) wants something else, and always: which of its stretches took the time, from its first run on any machine, without a `try` round trip to find out. step 18's needed three. `cineris_testkit::Phases` (since the sixth tooling step) names the stretches and prints a breakdown when the test is done:

```text
phases of `perf_a_cold_index_a_warm_one_and_a_warm_map`: 297.3 ms in all
  phase                             took   share  times
  generate the tree              24.0 ms    8.1%
  cold pass                      11.0 ms    3.7%
  change a file                   0.5 ms    0.2%  15
  warm pass                     139.9 ms   47.0%  15
  warm map                       18.9 ms    6.3%  15
  breakdown of a warm pass       82.3 ms   27.7%
  tear-down                      20.7 ms    7.0%
  outside every phase             0.1 ms    0.0%
```

That is the first test of `cineris-index`'s `perf.rs` on a tree of 3,000 files (`CINERIS_PERF_FILES=3000`, a release build on the reference machine, a run of 0.3 s).

- **The calls:** `let phases = Phases::new("perf_…")`; `phases.measure("name", || work)` runs the work as a phase and returns its value; `let _p = phases.phase("name")` is a phase until the guard is dropped; `phases.mark("name")` ends the phase the last `mark` began and begins another, `phases.end()` ends it, for a test written as a run of stretches. `phases.took("name")` is a phase's time so far, for a test that holds one stretch to a bound of its own; `phases.report()` prints at once and returns the text.
- **Phases of one name are added up** and shown in the order their names first appeared, with the number of times when it is more than one. What is outside every phase is shown too, so a stretch nobody named is not lost, and the shares add up to the whole. A phase inside a phase is counted twice: use phases for stretches that follow one another.
- **It prints when it is dropped,** unless `report()` printed already, so a test that fails half-way still says where its time had gone. The text is on standard output: nextest shows it for a failing test, and for a passing one with `--no-capture`; `cargo test … -- --nocapture` for the release runs the perf tests are made for.
- **It is always on and costs two clock readings and a push for each phase.** With `CINERIS_TESTKIT_PROFILE=1` each phase is also a phase of the test's [time profile](#where-a-tests-time-goes), beside the waits, so the two reports agree on the names.
- **Used in** `cineris-index`'s `perf_a_cold_index_a_warm_one_and_a_warm_map` (tree, cold pass, each warm round's change, pass and map, the breakdown of a pass, tear-down). A new `perf_` test begins with `Phases::new`.
- **How it is tested:** on a fake clock (`Phases::with_clock`), so nothing waits: the times and shares, phases of one name, marks and ends, the shares adding to the whole, a report printed once, an empty test, and the grouping of thousands.

### A throwaway test

A review that confirms a finding writes a failing test for it. `cargo xtask scratch-test CRATE FILE [--cargo-profile P] [--ignored | --include-ignored] [--no-capture] [-- NEXTEST ARGS]` runs the tests of one file as a module of the crate's integration tests (`use crate::…` reaches the binary's helpers) with nothing registered by hand:

- **The working tree is only read.** The run is made in a copy of the tree under `target/scratch-test/tree`, with a build directory of its own: the copy is brought up to date with the working tree (what git tracks and does not ignore, as `mutate`'s copies), the file goes beside `tests/it/main.rs` as `scratch_<its name>.rs` with its `mod` line, nextest runs that module alone, and the copy is brought up to date again, which takes it out. A run that is killed leaves the module in the copy, which git ignores, and the next run takes it out.
- It fails when a test fails, and when no test ran.
- **Arguments after `--` go to nextest,** and a filterset among them narrows the file's tests: `cargo xtask scratch-test cineris-stats f.rs -- -E 'test(/stuck/)'` runs the tests of `f.rs` whose names match (since the sixth tooling step; before it, nextest took the given filterset beside the module's own and ran every test of the file). Without a `.git`, the tree is read as `mutate` reads it: every file outside `.git` and any `target` directory.
- **The first run builds** the crate and what it needs in the copy's build directory (about 10 s for `cineris-base` on the reference machine); later runs rebuild what changed. One run at a time.
- **A measurement is a throwaway test too** (since the seventh tooling step). A review's measurement is an ignored `perf_` test, and a number from a debug build says nothing about the optimised one, so the file can be built and run as one:
  - **`--cargo-profile P`** builds and runs the file in cargo profile `P` (`release`, `ci`, `dist`...): nextest's `--cargo-profile`. The profile has a build directory of its own inside the copy's, so the first run of a profile builds the crate and what it needs from cold (15.4 s for `cineris-base` in `release` on the reference machine, 2.7 s for the next run, the measurement itself 0.02 s; a crate with a heavier tree is minutes). `cargo xtask try` and its `perf` command ("Running an experiment on the runners") are for a number from a hosted runner.
  - **`--ignored`** runs the file's ignored tests **alone**, the meaning the flag has in `cargo test`; **`--include-ignored`** runs them with the file's others (nextest's `--run-ignored only` and `all`). Both together are refused. A run of ignored tests is a measurement, so it also shows the tests' output (`--no-capture`, which runs one test at a time) and runs under nextest's `perf` profile, whose limit on a test is half an hour where the default stops one at 10 s. A nextest profile or `--no-capture` given after `--` is the caller's and is not given twice (nextest refuses a flag twice). `--no-capture` alone shows the output of an ordinary run.
  - **What it keeps:** the working tree is only read, the module is taken out of the copy again, and the run fails when no test ran. `cargo xtask scratch-test cineris-index f.rs --cargo-profile release --ignored -- -E 'test(/perf_/)'` is a review's measurement of one test.

### A reviewer's quick start

What a review needs in its first hour, in the order it needs it (since the ninth tooling step). The kit for the hard findings is the next section.

1. **Keep your own files in a folder of yours under `target/`** (`target/review/` here). Git ignores `target/`, and a clean of the repository's sources never touches it: a finding's test, a log, a run's report, a list of breaks. Nothing there reaches a commit.
2. **Run a test file without registering it:** `cargo xtask scratch-test <crate> target/review/review_x.rs` runs the file as a module of the crate's integration tests in a copy of the tree, and fails when no test ran ("A throwaway test"). `-- -E 'test(/name/)'` narrows it; `--cargo-profile release --ignored` makes it a measurement.
3. **Drive the TUI's `App` without a terminal:** `cineris-tui`'s test kit (the feature `test-kit`, as a dev-dependency) gives the scripted link the application speaks to in place of a core, a `SessionState` builder, the capabilities, and the steps a test repeats: open a session, ask a question, wait for its dialog ("Testing the TUI"). Each input is given its time, so typing, a paste and a held key are told apart by `cineris_testkit`'s typing helper, with no real waiting.
4. **Drive the real binary under a pseudo-terminal:** `cineris-cli`'s system tests start it with `Machine::tui(&[…])`, a `cineris_testkit::pty::Session`: `send` types bytes, `wait_for` waits for a screen, `answer_dialog` answers a dialog and waits for what only the answer brings, `wait_exit` reads how it ended. A wait is bounded, and on a timeout the test kit says what the test was waiting for. On Windows the terminal is ConPTY, which re-renders ([conpty.md](conpty.md)).
5. **Drive the core without a client of your own:** the core's rig (`crates/cineris-core/tests/it/main.rs`) has a `World` and a `Front`: `turn`, `turn_allowing`, `turn_denying` and `turn_answering` run a turn and answer what it asks; `approval()` waits for a question and fails at once when the turn ends without one; `told("…")` waits for a notice; `results()` gives each call's tool, outcome and text, and an outcome compared with a name no outcome has (`"ok"`) fails the test.
6. **Hand the breaks back:** a confirmed finding comes with the test, the command that runs it, and the break that shows the rule untested ("A reviewer's kit", below): one break with `cargo xtask mutate --break FILE --find '…' --replace '…' --test NAME`, or a list of your own in `target/review/<name>.breaks` run with `mutate --file`. A step built in several parts keeps `mutations/<step>-<part>.breaks` for each part, merged with `mutate --merge <step> <part>…`.
7. **Ask a runner:** `cargo xtask try --dispatch <branch> --command "test -p <crate>" --os windows` dispatches the `try` workflow and prints the run's id; `cargo xtask try-result <run>` reads it when it ends ("A run on the runners without a pull request").

### A reviewer's kit

A review that confirms a finding hands over three things: **the failing test's file, the command that runs it, and the break that shows the rule untested.** The first two are `cargo xtask scratch-test <crate> <file>` ("A throwaway test", above), which registers nothing and leaves the tree as it was; the third is a break kept in a file of the reviewer's own, `cargo xtask mutate --file <list>` ("Breaking the code on purpose"), or, for one break, `cargo xtask mutate --break <file> --find '<code>' --replace '<code>' --test <name>`, which needs no list. On the code as it is, the break must make the test fail; and the test must pass without the break. The kit is what a review needs to write such a test for the findings that are hard to provoke: a race, a second process, a disk that does not answer, a huge allocation. Each technique below is one that a test in the repository already uses, which passes in the blocking tiers and fails when its rule is broken; the test is named so that a review can start from it.

| To stand in for | Use | The test that holds it, and its break |
|---|---|---|
| The library stopping between two of its steps (a read and the write that follows, a file's content and its rename) | A point the library marks and a `Hold` ([A point the library stops at](#a-point-the-library-stops-at)). The reviewer may add a point to the finding's code in the throwaway tree: `cineris_base::hook::point("…")`, and `Hold::new("…")` in the test. | `cineris-base`'s `a_reader_sees_the_old_content_or_the_new_and_never_a_mixture`; `a-write-truncates-the-target-before-the-rename`. |
| **Two processes sharing a state folder** | Two cores of one `World`: `world.core()` twice, each with a front of its own. They share the world's configuration, state and data directories; each has its own recorder and its own sessions, as a process has. **A lock between them is the operating system's** (`flock` on Linux, `LockFileEx` on Windows, which keep two opens apart even in one process: `cineris_base::private::hold_lock`), so one core's writer excludes the other's. A lock that belongs to the process (POSIX `fcntl`) would not. And whatever is a process-wide static is shared by both cores, as it is not by two processes. | `cineris-core`'s `two_processes_together_hold_the_day_to_its_limit` (`tests/it/day.rs`): the first core's spending is read by the second, which is held to the day's limit by it; its breaks are in `mutations/step-16-fixes.breaks`. |
| **A disk that does not answer** | A FIFO with nobody at its other end, which stalls whoever opens it, in the kernel, with no timing: `cineris_testkit::stalled_file(path)` (Unix only) makes one, and `release()` or dropping it lets a *writer* that waits go on (it opens the other end for reading; a reader that waits is not let go, as two readers of a FIFO both wait for a writer). The code that opens it runs on a thread of its own, waited for with a bound, so that a mistake is a failed test and not a hung one. **On Windows nothing stands in:** an open of a file there fails or succeeds at once, and a test of what a stall does is `#[cfg(unix)]`. What stands in on both systems for a **writer that is stuck** (not a disk that is) is a lock another party holds, `cineris_base::private::hold_lock`. | The helper's own `stalled::tests::sys_a_stalled_file_is_a_fifo_and_holds_an_opener_until_it_is_released`; the break `a-stalled-file-is-an-ordinary-file`. Used in raw form (`mkfifo`) by `cineris-core`'s `a_stalled_disk_holds_a_days_request_at_most_a_second` and, for a file swapped for a FIFO, by `cineris-session`'s and `cineris-tools`' `sys_` tests; a stuck writer by a lock in `a_stuck_statistics_writer_holds_the_end_at_most_two_seconds`. |
| **A huge allocation that must not happen** | `setrlimit(RLIMIT_AS)` on the test's own process (Linux): an allocation over the limit fails, and Rust's allocator aborts, so that the test **fails every time** where on a machine with enough memory it would pass, or fail only by being killed. nextest gives each test a process of its own, so the limit holds for the test alone; under `cargo test` it would limit every test that runs after it in the process, so the test is Linux-only and written for nextest. It needs `unsafe`, which the workspace denies: the test file allows it with a reason (`#![allow(unsafe_code, reason = "a test that limits its own address space")]`), as the one that exists does. `libc` is a dependency of `cineris-base`, `cineris-sandbox` and `cineris-term`, so a test in one of them may use it; **the test kit has no `libc`**, and a helper there (`cineris_testkit::limit_memory(bytes)`) would add an edge and a change to the dependency's row ([dependencies.md](dependencies.md)), which this step did not make. On Windows a Job Object's process memory limit does the same, and no test uses it. | `cineris-base`'s `a_zone_file_that_lies_about_its_counts_is_refused_without_a_large_allocation` (`tests/it/zone.rs`); `the-zone-reader-allocates-before-it-checks` (`mutations/step-16-fixes.breaks`). |
| **How much of the heap a structure holds** | A counting global allocator in the crate's test binary (`#[global_allocator]`, the system's allocator with a counter of the bytes given out and not had back), read before and after the work. nextest gives each test a process of its own, so the count is the test's. It needs `unsafe`, allowed on the allocator with a reason, as above. What a structure holds whatever its size (a cache near a window) is taken out by measuring two lengths and keeping the difference. Every test of the binary goes through the counter: an atomic add on each allocation. | `cineris-tui`'s `memory::a_finished_reasoning_is_held_once_in_each_presenter` and `memory::a_long_reasoning_is_held_to_its_bound_in_each_presenter` (`tests/it/memory.rs`); `reasoning-copied-into-its-summary` and `reasoning-kept-without-a-bound` (`mutations/step-25-fixes.breaks`). |

**What a reviewer hands over, for a finding of this kind:**

1. The test, as a file that `scratch-test` runs: `cargo xtask scratch-test cineris-base review_atomic.rs`. It uses `crate::…` for the crate's own test helpers, and the library's `test-hooks` points (the test kit's `Hold`, or `cineris_base::hook::Hold` in `cineris-base`) where it needs the library to stop.
2. The command that runs it, and what it prints when the finding is true (the test's failure message, with the rule it says is broken).
3. The break that shows the rule untested, in a list of its own for `mutate --file`, with the test it must be caught by in `caught-by`; or the one-break form. The break names the code that does the rule's work: made, the test must fail (a break nothing fails is the finding). Before the fix the test fails without it.
4. If a point was needed, the line that marks it, in the finding's own change, so that the author keeps it.

**What the kit does not do.** A race in a library that is not behind a point still needs a point; one made by adding it to the code under review, in the throwaway tree, is what the first row is for. Two cores in one process share what is global to it. A FIFO has nothing to say about a disk that answers slowly. An address-space limit finds an allocation that a check should have prevented, not one that a machine with little memory would refuse for other reasons.

### An adversarial confined command

The sandbox reviews each built their own adversary. The core's tests have one now (`crates/cineris-core/tests/it/adversary.rs`, Linux only, since the fourth tooling step):

- **`Reach::run(test, script)`** runs one turn at `autonomous`, in a trusted repository, with the sandbox enforcing, whose one call is `sh adversary.sh`: the script is a file in the workspace, so the command analysis cannot see what it aims at and ask about it. It names its targets with `{outside}` (a folder outside the write roots), `{port}` (a listener on the loopback interface) and `{marker}`. Every question the turn asks is refused, and listed.
- **What it reports** (`Reached`): the protected paths whose content changed (under the workspace's `.git`, `.cineris` and `.cargo`); the files outside the write roots made, changed or removed (anything under the world's folder but the workspace and the harness's own configuration, state and data); the connections that reached the listener; and the processes still alive after the turn whose environment holds the marker, which the shell passes to all it starts, killed once counted.
- **A test with more turns** wraps them in `Reach::start(&world)` and `reach.reached(&world)`, and gives the shell `reach.env()`. The stopped-turn sandbox test does.
- **The shell's grace for a process left running is on the world's fake timer,** which moves only when a test moves it: `run` lets each wait shorter than a minute go while the turn runs, asking again at once each time, so it keeps a core busy while the command runs (milliseconds). The call's time limit is minutes, and is never reached.
- **What it does not see:** a process that clears its environment (`env -i setsid sleep 600 &`) loses the marker, and is neither reported nor killed by the fixture; the kill's own sweep is what holds it.

### Breaking the code on purpose

A done criterion of the form "X is tested" is evidence only if the test fails when X is broken. Every step since step 5 has broken its own rules on purpose, one at a time, and listed which tests failed. The breaks are now data in the repository, and one command runs them.

**The lists** are `mutations/<name>.breaks`, one file for each step or crate, so that two steps adding breaks at the same time do not touch the same file. A break is a small change that breaks one rule, with the tests that must fail when it is made:

```text
[break] cancellation-not-seen-in-wake
rule: Cancellation is an input: a wait for the response or the round is ended by the turn being cancelled.
file: crates/cineris-agent/src/turn.rs
in: wake
find: cancelled.as_mut().poll(context).is_ready()
replace: cancelled.as_mut().poll(context).is_ready() && false
caught-by:
    transitions::an_interruption_is_taken_in_every_state_that_waits
```

That one is from `mutations/step-07.breaks`.

| Key | What it is |
|---|---|
| `[break] <name>` | Begins a break. Lower-case letters, digits and `-`. It is shown, and chosen, as `<file>/<name>`: `step-12/deny-beats-allow`. |
| `rule` | The rule the break breaks, in a sentence. Required. |
| `file` | The file to change, relative to the repository. Required. |
| `in` | The item the code is in: names separated by `::`, such as `Round::spawn`. An `impl` block answers to every name in its header. Optional. |
| `find` | The code to replace. Required (or `in-string`). It must be there exactly once, in the item if one is named. |
| `in-string` | Instead of `find` (since the eighth tooling step): a text to change **inside a string literal**, with `replace` the text to put in its place ("A word of a string constant", below). |
| `replace` | What to put there. Required, and may be empty. |
| `occurrence` | Which match of `find` is meant, counted from 1, when there are several. Optional. |
| `packages` | The crates whose tests to run, separated by commas. Without it, the crate the file is in. |
| `caught-by` | The tests that must fail, one a line: a test's name as nextest prints it, or its binary and its name (`cineris-agent::it denials::…`). A `*` at the end stands for any ending. Without it, any failing test catches the break. |
| `expect: survives` | Marks a break that changes nothing a test can see, kept so that nobody tries it again. Its `rule` says why. |
| `os` | `windows` or `linux` (since the seventh tooling step): the break is of code only that system builds, and only that system runs it. `windows` is what `cfg!(windows)` decides and `linux` is every `cfg!(unix)` system; anything else is an error. On the other system the break is applied in memory by the pre-flight, as on every system, and then left alone ("Breaks of code one system builds", below). Optional: without it the break runs everywhere. |

A value is on the key's line, or on the indented lines after a key that ends its line. A line that begins with `#` is a comment. Anything else is an error with its line: an unknown key, a key given twice, a break without a required key.

**`find` is matched by token,** not as text. White space, comments and a comma before a closing bracket are not tokens, so however `rustfmt` lays the code out, the break still applies. An exact-text break stops applying the day a line is wrapped, and that is how breaks were lost in earlier steps.

**A word of a string constant: `in-string`** (since the eighth tooling step). A string literal is one token, so a `find` that changes one word of a message, a path or a name kept in a constant had to repeat the whole literal, and was one more line to keep in step with the code. `in-string` names the word alone:

An illustration:

```text
[break] overload-message-says-something-else
rule: The message for an overloaded provider tells the user to retry.
file: crates/cineris-llm/src/error.rs
in: Overload::message
in-string: retry later
replace: give up
caught-by:
    error::tests::an_overload_tells_the_user_to_retry
```

- **Where it is looked for:** only inside the string literals of the file, or of the item `in` names (ordinary, byte, C and raw strings, with any number of `#`). Code, comments and character literals are never searched, so `Budget` the type and `"Budget"` the word are not mixed up. The text is matched **as the literal is written in the source**: an escape is typed as it is (`\n`, `\"`), a raw string is what is between its quotes, and the quotes are not part of the text (a text that begins with `"` finds nothing). It may begin or end in the middle of a word, and it cannot run from one literal into the next.
- **It must be there exactly once**, like a `find`: a text that is in two literals, or twice in one, is refused with the lines, and `occurrence` (counted from 1, across the literals in source order) or a longer text or an `in` picks the one meant. `occurrence` and `in` work as they do for `find`.
- **`replace` is text, not code.** It goes over the text found and nothing else of the literal changes, so the literal must still be one string after it: a `"` that no backslash escapes, or a `\` at the end that escapes the closing quote, is refused ("makes a string literal that is not one string"). A quote is written `\"`.
- **One-line values are trimmed,** so an `in-string` or a `replace` cannot begin or end in a space: put the neighbouring characters in the text (`in-string: retry later` and `replace: give up`, not `later` and a space). A value on indented lines keeps its inner layout and loses the white space at the ends of its lines.
- **The pre-flight holds it as it holds a `find`:** the text must be found once; a `replace` that is the text is a break that changes nothing; and two breaks that make the same change are refused, **whichever key made it** (an `in-string` of one word and a `find` of the whole literal with that word changed are one break: the comparison is of the string token after the change). The checks that are about code do not apply: a comma at the end of an `in-string` is text, not a comma that `rustfmt` moves. `--check --fix-in` finds the item that holds an `in-string` text after the code moved, as it does for a `find`. `--merge` and the lists' other tools see a break whose `in-string` is its text, kept as written.
- **What a result is kept for** includes the key: an `in-string` and a `find` of the same words are two breaks.
- **For one break with no list:** `mutate --break FILE --in-string TEXT --replace TEXT`, in the place of `--find` (the two do not go together).

**A break that does not apply is an error, never a result.** `cargo xtask check` has a step, `breaks`, that applies every break of every list to the tree in memory and fails when one cannot be made: its code is gone, or is there twice, or its replacement is the same code. It reads the files the breaks name and runs nothing (0.2 s on the reference machine for the 617 breaks there are, the pre-flight below included, which is what it took without it), so it is in the blocking checks: a change that moves code out from under a break updates the break in the same pull request, or deletes it if the rule is gone. The message names the list and the line, and says which.

**The pre-flight** (since the third tooling step) is the same step, and `cargo xtask mutate --check` runs it alone. Besides a break that cannot be made, it refuses one that will go wrong later, or that tests nothing another does not:

| It refuses | Why |
|---|---|
| A `replace` that is its `find` once both are read by token | The break changes nothing, and would be reported as one no test caught. |
| A `find` that is not there, or is there more than once | The break is not made, or is made in a place nobody chose. |
| A `find` that ends in a comma | `rustfmt` adds and removes the comma before a closing bracket, and the search passes over such a comma, so the day the line is wrapped or joined the break no longer applies. The comma after a match arm that is an expression (`503 => Signal::Overload,`) is the formatter's always, and passes. A `find` whose last comma comes before a closing bracket in the file cannot match at all (that comma is not a token there, and the `find`'s is), and the pre-flight says so, with the line, and to end `find` before the comma (since the fifth tooling step); `cargo xtask replace` says the same of a `--from`. |
| An `in` that names more than one item | Two `impl` blocks with a `new`: the break is made in whichever holds its code today. Not when each of them is under a `#[cfg(…)]` about the system (`unix`, `windows`, `target_os`, `target_family`, `target_arch`): a function for each system, of which a build has one. A `cfg` of a test or a feature does not count: whether two such items can be built together depends on what the `cfg`s say, and the pre-flight does not read them, so it refuses them and a more precise `in` (or none) is needed. |
| Two breaks, in any lists, that make the same change | Read by token, from the first token they change to the last: `a && b` made `a` is `&& b` made nothing. The second tests nothing more. |
| A `caught-by` that names no test (since the sixth tooling step) | A renamed or removed test would be found only when the break is run, after a build. Here it is found from a list of the tests that exist, which has to be built once and is then kept ("The tests a `caught-by` names", below). |

What it cannot see is **a break that a second guard in the code has made unable to fail.** Step 13a's fixes closed the Windows credentials file by name and then its whole folder: after that, the break that removed only the first could fail no test, because the second still refused the read. Only running the breaks shows it, and `mutate` then says NOT CAUGHT, and for a break that names its tests, that another guard may now cover it: break both in one break, or mark it `expect: survives` and name the other guard in its `rule`.

When the pre-flight was written it found 43 breaks whose `find` ended in a comma, in the lists of steps 9, 12, 13a, 20, 26 and 31 and both earlier tooling steps. 38 of them had a `replace` that ended in one too, and lost both; the five others were match arms, which is how the rule came to pass them. It found two `in`s of its own step's that named a trait and its `impl` in one file, and no two breaks that made the same change.

**The tests a `caught-by` names** (since the sixth tooling step). A name that no test has is a test that was renamed or removed, and `mutate` found it only when it ran the break (NAMES A TEST THAT DOES NOT EXIST, below), after a build. `mutate --check` finds it before, with the break's list and line and the name, and when the test only moved it names the tests of the same last name. To know which tests exist it needs the tests built, which `cargo xtask check` must not do (`lint-linux` builds no test binary, only clippy), so the list of tests is **kept, and read when it is fresh**:

| Command | What it does with the names |
|---|---|
| `cargo xtask mutate --check --tests [BREAK…]` | Builds and lists the tests (`cargo nextest list`, once for each set of crates that the breaks run, as `mutate` judges a break against the tests of exactly its build), keeps each list in `target/mutate/test-lists.json`, and checks every `caught-by` against them. With names, only those breaks' names are checked (the rest of the check is always the whole list). |
| `cargo xtask mutate --check` and the `breaks` step of `cargo xtask check` | Check against the lists that are kept **and fresh**, and build nothing. A set of crates with no list, or a stale one, is left unchecked, and the output says so and says how to check it. With no file at all the tree's files are not even read: the step costs what it did. |

- **A list is fresh** when the files of its crates are what they were when it was listed: the name and content of every file under `crates/<name>/` of each crate in the set (`xtask/` for `xtask`), the workspace's `Cargo.toml`, `Cargo.lock` and `.config/nextest.toml`, as one 64-bit FNV-1a hash (the one the results of `mutate` are kept by). A change to another crate leaves it fresh: a test's name is in its own crate's files. A test added or renamed in the crate makes it stale, so the check never calls a new test missing: it skips it until `--tests` lists again.
- **Why the list is kept, and not built by the check, and not read from the sources.** Building is minutes the first time and the blocking check must stay as cheap as today (rule 2). Reading the names from the sources would be a guess: `proptest!` and the test kit's macros make tests no `#[test]` names, and a module path comes from `mod` lines across files, so a wrong guess would fail the blocking check on a test that exists. A list from nextest is the names a run has. The price is that the blocking check finds a wrong name only where a fresh list is kept: in a checkout where `--tests` has been run, which is the command to run after renaming or removing a test that a break names. `lint-linux` has no list, so there it prints one line saying the names were not checked. **The nightly workflow's `break-names` job runs `mutate --check --tests`** (since the tenth tooling step), so a name that a renamed or removed test left behind is found within a day, and its failure opens the nightly's issue. A blocking job that has built the tests anyway (the test jobs) could run it at the cost of the listing, for every set of crates the breaks run (42 sets today, most of them a rebuild with other features): that would add minutes to a blocking job, and is the owner's to decide ([caught-by-names-in-a-blocking-job](outstanding/caught-by-names-in-a-blocking-job.md)).
- **An `#[ignore]`d test cannot catch a break:** a run of `mutate` never runs one. A `caught-by` that names only an ignored test is refused, and says so.
- **What the list is of:** the build of exactly the crates the break runs, as `mutate` builds them. `-p cineris-sandbox` alone has no confinement tests (see "What it cannot tell"), so a break there names `cineris-core` in `packages` and is listed with it.
- **Measured** (the reference machine, a debug build of `xtask` as `cargo xtask` runs it; this tree's 1,024 breaks, whose `caught-by` names are of 32 sets of crates and 780 KB of lists): `mutate --check` with no list kept 0.39 s, as before the step (the second machine, before and after the step on the same 1,010 breaks: 0.852 s and 0.852 s); with every list kept and fresh 0.56 s, the 0.17 s being the reading of each crate's files once and of the lists, and the matching of the names. `mutate --check --tests`: the first time, on the reference machine with nothing built, 249 s, nearly all of it the build of the workspace's tests, which `cargo xtask test` then finds built; with the tests built and one crate changed, 17 s for all 32 sets, 6 s of it the build of `xtask`'s own tests, and a few seconds when only a few sets are stale.
- **Measured again with the seventh tooling step's `os:`, `--break`, `--flagged` and `--merge`** (the second machine under other builds, a debug build of `xtask`, `mutate --check` with no list of tests kept, the fastest of 8 runs each): 0.77 s before the step on its 1,277 breaks and 0.77 s after on 1,307. The pre-flight reads a break's `os` as it reads any key and does not look at it again.
- **The names of a break of the other system's code** (since the eighth tooling step).
