A list of tests is of what the build here builds, so a test that only Windows builds is in no list made on Linux, and the names of a break marked `os: windows` were checked nowhere until it ran on Windows (found by the seventh tooling step's review). The check now looks for each of them, on any system and with no list, **among the words of the source**: the name's last segment (or, for an entry that ends in `*`, its beginning) must be a name token of some Rust file of the crates the break runs (comments and strings do not count). A test that was renamed or removed is found by `mutate --check` and the `breaks` step of `check`, as a name that no test has is, with the break's list and line. A list of the tests of a Windows build, kept and read on Linux, would be exact, and is **not** what was done, for three reasons: such a list is produced only by a build on Windows (`try -f os=windows`, a runner), so it would be a file to commit, to bring back from an artifact, and to make again after every change to a crate that a Windows break runs (the check's own freshness rule: any change to the crate makes it stale); a list that is stale checks nothing, so it would check the names on the days nobody changed the crate; and it adds a generated file and a workflow step outside what `xtask/src/mutate` owns. **What the words cannot tell:** a test from a helper of the same name, a wrong module path, a test that is `#[ignore]`d, and a name a macro makes from pieces (it is no word of any file: name it by the beginning of a word that is there, with a `*`, or by the macro's argument). An entry that ends in `::*` is not looked for at all. The run on Windows still judges them exactly.
- **What it found when it was written:** four names of the lists of steps 13a's and 15's fixes that no test has (two of them a test renamed with `sys_` in front, one a name reworded at its end, one a test of another crate than the break runs). They are left for their steps to correct.

**`mutate --check --fix-in`** (since the sixth tooling step) is for the day a refactor renames an item or moves code into another: the break's `in:` then matches nothing, and the check says its `find` is not in the item, and nothing of where it went. With `--fix-in`, for each break whose `in:` no longer holds its `find`, it looks for the code in the whole file, and prints the `in:` that names the item that holds it now, with the code's line:

```text
mutations/step-07.breaks:41: break `step-07/cancellation-not-seen-in-wake`: its `in: wake` no longer holds its `find` in `crates/cineris-agent/src/turn.rs`.
  The code is now at line 212 of `crates/cineris-agent/src/turn.rs`. Use:
      in: Round::poll
```

- **It offers only what it is sure of.** The `find` must be in the file exactly once, and in an item (a module, an `impl` block, a function, as `in:` names them). The name is the innermost item's, with the one around it added until the name picks out that one item and that place (an `in:` that names two items is refused by the pre-flight, and `new` in two `impl` blocks is `A::new`). A `find` that is in several places (it names each one, with its line and its item), nowhere in the file (the code is gone or has moved to another file: change `file:` by hand), in no item, or that asks for an `occurrence` above 1, is **not guessed at**: the message says why, and the break is left to a person.
- **`--fix-in --write`** rewrites the `in:` lines in the lists, only those: other lines, comments and line endings stay, and a value written on indented lines becomes one on the key's line. It then checks the lists again. Without `--write` nothing is changed and the check fails as before.
- A break whose `in:` still holds its code, or that has none, or that fails for another reason (its `find` twice in the item), is not touched. Run `cargo xtask mutate BREAK` after, to see that the tests still catch it: the code is where it was meant, not necessarily doing what the rule says.

**`cargo xtask mutate [BREAK…]`** runs the breaks: all of them, or the ones named (a break, a whole list, or the end of a name). **`--file PATH`** (since the sixth tooling step) runs the breaks of a list kept anywhere, and no others: a review's throwaway breaks need no file in `mutations/`, where one forgotten fails `check`. The list is named for its file, and a name that is already a list's of `mutations/` is refused.

1. Every break is applied in memory, as `check` does. Nothing is built if one fails.
2. **The work is done on a copy** of the tree under `target/mutate/`, with a build directory of its own. The working tree is only read. The copy is brought up to date at the start of each run, which also undoes a break that a killed run left in it. The first build of the copy is a whole build; after that each break rebuilds one crate.
3. **The tests are run once with nothing broken,** for each set of packages that a break runs. A test that fails by itself stops the run: it is no evidence about anything. A known flake that fails, with nothing else failing, is run again once, and the run goes on when it passes ("Known flakes", above; since the tenth tooling step). A break is judged against the tests of exactly the build it runs in, because which tests there are depends on what is built together.
4. **Each break is compiled** (`cargo check`) before any break's tests are run, and the ones that do not compile are named.
5. **Each break's tests are run,** and the tests that failed are read from nextest's JUnit report (profile `mutate`), not from its terminal output.

What it says of each break:

| Verdict | Meaning | What to do |
|---|---|---|
| caught by N test(s) | At least one test failed, and every test of `caught-by` did. | Nothing. |
| survived, as its list expects | A break marked `expect: survives`, and no test failed. | Nothing. |
| NOT CAUGHT | No test failed. | Write the test. If nothing a test can see changes, mark the break `expect: survives`. |
| NOT CAUGHT BY ITS OWN TESTS | Some test failed, and one that `caught-by` names passed. | A test that used to catch this no longer does: find out why. |
| NAMES A TEST THAT DOES NOT EXIST | A `caught-by` entry matches no test. | The test was renamed or removed: correct the list. `mutate --check --tests` finds this without running the breaks. |
| CAUGHT, AND EXPECTED TO SURVIVE | A break marked `expect: survives` made a test fail. | Take the mark off and name the test. |
| DOES NOT COMPILE | The code does not build with the break made. | Correct `replace`. |
| COULD NOT BE JUDGED | The run failed and named no failed test. | Read the log it names. |
| left to a Windows run (or a Linux one) | The break has an `os:` of the other system. It was applied by the pre-flight and not run here. | Nothing here: it is neither a pass nor a failure of the run. Run it where its code is built ("Breaks of code one system builds"). |

It exits 0 when every break did what its list expects, 1 when one did not, and 2 when its time ran out.

**It stops, and carries on.** Every result is written to `target/mutate/results.jsonl` as it is known, under a key made of the code (every file but the lists) and the break. A run does not start new work after its time budget (`--minutes N`, 5 when not given, 0 for none), and the next run starts where it stopped: run the same command until it exits 0 or 1.

- **The budget is on starting work, not on finishing it.** One piece of work, a break's build and tests, is seconds. The first build of the copy is a whole build of the crates named, and runs to its end whatever the budget. After it, a long list gives a shared build machine back every five minutes or so.
- A run that is killed loses at most the break it was in.
- **One run at a time on a copy.** A second run on the same copy is refused while the first holds it: it would bring the copy up to date under the first, which would then report a break as survived that was no longer made. The lock is the operating system's, so a killed run lets go of it. A copy that cannot be locked at all is not used.
- **A lock that is held is tried again for 200 ms** (20 times, 10 ms apart) before the copy is called busy (since the seventh tooling step). Under plain `cargo test`, where `xtask`'s tests are threads of one process, a test that runs `mutate` twice on one copy found the lock still held by the first run: a process another thread had forked held the lock's descriptor from its fork to its `exec`, after the first run had let go of it. The descriptor is not inherited by what runs (the standard library opens every file close-on-exec, and a Windows handle is not inheritable), so the moment is the fork's alone and the retry ends it; a run that really holds the copy is still refused, 200 ms later. The wait is a parameter, so that no test sleeps: the tests of the retry count the waits instead.
- A change to the code forgets every result. A change to a list forgets only the breaks it changed.
- **A run that ended without a verdict is tried again by the next run:** a break that did not compile, or a test run that failed and named no failed test. The cause may have been the machine's. A break that did not compile is reported with the compiler's first errors, never cargo's progress lines: cargo runs with `CARGO_TERM_COLOR=never` whatever the caller set, since an `error` in escape sequences is not found (CI sets `always`, which is how the nightly run of 2026-11-14 failed, issue #40). A test run that named no failed test keeps its whole output in a file of its own under `target/mutate/logs/`, which the message names.
- `--again` forgets the results of the breaks named, and runs them again. To carry on after the time ran out, leave it off: with it, the next run would forget what this one did. The command says so.
- `--report` prints what is known and runs nothing.

**`--jobs N` runs N breaks at a time,** two when it is not given (since the sixth tooling step: four jobs at once stalled the reference machine's disk in wave 9, and baselines were lost). Each job has a copy of the tree (`target/mutate/tree`, `tree-2`, …) and a build directory of its own (`target`, `target-2`, …), and takes the next piece of work when it is free. The three rounds stay in order, each finished before the next begins: the tests with nothing broken, every break compiled, every break's tests. The run's own thread alone writes the results and the report, in the order pieces finish. With one job nothing changes: the pieces are done in order, on the run's own thread.

- **Each job's tests have a scratch base of their own:** `CINERIS_TEST_TMPDIR=<base>/job-<n>`. Under the caller's `CINERIS_TEST_TMPDIR`, `<base>` is a folder named for the command, `cineris-mutate-<hash>` (a hash of the folder of the copies, which the run's lock is on), so that nothing of the caller's is taken for a job's folder and two commands from two checkouts given one value never meet. It is removed once all the command's jobs are done, never while one runs: on Windows a folder removed while something holds it open stays until the last handle goes, and a job then could not make its own folder in it (`Access is denied`; 13 of 100 repeats of the two-job test on a hosted runner). A relative value is made absolute from the command's directory, and an empty one counts as unset. Without it, `<base>` is the job's build directory's `tmp` (Cargo's `CARGO_TARGET_TMPDIR`, where the tests would put their folders). The folder is emptied and made before each run of the tests. `mutate` removes it after a break's run, with whatever a test that failed left in it. A run with nothing broken that fails keeps it, moved aside to `job-<n>.kept` (`job-<n>.kept-2` for a second) where the job's next run does not touch it, and the message says where; the next command removes it once it holds the copies' lock. A folder that cannot be emptied, moved or removed is noted in `target/mutate/logs/scratch-folders.log`, which nothing writes over. A test's scratch folder is `<base>/<crate>-it/<test>`, so with one `CINERIS_TEST_TMPDIR` for the whole command, two jobs running one crate's tests at once gave each test one folder, and each copy of the test removed and filled the other's: a slow test failed by the other job's doing, and was counted as catching a break it could not see. Measured on the reference machine with `CINERIS_TEST_TMPDIR` set for the command: two jobs on the 67 breaks of `cineris-knowledge` in `mutations/step-17.breaks` had 47 such failures, in 12 of the breaks, and none with a base for each job (one break read "caught by 8 test(s)", and with a base for each job is caught by its own test alone).
- **A busy disk starts a run with one job.** When every task has waited on the disk 10% or more of the last minute (`full avg60` in `/proc/pressure/io`, Linux only), a run asked for several jobs starts with one and says so: a review's two jobs on a busy machine lost a baseline to a stall at 66%, where one job ran clean.
- **A stall is not a verdict.** A test run whose timeouts ended together ("Running the tests many times over", above) comes back as the machine's. A set of tests with nothing broken that stalled is run again, once the round's other pieces are done, with one job, and the run says so; if it stalls with one job too, the run stops and says the machine is too busy. A break's run that stalled is reported as COULD NOT BE JUDGED with the stall's message, and the next run tries it again: a test that failed because the disk stopped would otherwise make the break look caught.

- **The first build of each new copy is a whole build** of the crates named. On the reference machine that was 57 s for four copies of `cineris-core` together, the first time.
- **Measured on the reference machine**, on the 24 breaks of `mutations/step-13a.breaks` in `cineris-core`'s `assemble.rs`, once every copy was built: 43.5 s with one job, 15.8 s with four, which also ran the tests with nothing broken again. 2.8 times as fast.
- **`--minutes` is the budget of all the jobs together:** no new piece starts after it, so several jobs hold a machine for no longer than one job with the same budget would, and do more work in it. What `--jobs` adds is cores.
- **The time is asked before any job starts,** then as each piece finishes and a few times a second. **The answer "up" is binding** (since the eighth tooling step): a job takes its next piece under a lock that the run's thread holds while it asks the time and writes the answer down, so a job either took its piece before the question, or finds the answer. The seventh tooling step's stress found `no_job_starts_a_piece_once_the_time_is_up` failing once in 50 repeats: the test lets the pieces go from inside the question, a job that was let go looked for its next piece before the answer was written, and with the gate open it went through every piece. The test is the same with a few milliseconds' window given to a job to try (a bound on the clock of a few milliseconds, which a passing run spends in full, since with the lock no job can take a piece in it, and a failing one leaves early; whether a missing lock is caught depends on a thread that is let go being scheduled within it, which a loaded Windows runner may not do), and 200 repeats, alone and under load (`--cores 2 --load 6`), pass.

**Only the breaks a diff can affect: `mutate --changed REF`** (since the eighth tooling step). A full run of a big step's breaks is half an hour, and again after each fix of a review. `--changed REF` runs the breaks the working tree's diff from `REF` (a branch, a tag or a commit) can affect, and says how many it left and why:

```text
--changed origin/main: the diff touches 9 file(s). 44 of 1663 break(s) chosen (3 new or changed in their lists, 38 by their file, 1 by a test they name, 2 whose test names could not be placed in any file, chosen to be safe); 1619 skipped: the diff touches neither their file, nor a test they name, nor their text, nor the build's settings. A full run (no `--changed`) is what a report rests on.
```

A full run stays the default, and is what a step's report rests on; `--changed` is for the loop of fix and run (0.5 s to choose, on 1,663 breaks).

**The diff** is the working tree against `REF`: `git diff --name-only --no-renames REF` (a file that was moved or deleted counts under both names) and the files git does not know yet (`git ls-files --others --exclude-standard`). **A break is chosen when the diff**

1. touches the build's settings: the workspace's `Cargo.toml`, `Cargo.lock`, `.config/nextest.toml`, `rust-toolchain.toml` or `.cargo/config.toml` (every break's build reads them: all are chosen), **or any file outside `crates/`, `xtask/`, `docs/`, `mutations/` and `.github/` that is not Markdown and that no crate's Rust names** (what the rule does not know is taken to touch every break; found by the step's review; since the twelfth tooling step a file a crate names is read by rule 5), or the `Cargo.toml` or `build.rs` of a crate the break runs (`packages`, or its file's crate) or of a crate it is built on (since the twelfth tooling step); or
2. **adds or changes the break itself:** no break of the lists at `REF` has the text that decides what it does (its file, item, `find` or `in-string`, `replace`, `occurrence`, crates, `caught-by`, `expect` and `os`: a reworded `rule` is not a change, and a break moved to another list, by `--merge` or otherwise, is the break it was). The lists the diff does not touch are the same as at `REF`; those it touches are read as they were (`git show REF:mutations/NAME.breaks`). A break read from `--file` is judged the same way: one that no list of `mutations/` at `REF` has is new; or
3. touches its `file`; or
4. touches a test its `caught-by` names; or
5. **touches a file that is not Rust that a crate's Rust names** (since the twelfth tooling step: a data file such as `catalog/models.toml`, a fixture, a page a test reads), and the break runs that crate, or a crate built on it or whose tests use it. "Data files", below, has how a reader is found.

**It never skips a break that rule says to choose.** What it chooses when it cannot tell is the part that takes care:

- **Which test a name is.** A test's module path comes from `mod` lines across files, macros make tests no `#[test]` names, and a `*` ends a name, so the name is not mapped to a file by its path. A `caught-by` is read by its **last segment** (`no_job_starts_a_piece_once_the_time_is_up` of `mutate::jobs::tests::…`; the binary of an entry, `cineris-llm::it`, is dropped). A test is touched when a file of the crates the break runs that the diff touches has that word among its tokens **anywhere**: in a definition, as a macro's argument, in a call. The file is read as it is, and as it was at `REF`, so a test that was removed or moved is found by where it was.
- **What that over-chooses:** a helper or a test of the same name in another module, and a call of a function of that name in a file the diff touches.
- **When no file of those crates has the word at all** (a test that a macro made from pieces, a name that is gone), **or the entry ends in `*`** (a pattern can end inside a module's name: `open*` is every test of `openai_chat`, and no word of a test's own says so; found by the step's review), the name cannot be placed, and the break is chosen when **any file of those crates** was touched. **A touched file of those crates that is not Rust** (a fixture, a snapshot, a file a test reads) chooses it too. The summary counts these ("chosen to be safe"), and `--list` says for each break why it was chosen.
- **Only the crates a break runs are read:** a crate with no touched file is not read at all, so a diff that leaves a crate alone costs the choice nothing there.

**What it does not see**, and a full run is for: a change to Rust code a test calls that is not the break's file and names no test the break names (a helper elsewhere in the crate, the test kit, a crate it depends on), and a break that names no test (`expect: survives`, or any failing test catches it): that one is chosen by its file, its own text and the settings only.

**It combines** with the break and list names given (it narrows them), `--file` (the lists' breaks, narrowed), `--flagged` (the breaks of the narrowed set that were left without a verdict), `--again` (forgets the results of the narrowed set only), `--list` (which breaks, each with why), `--report`, `--jobs`, `--minutes` and `--budget`. **It is refused** beside `--break` (one break of its own: nothing to choose among), `--merge`, and `--check` (a check reads every list whole, since a break that does not apply is wrong whether or not it is run today). A `REF` that is not a commit is an error that names it, never "nothing changed", and a `REF` that begins with `-` is refused as an option. A diff that touches nothing any break depends on says "Nothing to run" and exits 0.

**Data files** (since the twelfth tooling step). A file that is not Rust is read by the crates whose Rust names it in a string literal: an `include_str!` or `include_bytes!` path (from the source file's folder), a build script's `cargo::rerun-if-changed=PATH` or a path joined to `CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR` (from the crate's folder), or a path from the top of the repository (`"docs/outstanding"`). Each names the file, or a folder it is in; from the top only by two names or more (`"AGENTS.md"` alone names a project's own file as often as this repository's), and a literal that only climbs (`..`, `a/../..`) names nothing. A format string's literal is read by its text after its last hole, from where the hole leaves it (`"{}/../../docs/a/4.txt"` with the crate's folder in the hole). A literal of two names or more that ends the file's path (`"catalog/models.toml"`) names it from anywhere; a file's name alone (`"request.json"`) does not, since many crates name files of that name. A reader in its crate's tests alone (a file under the crate's `tests/`, `benches/` or `examples/`, or inside a `#[cfg(test)] mod … { }`) is seen by that crate's breaks only; any other is seen by the breaks of every crate built on it or whose tests use it, as `test --dependents` counts them. A `.rs` file a crate holds and does not build (under its folder, outside `src/`, `tests/`, `benches/` and `examples/`, and not its `build.rs`, such as `cineris-index`'s `fixtures/outlines/sample.rs` and `xtask`'s `fixtures/rules/*.rs`) is a data file too, read the same way. The rule reads every crate's Rust, as it is and, for a file the diff touches, as it was at `REF`: a reader the diff removed still counts. The summary names each data file's readers and the crates that can see it:

```text
--changed HEAD: the diff touches 1 file(s). 3161 of 3208 break(s) chosen (3161 by a data file a crate they run reads); 47 skipped: …
  `catalog/models.toml` is named by the code of cineris-llm, cineris-stats (and so every crate built on it): the breaks that run one of these 17 crate(s) can see it: cineris-agent, cineris-cli, …
```

The catalog is compiled into `cineris-llm` by its build script, and most crates are built on `cineris-llm` or test with `cineris-testkit`, which is: so most breaks can see it, and are chosen. A fixture of `cineris-llm` chooses the breaks of the crates that name its folder (`cineris-llm`'s and `cineris-cli`'s tests, and `xtask`'s `regen`), not every break.

**What the data-file rule cannot see:** a path made at run time from pieces that do not name the file by two names (`root.join("corpus").join("PIN")`), or read from the environment or a setting, and a file that a program the tests start reads by a path of its own. Such a file outside the folders read one by one, named by no crate, still chooses every break, with the file and why in the summary; one inside them (`docs/`, `.github/`, a crate's own folder) is left to the other rules, as before (a crate's own files choose its breaks).

**The most relevant breaks first, within a time: `mutate --budget TIME`** (since the twelfth tooling step). `TIME` is a number and its unit, `s`, `m` or `h` (`90s`, `30m`, `1h30m`). The chosen breaks run in four classes, each in the lists' order:

1. **touched:** the diff touches the break's `file`, or the break is new or changed in its list;
2. **by a test:** a test its `caught-by` names was touched;
3. **by its crate:** a file of a crate it runs was touched (a test name that could not be placed, a file that is not Rust, the crate's manifest or build script, or a crate it is built on), or a data file such a crate reads;
4. **the rest:** chosen for another reason (the build's settings, a file no crate names), and every break when there is no `--changed`.

Each class is a run of its own: the tests with nothing broken for the crates its breaks run, then each break. No new break is started once `TIME` is spent; those under way finish and are kept, so the same command run again carries on. The compile of every break before the first is left out (it would spend the budget on breaks never reached): a break that does not compile is found when it runs. At the end it says how many of each class it reached, names each break it left, and gives the commands that run them:

```text
$ cargo xtask mutate --changed 6806291f --budget 4m --jobs 2
--budget 4m: 1862 break(s), run in this order: 80 touched (its file, or the break itself), then 12 by a test it names, then 1770 by its crate (a file of it, or a data file it reads).
…
== 80 break(s) touched (its file, or the break itself) ==
…
== 12 break(s) by a test it names ==
…
The budget was spent: 92 of 1836 break(s) reached.
  touched (its file, or the break itself): 80 of 80 reached
  by a test it names: 12 of 12 reached
  by its crate (a file of it, or a data file it reads): 0 of 1744 reached
Left (1744):
  flaky-test-fixes/the-threads-do-not-wake-the-run  (by its crate (a file of it, or a data file it reads))
  …
Run the same command again to carry on from here (what was found is kept):
  cargo xtask mutate --changed 6806291f --budget 4m --jobs 2
```

That is this change's own first run on the reference machine: its diff touched `xtask`'s `mutate`, `AGENTS.md` and this page, which `xtask`'s code names, so the third class is every break of `xtask` and of the crates that named those pages (then also the crates that name a project's `AGENTS.md`, which one name from the top no longer counts for). The 26 breaks of code only Windows builds were left to a run there, as in any run. Past 40 breaks left, the command that would name each is not printed: the same command carries on.

`--budget` is the run's time limit, so it is refused beside `--minutes N` (`--minutes 0`, no limit of its own, which `try` gives a `mutate` command, may stand beside it), and beside `--check` and `--merge`, which run no break. `--list --budget TIME` prints the order, each break with its class, and runs nothing. A run that stops for the time exits 2, as `--minutes` does.

**It needs git's history.** In a tree with no `.git` (a copy of the sources made without one) `--changed` fails and says so. Name a ref the checkout has (`main`, brought up to date with `git fetch` and a fast-forward first); a ref it lacks is an error, never "nothing changed". **A diff it cannot read is exit status 3** (since the ninth tooling step), apart from 1, a break that did not do what its list expects: a tree with no `.git`, a `.git` git cannot read (a linked working tree's pointer to a folder that is not in a copy), a `.git` whose work tree is another folder (a copy inside another repository, whose diff would be that repository's), or a reference that is no commit. Nothing is run, and the message says how to have the history.

**One break with no list entry: `mutate --break`** (since the seventh tooling step). A break to try once, or to show a reviewer, needs no line in a list:

```text
cargo xtask mutate --break crates/cineris-agent/src/turn.rs \
    --in wake --find 'cancelled.as_mut().poll(context).is_ready()' \
    --replace 'cancelled.as_mut().poll(context).is_ready() && false' \
    --test transitions::an_interruption_is_taken_in_every_state_that_waits
```

`--break FILE`, `--find CODE` and `--replace CODE` are the break; `--in ITEM`, `--occurrence N` and `--packages A,B` are the keys `in`, `occurrence` and `packages`, and `--test NAME` is a `caught-by` entry, given once for each (the same matching, a `*` at the end). With no `--test` any failing test catches it.

- **It is the same run as a list's break:** the same pre-flight (the `find` by token, exactly once; a `find` that ends in a comma, an `in` of several items, a `--test` that no test has where a fresh list of tests is kept), the same baseline of the crates' tests, the same compile, the same judging and the same verdicts. It reads no list and writes none: `mutations/` is not touched, so there is nothing to forget and `check` has nothing to fail on.
- **It is named `one-off/<file's name>-<eight hex digits>`,** the digits from the break's own text (every key given, `--test`s included), as in `[ 1/1] one-off/turn-3fa9c2d1  caught by 1 test(s): ...`.
- **Its result is kept** in `target/mutate/results.jsonl` like any other, under a key made of the code and the break's text with a mark that a list's break never has: so a second identical run is instant (nothing is built or run), and a one-off break of the code of a list's break neither takes that break's result nor gives it one. What they share is the tests with nothing broken, which are the same for the same crates and the same code, and are kept once.
- **It is refused beside break names, a list's name and `--file`** (a run is of the one or of the lists), beside `--flagged` and `--merge`, and without `--find` and `--replace`. `--check`, `--list` and `--report` work on it: the pre-flight alone, its name, and what is kept for it.

**Only the breaks a run left without a verdict: `mutate --flagged`** (since the seventh tooling step). A run that a stall or a failed build left unjudged used to be found by reading its output and naming the breaks again. `--flagged` reads `target/mutate/results.jsonl` for the code as it is and chooses, among the breaks the names given choose (all of them without names, or those of `--file`), exactly these:

- **A break whose test run failed without naming a failed test** (the result's status `broken`: COULD NOT BE JUDGED, which is also how a stall is kept: "the machine stalled"), and
- **a break that did not compile** (the result of the compile of every break holds an error, or the status of its test run is `does-not-compile`: DOES NOT COMPILE).

These are the results the next run forgets and tries again whichever breaks it runs, so `--flagged` runs the same pieces with nothing else to name them. It prints each break it chose and why (`step-12/deny-beats-allow  COULD NOT BE JUDGED: the machine stalled. STALL: 3 tests timed out ...`), and runs them. Not chosen: a break with a verdict of its tests, good or flagged (NOT CAUGHT, NOT CAUGHT BY ITS OWN TESTS, NAMES A TEST THAT DOES NOT EXIST, CAUGHT AND EXPECTED TO SURVIVE: asking again gives the same, and `--again NAME` runs one again if a test is suspected); a break not run yet (the same command, run again, carries on); and anything found for other code, which a change to a file forgot. With nothing to choose it says "Nothing is flagged" and exits 0. It works with `--list` (which breaks it would run) and `--report`; a one-off `--break` is not chosen by it, since its text is all it has: give it again.

**The parts of a step's list, put together: `mutate --merge STEP PART…`** (since the seventh tooling step). A step built in several parts gives each part a list of its own, `mutations/<step>-<part>.breaks`, so that no two of them write one file. At the end, `cargo xtask mutate --merge STEP PART…` makes the parts named `mutations/STEP.breaks`:

- **The parts are named,** each `PART` the file `mutations/STEP-PART.breaks`, and they are merged in name order. A list of its own can have a name of the same form (`step-12-fixes` beside `step-12`, `tooling-2` beside a step called `tooling`), and merging it would rename its breaks and delete it (found by the review of the step): with no `PART` the command names every list that looks like a part of `STEP` and merges nothing, and a `PART` with no file is refused. Each part's text is kept as it is, its comments with it, under a comment line that names it: `# From mutations/tooling-7-mutate.breaks (part `mutate`).`. A list `STEP.breaks` that is already there is kept, and the parts follow it.
- **All or nothing.** It refuses a part that is not a list, and two breaks of one name (it names both files and lines: in one list a name is one break). Then it runs the whole pre-flight on what the lists would be afterwards (the other lists as they are, and the merged one): a break that does not apply, one that makes the same change as another, a `find` that ends in a comma, an `in` of several items, and a `caught-by` against the kept lists of tests where a fresh one is kept (with none, the names are not checked, as in `check`: `mutate --check --tests` checks them). Only when that passes is the list written (beside its place, then moved over it) and each part deleted. Otherwise nothing is written, no part is deleted, and the problems are printed with the lines the merged list would have.
- **`--dry-run`** says which parts it found, how many breaks each has and what the pre-flight says, and does none of it.
- **What was kept for the parts' breaks is kept.** A result is stored by the code and the break's own text, never by the name of the list it is in, so a break that moves from `step-13a-fixes` to `step-13a` finds the result it had, and `mutate step-13a` after a merge is as quick as the runs of the parts were. Nothing is forgotten.

**Breaks of code one system builds (`os:`)** (since the seventh tooling step). A break is made in the text, so a break of code that only Windows builds applied on Linux changes code the build does not compile, and its tests pass because nothing changed: it used to be reported as not caught, which it was not. A break with `os: windows` (or `os: linux`) says so:

- **The pre-flight applies it on every system:** `mutate --check` and the `breaks` step of `check` read the file and make the break in memory whichever system they run on, so its code is held and a day's refactor that moves it is found where Linux CI runs, as for every other break. A `caught-by` of it is checked against the kept lists of tests only on its own system, whose tests are the ones built there; on the other system its names are looked for among the words of the source ("The names of a break of the other system's code", below).
- **`mutate` runs it only on that system.** On the other it names the breaks it left and why, in a block of its own before the run, and says how to run them on the system that builds them:

```text
1 break(s) are of code only Windows builds, applied here by the pre-flight and left to a Windows run, which is neither a pass nor a failure of this one:
  tooling-7/nofollow-windows-follows-a-reparse-point  (mutations/tooling-7.breaks:1245)
Run one on a hosted Windows runner: `gh workflow run try --ref <branch> -f os=windows -f command="mutate tooling-7/nofollow-windows-follows-a-reparse-point"`.
```

  It is neither a pass nor a failure: it is not counted among the breaks that did what the list expects or did not, and the run's status is as if the break were not there. A run whose every chosen break is of the other system runs nothing and **fails**, saying so (since the review of the step: an exit of 0 would read as every break caught). (A run on Windows, where `os: windows` is run, the same for a break marked `linux`.)
- **Where it is run, it is judged as any break is,** against the tests that system builds: the Windows-only tests of the crate (a `#[cfg(windows)]` module) are in the Windows build and not in the Linux one, so a break of Windows-only code is caught only by a test that is built and run there. `mutations/tooling-7.breaks` has one as the demonstration: `nofollow-windows-follows-a-reparse-point` makes `cineris-base`'s Windows-only open follow a symbolic link or a junction, and the three Windows-only tests of `nofollow` must fail.

**What it cannot tell.**

- **A break in code the build does not compile is reported as not caught,** unless it says which system builds it. A break is made in the text, so it can be made in code that is behind a feature that is off, and the tests then pass because nothing changed. Run such a break where the code is built, and name in `packages` a crate whose build turns the feature on. Code only Windows (or only Linux) builds has the `os:` key above.
- **A crate alone.** `cargo xtask test`, `stress` and `ci --tiers` of `cineris-sandbox` on Linux turn its `backends` feature on, and say so: without it a build of the crate alone compiles the confinement tests out, and passes having confined nothing (two sessions were misled by such a run). A build of the whole workspace turns it on through `cineris-core`. `mutate` does not add it: a break in the sandbox names `cineris-core` in `packages`, or runs where the workspace is built.
- **Which tests exist depends on what is built.** `cineris-tools` has tests of the seeded order that are only built when `cineris-agent`'s tests are in the same build, because that is what turns `test-hooks` on. A break that names them in `caught-by` names `packages: cineris-tools, cineris-agent`.
- **"Caught" with no `caught-by` is one failing test in one run.** A test that fails by chance would do. The tests with nothing broken are run once, not many times: stress the crate first, and name the tests a break should fail.
- **`find` passes over a trailing comma,** so `f(a)` also finds `f(a,)`, and `(x)` finds the tuple `(x,)`. Whether a break changes anything is judged with commas counted. A match never begins or ends inside an operator of two characters: `= 0` does not find the second half of `x == 0`.
- **A break's tests are run without the caller's test settings:** under seed 0, with the ordinary number of property cases, and writing no snapshot or generated file. A result is kept by the code and the break alone.

**Adding a step's breaks.** Write `mutations/step-NN.breaks` as the rules are built, run `cargo xtask mutate step-NN`, and put the verdicts in the step's report. A break that nothing catches is a missing test.

**The lists as seeded.** Wave 4's breaks were kept only in the reports of the sessions that made them. The lists of steps 7, 8, 9, 10, 12 and 14 were written again from the pull requests' descriptions, the code's own documentation and the tests: 101 breaks, 14 to 20 a step. Every one is caught, by the tests its list names. All 101 run in under five minutes on the reference machine, about a second each once the copy is built. What running them showed is in [steps/tooling-w5/answered.md](steps/tooling-w5/answered.md). This step's own code has a list too, `mutations/tooling-w5.breaks`: 23 breaks of the seeded order, the scratch directory and the commands of `xtask`, written with the code. And `mutations/step-31.breaks` is the 87 breaks step 31's session kept, each as text before and after, converted by a script. All 87 apply and are caught. Six of them no longer applied in the session's own last run, which matched the text exactly: by token, they still find their code.

**How `--changed`, `in-string` and the jobs' answer are tested.** `--changed`: the rule as pure functions over a list of changed files, the breaks and the files' texts as they are and as they were (`mutate::changed::tests`: nothing changed, a file, a test in a file of its own and in a shared one, a test removed or moved, a name no file has, a name that ends in `*`, a break new or changed in its list, a list renamed, a reworded rule, the settings, `packages`, a break with no test), the summary's counts, the refusals of the options, data files (`mutate::readers::tests`: each way a literal names a file, a climb that names nothing, a reader in its tests alone; and in `mutate::changed::tests` the crates built on a reader, a file no crate names, a reader the diff removed, a crate's manifest), `--budget`'s classes, order, stop and report (`mutate::budget::tests`, and `each_chosen_break_is_classed_by_how_near_the_diff_came_to_it`), and one test with a real small git repository in a temporary folder (`sys_a_real_repository_gives_the_files_a_diff_touches_with_both_names_of_a_move`: a changed file, a file moved and staged, a new file git has not been told of, a bad reference). `in-string`: the literals' text (`lex`), where a text is found (code and comments are not searched, once, `occurrence`, the item), the break as `apply` makes it, the refusals (changes nothing, not one string, the same change as another break), the lists' keys, `--break`'s options and `--fix-in`. The jobs: `mutate::jobs::tests::no_job_starts_a_piece_once_the_time_is_up`. Each has breaks in `mutations/tooling-8-mutate.breaks`.

**How the command is tested.** With a runner that reads the copy and says which tests "fail" for what it finds: three breaks, two that are caught and one that nothing catches, and each verdict in the table (unit tests, in the blocking checks). And against the real cargo and nextest, on a small workspace kept for it (`xtask/fixtures/mutate`), with five breaks: two caught, one not, one expected to survive and one that does not compile. That test builds a workspace ten times over (0.8 s in all on the reference machine, several times that on a hosted Windows runner), so it is ignored in the blocking checks. The nightly workflow runs it on Linux, and so does anyone who changes the driver: `cargo xtask test -p xtask --run-ignored only sys_the_real`.

### Asking the permission engine

`cargo xtask decide [--level LEVEL] [--rule RULE]… [--shell posix|powershell|cmd] [--workspace PATH] [--cwd PATH] [--untrusted] [--] COMMAND…` prints what the permission engine decides of a `shell` call running the command: allow (and whether read-only), ask with each reason and the rule an approval would add, or deny with its reason, and the command analysis it stands on. The engine is the one the command table's tests use: workspace `/work/app`, home `/users/dev`, a trusted project, an enforcing sandbox, level `supervised`, no rules, POSIX syntax, unless the flags say otherwise. A rule is `user|project allow|ask|deny read|write|exec|network|ext PATTERN`, with the patterns of the configuration: `--rule 'user allow exec cargo build *'`.

```text
$ cargo xtask decide --rule 'project deny exec git push *' -- git push origin main
command   git push origin main
…
decision  DENY
  because The command is refused by the rule `deny exec git push *` from the project's configuration.
```

It runs the probe that is an ignored test of `cineris-policy`'s integration binary (`decide::probe`), with the case in its environment: `xtask` does not build the engine, which would add its crates to every CI job. A test holds the probe to rows of the command table.

### Invariants to test

An architecture page may end with a section **"Invariants to test"** (since the fourth tooling step): what a review should try to break, so that each review does not have to find the rules again. Pages 06 and 07 have one, seeded from what their reviews found.

- **One row an invariant,** in a table `| Invariant | How a review breaks it | Held by |`: the rule as a sentence that can be false; an attack or a mistake that would make it false (a command, a sequence, a file a repository could carry); and the tests or the break list that hold it today, or "nothing yet", which is a gap to fill.
- **Found by a review, said where:** a row that a review's finding taught names it (`step 26, finding 2`), so the next review knows it was broken once.
- **A row is added when a review finds a new way in,** and changed when the code moves; a row is never removed because a test now holds it.
- **The tools for it:** `cargo xtask scratch-test` for a failing test without registering it, the core's adversarial fixture (`Reach`) for a confined command, `Rig::fill`, `fill_next` and `fill_after` for a context test at a pressure, `cineris_testkit::Phases` for where a `perf_` test's time goes, `cargo xtask mutate` to see a test catch its break.

## Testing the TUI

Three levels, matching the tiers.

| Level | What is tested | How |
|---|---|---|
| **View functions** (tier 1) | That state renders to the right lines. | Pure functions from state, width and theme to styled lines. Snapshots as text with inline style markers, at 60, 80 and 120 columns, in both glyph sets, with and without colour. |
| **Presenters** (tier 2) | That frames reach the terminal correctly. | The presenter writes into a virtual terminal (a terminal-state parser from the test kit). Tests assert the resulting screen and scrollback, and the **no-flicker properties** for every frame: one write, inside a synchronized update, no clear-then-redraw, nothing above the live region touched, bytes per frame under a cap. |
| **The real binary** (tier 3) | That it all works in a terminal. | The binary runs under a pseudo-terminal (ConPTY on Windows), driven by scripted keys, with the scripted provider. Tests assert on the parsed screen, on terminal restoration after exit, panic and signals, on the first frame coming within a generous limit, and on input latency staying under one: each key of a word typed into the prompt (`KEY_ECHO` in `crates/cineris-cli/tests/system/tui.rs`, since the fixes of the review of step 23, which found no such limit on the real binary). |

The no-flicker properties and the latency checks use the method developed for the TUI language comparison ([measurements/tui-language-spike.md](measurements/tui-language-spike.md)).

**What step 20 built for them.**

- **The virtual terminal** (`cineris_testkit::VTerm`) is the test kit's own: an xterm-like screen and scrollback, with wide characters, automatic wrap, margins, the alternate screen, styles, the private modes the terminal layer uses, and the queries it sends. It keeps the enhanced keyboard protocol's flags as a stack per screen, as the protocol has it and as kitty and ghostty do, the alternate screen's kept from one use of it to the next: `keyboard()` is the shown screen's, `main_keyboard()` and `alternate_keyboard()` each screen's (since the fixes of step 25's review, which found one stack for both hiding flags left on the alternate screen). It does not re-wrap on resize. `watch()` and `report()` record what the bytes between them did, checked after every action and not only at the end: the rows touched, counted from the top of the scrollback so that a row keeps its number when the screen scrolls; the rows an erase left blank that had text before and after (a clear followed by a redraw); clears of the whole screen; and whether the bytes began and ended one synchronized update. `Report::problems(live_top, sync)` is the list of the properties a frame breaks. It takes `U+009B` as the start of a control sequence, as some terminals do, so a renderer that let one through is seen.
- **The presenter's tests** (`crates/cineris-term/tests/it/presenter.rs`, tier 2) check every frame of a generated script of commits, live regions, cursors, repeated frames and height changes (150 scripts on a pull request): one call to the sink, the properties above, and the right scrollback and screen after it, with the cursor where the frame put it. Others check a streaming frame's size (median under 1 KiB, none over 4 KiB), a narrower window, and content full of escape sequences.
- **The pseudo-terminal driver** (`cineris_testkit::pty::Session`) runs a program in a pseudo-terminal (ConPTY on Windows), records each piece of output with the time it arrived, parses it with a `VTerm`, types keys, answers queries as a modern terminal would when asked to, and waits on what the screen shows. The system's half (opening the pseudo-terminal, starting the program in it) is in `cineris-term` behind its `pty` feature, beside the crate's other `unsafe` code; `cargo xtask rules` lets only dev-dependencies and the test kit turn the feature on.
- **The system tests** (`crates/cineris-term/tests/it/system.rs`, tier 3) run the crate's own test binary as a small shell, which records the terminal's modes (on Windows it first turns virtual-terminal processing off, as the old console host has it), runs the demo as a child, and says whether the modes after are the ones before. The demo draws, echoes keys and is resized, and is ended by Ctrl+C, by a panic, and by SIGTERM or a Ctrl+Break event; each time the modes must be back, the cursor shown, and nothing left on. On Windows the virtual terminal sees ConPTY's own rendering, not the demo's bytes, so there the shell's comparison of the console's modes is the check that speaks for the demo. On Linux a further test stops the demo with Ctrl+Z and continues it, and checks that everything is put back while it is stopped and on again after. The first frame and a key's echo have coarse limits there: five times the runner's first-frame budget, and 250 ms.
- **`cargo xtask perf`** builds the demo optimised and runs the measurement (`crates/cineris-term/tests/it/perf.rs`, ignored in every ordinary run) against the TUI's budgets. It runs on Linux and on Windows; no workflow runs it yet ([outstanding/perf-in-the-main-workflow.md](outstanding/perf-in-the-main-workflow.md)). **On Unix it runs each demo twice** (since the fifth tooling step), in both glyph sets: under `LC_ALL=C.UTF-8` (Unicode) and under `LC_ALL=C` (ASCII), which is how the terminal layer chooses the set there; a `--json` path gets `.ascii` for the second. On Windows the console decides, and ConPTY gets the ASCII set, so it runs once.

**Answering a dialog in a system test** (since the eighth tooling step). The application reads keys that arrive in one read as typing, and a dialog takes none of them as an answer (`App::burst`), so a key typed before the dialog has taken the one before can make the answer go to the input box. `Session::answer_dialog(limit, answers, key, answered)` waits for the dialog's row of answers (drawn only once the dialog takes keys), types the key alone, and returns once the row has left the screen and `answered` holds: something only the answer brings (the header after trust, the tool's result after an approval), since a frame can reach the terminal in pieces and one piece can lack the row while the dialog is up. Every dialog answer in `cineris-cli`'s system tests goes through it. It reads only what the screen shows, so it holds whatever rule decides when a dialog takes keys, as long as its answers are drawn only when they can be pressed. The flake it ended (`sys_a_projects_first_limit_is_listed_and_in_force_once_trusted`, 2026-11-16) was a wait for the header that matched the text `Cineris` in a path on the screen (the trust question shows the workspace's path, under the temporary folder, which can hold the repository's name when the temporary folder is inside a checkout named after it; a hosted runner's temporary folder does not): it passed at once, the test typed `go` and Enter a few microseconds after `y`, and on a fast build all four were read together. A wait for the header matches its title and version. And a command that needs the turn to have ended (`/undo`) waits for the idle input box first, not for the turn's last answer, which is drawn before the turn ends.

**What the eighth tooling step added.** Wave 11 found two kinds of Windows-only difference late, through `try`: the wording of how a process ended, and the ASCII stand-ins for the interface's glyphs. A test spells neither now, and the console's closing is driven.

- **How a process ended** (`cineris_testkit::exit`). `ExitStatus` says `exit status: 1` on Unix and `exit code: 1` on Windows, a signal as `signal: 6 (SIGABRT)`, and a fault as `exit code: 0xc0000005`. `Exit::parse` reads either system's wording into `Exit::Code(n)`, `Exit::Signal(n)` or `Exit::Fault(status)`, and `Exit::of(status)` makes the same from a status itself. `Verdict::parse(screen_text)` reads what a `ModeShell` printed (`restored: yes (exit status: 129)`) into whether the modes were put back and how the child ended, so a test compares one value: `assert_eq!(Verdict::parse(&shown), Some(Verdict { restored: true, exit: Exit::Code(129) }))`. A text that is not an ending, a stop, and a verdict cut short on the screen are `None`. Tested on both systems' texts, and, for each system, against the text of a real `ExitStatus` of that system. It cannot tell a Windows code with the high bit set from a signal: `Fault` is Windows' and `Signal` is Unix's, and a test that expects an abort writes the one its system has.
- **Either glyph set** (`cineris_testkit::glyphs`). The interface draws `◆` and `│`, or `>` and `|` where the console gets the ASCII set (every Windows console, and a Unix terminal under the `C` locale). A test spells a text once, as a function of the set, and `GlyphSets::shows`, `history_has_row` (with a `RowPattern` of a start and the texts a row holds) and `strip_prefix` and `strip_suffix` try each set. The sets are `cineris-tui`'s own (`Glyphs::UNICODE` and `Glyphs::ASCII`), which the test kit cannot depend on, so the test passes them: `GlyphSets::new(&[Glyphs::UNICODE, Glyphs::ASCII])`, and there is one copy of each glyph, the product's. It matches rows by text, not by style or cell, and a row that mixes the two sets does not match either.
- **The console closing under the program** (`Session::close_console`, below).
- **An application frame as a snapshot** (`cineris-tui`'s `testing::assert_app_frame`, crate-private). `assert_app_frame("app-<name>", &mut app, now, columns, rows)` resizes the `App` to a window of that size, draws its real frame (`App::frame`) and compares it with `snapshots/app-<name>.txt`: the lines the frame committed to the scrollback, the live region, and where the cursor goes, each line with its styles in the view snapshots' markup. `CINERIS_UPDATE_SNAPSHOTS=1` writes it, as for every snapshot of the crate. It is for a test of what the application shows as a whole (a dialog, the header and the hint row); a test of one fact still asserts the fact. One call draws one frame and consumes what it committed, so a test that wants three sizes makes three snapshots. The dialog tests in `app/tests.rs` that checked three rows of a frame with `contains` check the frame.

**The console closing** (`Session::close_console`, since the eighth tooling step). A closed terminal window is a way the application ends that no key or signal of the test's makes: on Windows the console closes under it (`CTRL_CLOSE_EVENT`), on Unix the terminal hangs up (SIGHUP). `Session::close_console` does that to the program in the session and returns when it did; the program's end is read with `wait_exit`.

- **On Windows** it calls `ClosePseudoConsole` while the program runs, which is what closing a Windows Terminal tab does: the console host sends every process on it the event and ends it when its handler returns. The call may wait for the program's handler, so it runs on a thread of its own and `close_console` does not wait for it. The console is gone with its screen: the output ends, nothing can be typed, and a shell of the test's goes with it, so its verdict is never on the screen. A test of the application reads what it leaves outside the terminal.
- **On Unix** it sends SIGHUP and then SIGCONT to the program's process group, as the kernel does when the terminal's master closes. The master itself cannot be closed under the driver's reader, which holds it open in a read, so a write to the terminal still succeeds afterwards where after a real hang-up it fails; that case (a failed write) is the application's own test of step 23. A `ModeShell` that should live through it to say how its child ended asks `surviving_hangup()`: a handler that does nothing, which, unlike "ignore", the child does not inherit.
- **The application's answer** is `sys_the_console_closing_ends_the_application_and_stops_its_turn` and `sys_the_console_closing_stops_a_running_command` (`crates/cineris-cli/tests/system/tui.rs`), beside the SIGHUP test they mirror. The real binary runs under its shell, a turn is streaming or a command is running, and the console is closed; the request is dropped or the command is gone, within 2 s, under the 3 s of the watchdog that would end an application that did not (an end the watchdog made fails). On Unix the shell's verdict then says the terminal was put back and the code is 129, as for SIGHUP. The tests close the console just after a whole frame (the spinner has turned, and the hint row, the frame's last, shows it): a signal that lands while the application writes a frame makes that write fail, and the application ends with exit code 1 and "the terminal failed: the terminal has been put back", not with the signal's code. That is a finding about the application, not a flake of the test, and is open (the eighth tooling step reported it); it shows in 1 of 600 runs on a machine held to two cores with six busy threads beside it, and in none of 1,000 on an idle one, and the SIGHUP test beside these has the same window. On Windows the only evidence is what the application leaves outside the console, and whether the handler ran in order, with the terminal put back, is not seen: nothing is on a screen that has gone.

**The application's test kit** (`cineris_tui::testkit`, since the ninth tooling step). The application driven as its loop drives it, with a scripted link in the core's place, for `cineris-tui`'s own tests and any other crate's. `Scripted` is the link: it records each operation sent under a new ticket (`take_sent`) and each answer to a request (`take_answers`), and gives what the test queued with `give`, oldest first; a clone is the same link. `session()` is a `SessionBuilder` with sane defaults (the session `s_test` in `/work/app`, its one write root, supervised, no sandbox, no trust standing, Claude Opus as the main model), so that a test states only what it cares about: `session().sandbox(SandboxStatus::Enforcing).build()`; it also sets the id, workspace, title, level, trust, sequence number, running turn and main model. `caps()` is a modern terminal (true colour, UTF-8); `app(now)` and `app_opening(now, prompt, open)` make the application and its link; `opened(app, link, now)` draws the first frame and answers the open (`opened_with` with a session of the test's); `drain` takes in what is queued; `event` wraps an event of the session; `approval(call)` is a request to run `cargo build`; `ask(app, link, id, request, now)` gives a request and draws its dialog's first frame; `shown_dialog(app, link, id, now)` gives an approval, draws it until it is ready, and returns the time one key answers it. Every call takes the time it happens at, and nothing waits. It is behind `cineris-tui`'s `test-kit` feature, off by default: the crate's unit tests have it with no feature, its integration tests through a dev-dependency on the crate itself, and another crate's tests ask for it with `cineris-tui = { workspace = true, features = ["test-kit"] }` under `[dev-dependencies]`. `cargo xtask rules` refuses any other way of turning it on, as for `test-hooks`, so the product never contains it. Its own tests are in `testkit.rs`; `crates/cineris-tui/tests/it/app.rs` uses it as another crate would.

**Keys at a person's pace** (`cineris_testkit::cadence`, since the ninth tooling step). The application tells typing from a paste, and a key pressed from a key held down, by time, so a test of that needs keys with times. `Cadence::starting(at)` is a timeline: `typing(text, every)` puts a key every `every`, the first one gap after the last input; `wait(pause)` moves on; `key(key)`, `together(text)` (keys in one read: a paste where the terminal has no bracketed paste, or typing a stall held back), `paste(text)` and `event(event)` come at the time of the last input; `hold(key, repeat, n)` is the press, then `n` repeats, the first `repeat.delay` after it and the rest `repeat.every` apart. `AutoRepeat::X11` (660 ms, then 25 a second, unmarked) and `AutoRepeat::WINDOWS` (500 ms, then about 30 a second, marked as win32 input marks them) are the systems' defaults, and `AutoRepeat::new(delay, every)`, `.marked()` any other; a marked repeat is `Event::Repeat`, an unmarked one the same `Event::Key` again. `strokes()` gives each event as a `Stroke` with the time it was read and `queued`, true when the next stroke was read with it, which is what the loop's `Terminal::queued` says. The test feeds them as the loop does: `App::burst(at)` for a key read with more input, then `App::terminal(&event, at)`, and a frame after each read (`feed` in `app/tests/dialogs.rs` and `tests/it/app.rs`). It is data only: there is no pseudo-terminal form, because a program in a pseudo-terminal reads its own clock, and keys typed into one at a pace would need the test to wait in real time.

### A dialog's times in a system test

A dialog and each step of the first run take no key until 0.3 s after their first frame and a second after the key that answered the one before, and a second after input they did not take (`cineris-tui`'s `app/ready.rs`). The binary's system tests wait those times in real time. A test that answers dialogs and does not test the rule's timing may have them ready in a tenth of the time: `Machine::quick_dialogs()` (`cineris-cli`'s system rig) sets `CINERIS_TEST_READY_DIVISOR` for the binary it starts, which only a build with the `test-hooks` feature reads (`cineris_base::hook::ready_divisor`; since CI budgets II, [steps/ci-budgets-2/answered.md](steps/ci-budgets-2/answered.md)). The rule divides each of its times by it where it reads one, so that it keeps its proportions.

- **The first run's flow test uses it** (4.4 s to 0.5 s). The rule's own tests are `cineris-tui`'s, on a fake clock, at its real times.
- **A test that types through a dialog, or answers one against the quiet second, must not:** that is the rule it tests, end to end. `tui::sys_typing_through_an_approval_over_attach_does_not_answer_it` runs on both systems at a person's pace, and so do the trust and undo questions' tests.

### Keys through `send_key`

A pty test sends each key with `Session::send_key(NAME)` (`cineris_testkit::pty`), never as the raw bytes of an escape sequence. Under Windows' pseudo-console the program has asked for Windows Terminal's win32 input mode, and there a key arrives as input records: an xterm sequence typed into it is misread or dropped, so a test that writes `b"\x1b[5~"` for PageUp passes on Linux and does something else on Windows ([conpty.md](conpty.md)). `send_key` sends the records on Windows and the xterm sequence elsewhere.

- **Names:** `f1` to `f12`, `up`, `down`, `left`, `right`, `pageup`, `pagedown` and `escape`, each with any of `shift+`, `ctrl+` and `alt+` before it (since the twelfth tooling step): `session.send_key("ctrl+up")`, `session.send_key("escape")`.
- **The rule** (`cargo xtask rules`, since the twelfth tooling step) reads the crates' test code (a file under a `tests` folder, or a test module in a file of its own) for each call of a sender: `.send(…)`, the tests' helper `type_text(…)`, or any function of the same file whose body calls `.send(`. It reads the call's literals (in any spelling of the Escape: `\x1b`, `\u{1B}`, the byte itself), the file's bindings of a literal that it names (`const`, `static`, `let`), and its array literals of bytes (`&[0x1b, b'[', b'A']`, `'\x1b' as u8`; an index or a repeat is no array). It fails on an escape sequence in them that is a key: a lone Escape, Alt with a character (`ESC x`), `SS3 X`, and a `CSI` sequence that ends as a key does. The mouse's SGR reports, bracketed paste's marks, focus reports and a terminal's answers (a string up to its BEL or ST) are not keys and pass. Product code is not read: it writes the same bytes to a terminal as output (the cursor's home is the Home key's sequence). The failure names the sequence and says to use `send_key`:

  ```text
  crates/cineris-cli/tests/system/tui.rs:1871: a pty test sends `ESC[5~` as raw bytes. Under Windows' win32 input mode a pseudo-console misreads or drops a plain sequence: send each key with `session.send_key(NAME)` (…)
  ```

- **A test of the input decoder itself**, which must send the bytes, says so on the line before the call or on any line of it: `// raw key: WHY` (`cineris-term`'s `sys_keys_arrive_with_their_modifiers`).
- **What it cannot see:** a sequence built at run time (`format!`), which is how a test writes the win32 records it checks (those are tests of the decoder too); a binding or a helper of another file; and a test module written inside a product file. A binding is found by its name in the whole file, whatever its scope. A cursor position answer for the first row (`CSI 1 ; m R`) has the bytes of F3 with a modifier, and is taken for the key.
- **What it costs:** only the test files that call `send(` or `type_text(` are lexed, a few hundred: a few milliseconds of the blocking lint job.

### The test binary in roles

A terminal's system tests run their crate's test binary again, as a small shell, as the program under test, and as what that program runs in turn (an editor). `cineris_testkit::role` (since the fifth tooling step) holds what `cineris-term` and `cineris-tui` each had a copy of:

- **`Role`:** a mark such as `cinterm-demo=`. `Role::command(options)` starts this binary with the entry test alone (`child_entry`) and the mark with the options after it; in the child, `Role::options()` gives them back, so the entry test knows which part to play. `arguments` gives the same for the pseudo-terminal driver, and `command_line` for a variable such as `$EDITOR`.
- **`Report`:** a file of `name: value` lines in the test's scratch directory, which a child writes and the test reads once it is gone. A child's standard output is the terminal, which ConPTY re-renders.
- **`ModeShell`:** records the terminal's modes, runs a child, and prints `restored: yes (<how it ended>)` when the modes after it are the ones before, or `restored: NO (<how it ended>)` with both, read back by `exit::Verdict::parse`. It can start the child as the old console host would (virtual-terminal processing off), pass the interrupt keys over while the child runs, and say a line first. It gives its children the modes it saw, so that a grandchild can ask `same_modes_as_the_shell()`.

**Signals made to order** (Unix). Step 21's hand-off of `Ctrl+C` to a child (`cineris_term::ignore_interrupts`) has three parts: a do-nothing handler while the guard lives, a drain of an interrupt still pending as it ends, and a grace of a second after, for one another thread took from the queue as the guard was ending. The race they close needs the kernel to hand a signal to a thread at the wrong moment, so they were reasoned and not tested. `cineris_term::pty::interrupt_pending_here()` leaves an interrupt pending on the calling thread, blocked and sent to it, as the kernel leaves one it has not handed over; `raise_interrupt()` runs its handler at a known point. With them `crates/cineris-term/tests/it/signals.rs` tests all three, each in a child of its own and each against a control with no guard, which must end the child with SIGINT. On Windows there is no pending interrupt to drain: a console control event runs its handler on a thread the system starts for it.

Snapshot changes are reviewed like code. A change in a view snapshot is a change in what users see.

**The view snapshots (step 22)** are text with inline style markers, in `crates/cineris-tui/snapshots/`: a style is written where it changes, as `{fg=a13 bg=#132A17 b d i u r link=…}` (`a` a palette colour, `i` an indexed one, `#` an exact one, then bold, dim, italic, underline, reverse), `{}` is the plain style, and each row ends in `|` so that its trailing spaces are seen. One test an element writes one file with a section a variant: 60, 80 and 120 columns in both glyph sets in true colour on a dark background, then at 80 columns true colour on a light one, 16 colours on both, 256 colours and no colour. Width and glyph set change the layout and touch no style; depth and background change styles and touch no layout, so each is covered against the other without the whole product, and the golden screens below cover the product for the reference session. `CINERIS_UPDATE_SNAPSHOTS=1` writes them.

### The golden screens

The reference screens of the chosen look ([architecture/tui-look/](architecture/tui-look/README.md)) are drawn again by `cineris-tui`'s real views, through the inline presenter, into the test kit's virtual terminal, and compared cell by cell with the reference files fed into another (`crates/cineris-tui/tests/it/screens/`). The session they show is Rust test data there, composed as the interactive application will compose it.

- **It is a tier-2 test, so every pull request runs it.** It draws 38 screens (19 on two backgrounds) in about 0.2 s in CI's build on the reference machine, with one highlighter for them all as the application has, the views and the highlighter included, so it is cheap enough for the blocking tier; it needs the build, so it could not be part of `cargo xtask check`, which builds nothing it does not lint.
- **Every row the views built so far draw is compared** (step 22's, and step 21's input box and menus), text, style and wide cells alike, as a cell is seen: a space is compared by its background and by whether it is reversed or underlined, which is all of a space that shows.
- **A row another step draws is counted as not built**, with the step that builds it: since step 23 composed its dialogs, the viewer's frame and the input box's placeholder into the screens, only the panels (step 24). It is never counted as compared. **A dialog is the application's own frame** (screens 2a to 2d, since the review of step 23): the real `App` is given the request by a scripted core, in a window of the screen's size, and the live region of the frame it draws once the dialog takes keys is compared, so that its answers, their order, their fitting to the window and its blank and hint rows are held, not a list the test wrote. The test holds each screen's count of compared rows, so a row skipped by mistake fails it, and a self-check gives the comparison a screen with one changed cell and one row marked not built, and requires the first found and the second left alone. When a step builds the rows it owns, it composes them into the screens and raises the counts.
- **Style is compared as well as text:** a cell is the same when its text and its style are, a space by what of it shows. A self-check gives the comparison a cell in another colour, one made bold and a space with another background, and requires each found, and a space whose foreground alone changed not (since the fifth tooling step). The report counts, for each screen, how many of the cells that differ do so in style alone: the owner's colour decision of 2026-11-15 showed as 320 such cells.
- **`cargo xtask tui-screens`** prints the report: each screen's rows, the rows compared, the rows not built by whom, and every cell that differs. **`--write`** rewrites the reference rows that differ from the real views, in the reference files' own form, and lists every changed cell, writes each screen's `.txt` copy from the dark one (a test holds the two together), then compares again writing nothing and fails unless that passes. That is the only way a reference screen changes, and the cells it changed go in the step's report for the owner to see.

## Windows coverage

Every row is a test, or a group of tests, that runs on the Windows job.

| Area | What is checked |
|---|---|
| Paths | Both separators accepted; case-insensitive comparison; device names and UNC paths rejected; long paths; `~` expansion. Built in step 4 (`crates/cineris-base/tests/it/paths_windows.rs`), with these further cases: trailing dots and spaces and alternate data streams rejected; drive-relative and root-relative paths rejected; a directory link and a junction that leave the workspace resolve to their targets; a link to a network share is refused without the share being contacted; a short (8.3) name resolves to the same path as the long one. The junction is made by `mklink`, so that test is a system test. No test reaches for a share, not even the machine's own: that an allowed share passes is tested on the text. |
| Paths, through the tools | Built in step 6 (`crates/cineris-tools/tests/it/paths_windows.rs`): `read`, `list` and `search` each accept both separators and show `/`; find a file asked for in another case and show its on-disk spelling, as one seen file; take an absolute path with a drive letter; and refuse, when the call is planned, device names, the device and extended-length forms, trailing dots and spaces, alternate data streams, the characters Windows does not allow, drive-relative and root-relative paths, and network paths (on the text, with a server name that cannot exist). A walk steps past a file whose name ends with a dot and one named `aux`, both made through an extended-length path, and says so. A path longer than 260 characters is read, listed and searched. Globs take either separator and ignore case. Ignore files with CRLF endings are honoured. |
| Shells | PowerShell 7, Windows PowerShell and `cmd.exe`: quoting, merged output, exit status, the model being told which shell it has. Git Bash where the runner has it. Built in step 11 (`crates/cineris-tools/tests/it/shell.rs`), as one set of tests run once for each shell: the two output streams arrive as one, in the order written; the exit status is the command's own, a program's when a program ran last, and 1 for a PowerShell cmdlet that failed; twenty-seven hostile texts (quotes, `&`, `\|`, `^`, `%VAR%`, `$(…)`, back-ticks, a line break, a trailing backslash, typographic quotes) arrive unchanged at a program that prints its arguments, and through PowerShell itself, and none of them runs; a command with quotes after backslashes reaches the shell as written; the environment has the settings that keep tools from asking; the working directory may have `&` in its name; the input is closed; 6,000 lines of output are cut to a head and a tail with all of it in the artifact; and the tool's description names the shell and the version the shell itself reported. Which shell is found, and the order of the fallbacks, is tested on both OSes for both (`shell_search.rs`). A command with a line break is refused for `cmd.exe`. |
| Permissions | Built in step 12. **PowerShell and `cmd.exe` commands** decided on the real file system (`crates/cineris-policy/tests/it/real_paths.rs`): a PowerShell command that only reads runs under any casing and either separator; a file that looks like a secret, a path a rule denies and a file outside the workspace are asked about under any casing; the spellings the resolver refuses (a trailing dot, an alternate data stream, a device name, the device and extended-length forms, a network share, drive-relative and root-relative paths, a provider path such as `Env:PATH`) are asked about, because what they name cannot be checked; more than one command, the call operator and an encoded command are asked about; a deny holds however the command is written. Every `cmd.exe` command is asked about, no allow rule applies to one, and a deny still does. **PowerShell's own parser** is asked about every generated line the analysis models (`differential.rs`). **Case:** an allow rule reaches the folder it names however the path is typed, and a folder that differs only in case, in a directory made case-sensitive with `fsutil`, is outside the scope and not granted by an allow for the other; the project key of one folder is the same under every casing, and two such folders have two keys (`crates/cineris-base/tests/it/paths_windows.rs`). **Files:** the stored approvals and the trust store are owner-only. |
| Line endings | CRLF and mixed files survive `edit` and `write` byte for byte outside the edited lines; `.gitattributes` keeps fixtures exact. Built in step 10, and run on both OSes because the rules are the same on both: the properties of the matcher (`crates/cineris-tools/src/matching/tests.rs`) and of the tools on real files (`crates/cineris-tools/tests/it/editing.rs`), which make their files byte by byte and use no fixture. On Windows only (`editing_windows.rs`): a new file is written with the line feeds the model gave, and a file with CRLF and a byte-order mark keeps both through an edit. |
| Process trees | A command with a grandchild is fully gone after cancellation, after timeout, and after the harness is killed (Job Objects). Built in step 11, for the tree itself (`crates/cineris-sandbox/tests/it/tree.rs`) and through the tool (`crates/cineris-tools/tests/it/process.rs`), with a grandchild that the polite interrupt does not stop (on Windows one with no console, which a console event never reaches): gone after the time limit, after cancellation, when the call's future is dropped, and when a harness that is a process of its own is killed. A process left in the background is stopped and reported, for each shell, whether it holds the command's output or not. |
| Files in use | Replacing a file another process holds open retries and then fails cleanly. Built in step 4 (`crates/cineris-base/tests/it/atomic.rs`): a file held with no sharing fails after seven attempts and half a second of waiting, all of it on the fake clock, with the file unchanged and no temporary file left; a file let go during the retries is replaced. Through the editing tools since step 10 (`crates/cineris-tools/tests/it/editing_windows.rs`): a file another program holds with no sharing cannot be read, so `edit` and `write` fail at once and wait for nothing; a file another program holds for reading is read, the replace is tried seven times over half a second of the fake clock, and the call fails with the file byte for byte as it was, no temporary file beside it, the session's table of seen files unchanged and the round's checkpoint holding the content it still has; a file let go during the retries is edited; a file with the read-only attribute is refused; and a file read under one spelling is edited under another case and the other separator. The open that follows no link is tested there against a real symbolic link to a file and to a directory, a real junction, and a file held with no sharing (`crates/cineris-base/tests/it/nofollow.rs`). |
| Terminal | ConPTY end-to-end tests of the TUI: key release events ignored, new-line keys, resize, restoration. Built in step 20 for the terminal layer (`crates/cineris-term/tests/it/system.rs`): the demo under ConPTY, started with virtual-terminal processing off as the old console host has it, draws without an escape sequence on screen, echoes keys, survives a resize, and puts back the console's two modes and two code pages after Ctrl+C, a panic and a Ctrl+Break event. Unit tests of the console modes the layer sets, and of a key outside the Basic Multilingual Plane arriving in two halves. The console closing under the real application (since the eighth tooling step, `crates/cineris-cli/tests/system/tui.rs`): `Session::close_console` closes the pseudo-console while a turn streams and while a command runs, and the request is dropped or the command gone within 2 s. Written without a Windows machine and run only by CI; what it cannot see is the order inside the application, since the shell goes with the console. |
| Script shims | A `.cmd`-shim MCP server and hook command start with arguments intact; unsafe arguments are refused. |
| Permissions on files | Session, credential and state files are readable only by the user.
