Built in step 9 (`crates/cineris-base/tests/it/private.rs`): a file made by `private::create_new`, a file left by an owner-only atomic write (new, and over one made by hand), and a directory made by `private::create_dir_all` each have the current user as owner and as the one entry of the access list, with full control and inheritance off, read back from the file system; a path longer than 260 characters works; a link under the name is not followed. One of them asks `icacls`, which shares no code with the module, for a second opinion, and is a system test because `icacls` is a process. The credentials file and its lock file are checked the same way (`crates/cineris-config/tests/it/credentials.rs`). Step 8 checks every file and directory a session leaves (the log, its lock, a cut line set aside, artifacts, the checkpoint store, a fork, the index and its lock) with `is_private`, on both OSes (`crates/cineris-session/tests/it/private.rs`). |
| Credentials | A key round-trips through the real Windows Credential Manager, under an entry name of the test's own that no real entry has, removed when the test ends, pass or fail (`crates/cineris-config/tests/it/credentials.rs`). |
| Configuration | Files with a byte-order mark and CRLF parse, and an error in one names the same line as it would without them. Built in step 9 (`crates/cineris-config/tests/it/loading.rs`). |
| Extension scripts | The script engine builds and runs, and a script's host calls are denied exactly as on Linux. |
| Command names | The binary behaves identically as `cineris.exe` and `cin.exe`. The install script creates the alias as a hard link, falls back to a copy, and takes a name another program provides only on the user's yes, never with no terminal to ask on, leaving the other program's file as it was ([decision 0038](decisions/0038-the-project-is-named-cineris.md)). |

What CI cannot check is how a real terminal window looks. That is the manual checklist in [measurements/windows-terminal-checklist.md](measurements/windows-terminal-checklist.md), run by the owner at the points the build plan names.

## CI layout

### Blocking, on every pull request

Six jobs, in `.github/workflows/ci.yml`: four in parallel, and Windows' two test jobs side by side after Windows' build job. Wall time is the slowest of `lint-linux`, `lint-windows`, `test-linux`, and `build-windows` with the slower of the two jobs after it. Each job has five minutes of its own.

| Job | Runner | Runs |
|---|---|---|
| `lint-linux` | `ubuntu-latest` | `cargo xtask check`, and the owner guard ("The owner guard", below): `cargo fmt --all --check`; the repo rules; the break lists, each break of which must still apply ("Breaking the code on purpose"); the generated files (the protocol's schema and TypeScript declarations are what the types generate); `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets --locked -- -D warnings`; `cargo deny check licenses bans sources`. |
| `lint-windows` | `windows-latest` | `cargo xtask check clippy`. Formatting, the repo rules and cargo-deny give the same answer on every OS and run once. Clippy runs on both, because code compiled only for Windows is otherwise never linted. |
| `test-linux` | `ubuntu-latest` | `cargo xtask ci test`: build the tests, run tiers 1 and 2 and then tier 3 (`cargo nextest run --workspace --locked --cargo-profile ci --profile ci`, with `-E 'not test(/(^|::)sys_/)'` and then `-E 'test(/(^|::)sys_/)'`), check the tier budgets and list each tier's slowest tests. Then the JUnit report of both runs is kept as an artifact. |
| `build-windows` | `windows-latest` | `cargo xtask ci test --archive DIR`: builds the tests as `ci test` builds them, from the main branch's build of the workspace's own crates where it can ("The workspace's own build, cached", below), into a nextest archive (`cargo nextest archive --workspace --locked --cargo-profile ci`), with `xtask`'s own binary beside it, kept as the artifact `windows-tests` for a day. Runs no test. |
| `test-windows-tiers` | `windows-latest` | `cargo xtask ci test tiers --from-archive DIR`, with the binary the build job kept: extracts the archive over the checkout, runs tiers 1 and 2, and checks their budgets. Builds nothing and reads no cache. Its report is kept as `junit-windows-tiers`. |
| `test-windows-system` | `windows-latest` | `cargo xtask ci test system --from-archive DIR`: the same, for the system tests, after the start probe (below), and the budget of tier 3. Its report is kept as `junit-windows-system`, with the probe's beside it. |

The first draft of this page had three jobs. `lint-windows` was added in step 2: it runs beside the others, so it adds nothing to the wall time, and it closes a gap that would have opened as soon as the first Windows-only module was written.

**Windows' tests are built in one job and run in two** (since the Windows split, [steps/windows-split/answered.md](steps/windows-split/answered.md)); until then they were one job, `test-windows`. A pull request that changes a crate every other is built on (`cineris-base`) builds every crate again, and on a slow runner the build (126 to 146 s), the two runs of the tests (about 110 to 120 s) and the runner's own steps (about 35 s) ran out the job's five minutes, sometimes after every test had passed. Linux's job is as it was: its build and its runs fit with minutes to spare.

- **What runs where.** The build job's archive holds the test binaries, the binaries the system tests start (`cineris`), and the metadata nextest runs them from. A test job extracts it over its own checkout of the same commit, so the paths the binaries were built with (`CARGO_BIN_EXE_*`, `CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR`, `CARGO_TARGET_TMPDIR`) are where they point, and runs `xtask` from the binary the build job kept beside it: nothing is built and no cache is read. A checkout at another place than the build's is refused, from the archive's own cargo metadata, before any test runs. The toolchain is still installed, for `cargo nextest` and the tests that run cargo. Each job runs nextest's `ci` profile with today's groups and lanes (`tiers-windows`; `system-windows` and `system-windows-shells`), and a run that chooses no test fails (`--no-tests=fail`).
- **The start probe.** Each tier is held to its budget above the start of a test's process, measured on the run's own unit and integration tests ("Test tiers"). The system tests' job has none of those, and the other job's runner is not its runner. So it runs a probe first: the unit tests of the libraries of `cineris-base`, `cineris-term` and `cineris-tools` (over 500, about 4 s at two at a time), whose report is kept apart (`start-probe.xml`) and whose start tier 3 is held above. A probe that runs no test, fewer than 500 (too few to measure a start on), or one of whose tests fails, fails the job; it never only moves the figure.
- **A part holds its tiers.** Each job's tier check refuses a report with a test of another tier, or with none of one of its own, and shows and judges only its own tiers. The tiers' verdicts are as they were: every test of a tier runs in one job.
- **A failed build fails the test jobs.** They run unless the run was cancelled, and their first step fails when `build-windows` did not succeed: a job skipped because a job it needs failed reports success, and as a required check would pass having tested nothing. `build-windows` is a required check as well.
- **What a pull request from a fork can reach.** The archive is an artifact of its own run, for that run's jobs: `actions/download-artifact` reads the run it is in, and `cargo xtask rules` refuses, in a workflow that runs on pull requests, a download that names another run or repository (`run-id`, `github-token`, `repository`). A run can upload only to itself, and its token is read-only. The archive holds what the pull request's own code built, which its test jobs would build themselves, and no secret: the one secret is the owner guard's, in `lint-linux`. A re-run of a failed test job reads the same run's archive again; a re-run of the build job replaces it (`overwrite`).
- **Reading a run's reports.** `ci-times`, `ci --tiers --compare` and the other readers of a run take the two parts' reports as one, tiers 1 and 2 first, as the one job's report was, and still read a run from before the split, which kept `junit-windows` (`xtask/src/runs.rs`, `report_forms`). A run that kept one part and not the other, or a part with its probe's report and not its own, has no whole report, and the readers say so and read none. **Each part is held above its own runner's start:** tiers 1 and 2 above the start their tests measured, as every report was, and the system tests above the start their probe measured, which the readers write on the system part's suites (`cineris-start`) when they make the report and read onto its tests, so the two runners' speeds do not mix: two runners can differ by 6 ms a test, about 2.5 s over the system tests. The tables say both starts (`ci-times`'s column `(5 ms; tier 3 8 ms)`).

**The jobs call `cargo xtask`.** What a job runs is defined in the `xtask` crate and nowhere else, so `cargo xtask ci` on a local machine runs what CI runs. Every step of a command runs even when an earlier one failed, so one CI round trip reports everything that is wrong. Each step is timed, and the timings go to the log and to the job's summary page.

Settings common to all jobs:

- `--locked` and `CARGO_INCREMENTAL=0`.
- Each job has `timeout-minutes: 5` on a pull request. That makes the budget a fact: a job that takes longer fails. **The test jobs, and Windows' build job, have 12 on every other event** (since step 25): only `main` saves the cache, and a run cut off saves nothing, so a run of `main` must be able to build from cold and save; otherwise one cold start (the first run after the rename to Cineris built for 3m22s on Windows and was cut off 10 s into its tests) cuts off every run after it, pull requests' included. The lint jobs keep five on every event: the cold Windows lint after the rename took 2m26s.
- The toolchain version comes from `rust-toolchain.toml` and is installed with `rustup toolchain install`. The version is pinned exactly, so a new stable release never changes the lints under a pull request. Installing it costs 8 to 9 s in every job, cache or no cache (11 to 13 s on Windows before `RUSTUP_HOME` was moved off the system disk).
- **Tests build in cargo's `ci` profile:** `dev`, with third-party crates unoptimised. The reason is in "Where the five minutes go". **No crate has debug info there** (since the CI-budgets fix, by the maintainers' decision; [steps/ci-budgets/answered.md](steps/ci-budgets/answered.md)): our own crates' line tables cost a Windows runner's cold build 19.7 s of 187, in a job A/B of two rounds, one in each order (`try` 44634824935), with the tiers unchanged. A failing test's panic still names its file and line, which come from the panic's location and not from debug info; only a backtrace loses its line numbers. To have them, set `CARGO_PROFILE_CI_DEBUG=line-tables-only` for the run, or build in `dev` (a plain `cargo test`). A few third-party crates are optimised all the same: `serde_json` and `sha2`, because the decoder replays and the session log's tests spend their time in the first, and every file a tool reads is hashed by the second; and since step 22 `syntect` with the crates it loads its grammars with (`fancy-regex`, `regex-automata`, `regex-syntax`, `flate2`, `miniz_oxide`, `bincode`), because every highlighter test and the golden screens load the grammars once a process, 0.09 s a test unoptimised and 0.02 s optimised on the reference machine. A third-party crate is built once and then served from the cache, so the exception costs a cold run a few seconds and a warm one nothing. Our own crates are not optimised there, though `cineris-llm` at `opt-level = 1` ran its tests four times as fast on the reference machine: it is rebuilt by most pull requests, and the build is where a Windows job's time already goes. Measured again in the fifth tooling step, at four jobs and without incremental builds as CI builds: `cineris-llm` at `opt-level = 1` took 1.6 s more to rebuild on the reference machine for 0.3 s less of its tests' time. **Measured on the runners in the seventh tooling step, and declined by the owner on 2026-11-16:** every job builds every workspace crate, the cache holding only third-party ones, so `cineris-llm` at `opt-level = 1` would cost every pull request 5.7 s of build on Linux and 9.3 s on Windows (11.5 s cold), for 4.3 s less of Windows' integration tier and 1.7 s of its unit tier, summed over the test threads, under 2 s of the run's wall time ([steps/tooling-7/answered.md](steps/tooling-7/answered.md)). **An optimised crate's generic code is not optimised:** a generic function is compiled in the crate that calls it, at that crate's level, so `serde_json::to_vec::<Value>` called from `cineris-session` runs unoptimised whatever `serde_json`'s entry here says. The entries help a crate's own non-generic work (escaping, number formatting, `sha2`'s rounds), not a caller's serialisation.
- Dependency caching with `Swatinem/rust-cache`. The key is made of the job, the OS, the toolchain version, the `CARGO_*` and `RUST*` environment, and a hash of `Cargo.lock`, every `Cargo.toml`, `rust-toolchain.toml` and `.cargo/config.toml`. When that hash has no exact match, the newest cache for the same job, OS and toolchain is restored instead, so **a lockfile change rebuilds the changed crates and what depends on them, not everything**. Only a toolchain bump, or caches that have expired, give a truly cold run.
- Only the main branch writes the cache, so pull requests read a cache that is always consistent and never push each other's out of the repository's 10 GB. A scheduled run of the same workflow, twice a week, keeps the caches from expiring. **`cargo xtask rules` holds every workflow, and every action of `.github/actions/`, to it** (since the eleventh tooling step): `actions/cache/save` only under an `if:` of its own that has `github.ref == 'refs/heads/main'` as one of the conditions it joins with `&&` outside parentheses, and no `||`; never `actions/cache` itself (it saves after any run); rust-cache only with `save-if: "false"` or the main branch; no `cache:` given to a setup action, and no cache action the rule does not know. A workflow that `pull_request_target`, `workflow_run` or `issue_comment` starts writes no cache at all: its runs build a pull request's code with `github.ref` the main branch's. The steps are read however YAML lays out a list, and action names in any case, as GitHub reads them. A cache is code the next run executes.
- On Windows, `CARGO_HOME` and `RUSTUP_HOME` point at a folder on the workspace's disk, not at their default place on the system disk, which is several times slower at unpacking files (measured below).
- **The Windows build and test jobs' temporary directory is on the workspace's disk too** (`TEMP` and `TMP`, since 2026-11-13). A test that needs a directory with no repository above it makes it in the system's temporary directory, which is on `C:` by default. Measured in one job, alternating twice: the 53 integration tests of `cineris-core` took 6.3 and 6.2 s with it on `C:` and 3.3 and 3.5 s on `D:`, and the three tiers together about 8 s less ([steps/11/ci.md](steps/11/ci.md)).
- `cargo-nextest` and `cargo-deny` are installed as prebuilt, checksummed binaries, not compiled.
- A new push to a pull request cancels the run in progress.
- Actions are pinned by commit hash. The workflow token has read-only permissions. No secret is used except the owner's protected terms, which the owner guard reads ("The owner guard", below). `cargo xtask rules` checks all three: the one secret is allowed only as the line `OWNER_TERMS: ${{ secrets.OWNER_TERMS }}`.

**Documentation-only pull requests skip the Rust build and tests.** Each job starts with a step (`.github/actions/changes`) that lists what the pull request changes. If every changed file is under `docs/` or ends in `.md`, and none is under `examples/` (the tests read the examples' files, a README's included, and take the rules their READMEs offer), the job's remaining steps are skipped and the job still reports success under its own name.

- The decision is made inside each job, not in a separate job that the others wait for. All six checks therefore always report, so they can be required by branch protection as they are, with no gate job, and a pull request that does touch code pays about a second for the question.
- **The owner guard runs on every pull request,** documentation-only or not, and whatever the steps before it did.
- **The repo rules still run on a documentation-only pull request, deliberately.** The check for home-directory paths, the check of `docs/dependencies.md`, the check that every link inside the repository leads somewhere and the check of where the step records are exist mostly for documentation, and skipping them on exactly the pull requests that write prose would open the hole they close. This compiles `xtask`, which is five small crates, and nothing else.
- Anything else in the change set (a manifest, a workflow, an asset, a source file) runs everything. So does every push to the main branch and every scheduled or manual run.
- **The generated files under `docs/protocol/` and `docs/configuration/` count as code.** They are checked against the Rust types by tests, so a pull request that touches them runs the tests, wherever else its changes are.

**The workspace's own build, cached** (the Windows test job since the eleventh tooling step, and its build job, `build-windows`, since the Windows split). rust-cache keeps the third-party crates and drops the workspace's own, and building those was most of the Windows job: 141 to 158 s of a 305 s job on a slow runner, every pull request, whatever it changed ([steps/tooling-11/ci.md](steps/tooling-11/ci.md)).

- **The main branch saves its whole build of them**, after the build and its archive succeed (before the split, after its tests passed: the build is as whole either way), with `actions/cache/save` under the key `workspace-build-<OS>-<hash of rust-toolchain.toml>-<commit>`: every file cargo made for a `cineris-*` crate or xtask, by package name (`.fingerprint`, `build`) or target name (`deps`, the uplifted binaries, `examples`), in the `ci` profile and xtask's `dev` build (`WORKSPACE_BUILD` in `ci.yml`). The main branch never restores it: each saved build is one commit's whole build, from rust-cache's third-party crates alone. A path the list misses is not cached, and is built again: a miss costs time, never a stale build.
- **Every other run restores the newest** (`actions/cache/restore`, by the key's prefix) and dates the checkout's files against the commit in the restored key (`.github/scripts/date-the-checkout.sh`). Cargo takes a workspace crate as built when no file it was built from is newer than the build, and a checkout dates every file at the moment of the checkout, so a restored build would be rebuilt whole. The script gives every tracked file a date long before any build (2000-01-01), then every file that differs between that commit and the checkout (added, changed, renamed, deleted: `git diff --name-only --no-renames`) the present. Cargo then rebuilds what changed and what depends on it, and nothing else.
- **What it cannot be sure of, it leaves to a full rebuild:** no restored key, a key without a commit, a commit that cannot be fetched, a diff that fails, or a file on disk that is not the checkout's commit (new, changed or ignored, outside `target/`: `git status --ignored`). In each case the dates stay the checkout's, newer than the build, and every crate is rebuilt as before the cache.
- **What cargo judges other than by dates** is not touched: the compiler (and the toolchain's hash is in the key, so a bump restores nothing), the profile, features, the environment variables a crate reads, and the third-party crates, through `Cargo.lock`. Files outside the checkout that a build reads are the registry's, which never change for a version, and the toolchain's.
- **Each case was tried on a runner** (a scratch branch standing in for the main branch): an ordinary change, a test that fails on purpose, an `include_str!` input, a build script's `rerun-if-changed` input, a deleted and a renamed input, a lockfile-only change, a toolchain change, and a file written into the tree after the checkout. Each rebuilt what it should and failed where it should ([steps/tooling-11/answered.md](steps/tooling-11/answered.md)).
- **Its cost in the cache:** about 350 to 380 MB an entry, one for each push to `main`, on Windows alone. Old entries go by GitHub's own eviction, least recently used first: an entry is read only while it is the newest, so a superseded one is never read again and goes before the third-party caches, which every run reads. Deleting superseded entries from the job would need `actions: write` in a job that builds code, which no job here has.

**nextest configuration** (`.config/nextest.toml`):

- the per-test limits of each tier, from the table under "Test tiers", in every profile;
- profile `ci`: fail fast off, quiet on success, JUnit output for the tier budgets;
- profiles `mutate` and `stress`, for the two commands of "Showing that the tests bite": fail fast off, only failures printed, JUnit output;
- a test group that limits pseudo-terminal and process tests (everything named `sys_…`) to two at a time, so they do not starve each other on a 4-core runner, and in profile `ci` a group of its own for them, `system`, three at a time (on Windows two lanes of one test each, `system-windows-shells` and `system-windows`), for the blocking job's second run, where they run alone ("Each tier is measured under its own load", above); and on Windows a group for tiers 1 and 2, `tiers-windows`, two at a time, for its first run;
- no retries, except tests in a module named `timing_sensitive`, which get one retry and are reported as flaky when it is used;
- profile `fast` for local use: tiers 1 and 2 only.

### Each pull request's binary size

Since step 33's size fix, a fifth job of `ci.yml`, `pr-size`, builds the shipped binary on both systems for a pull request and reports its size before the pull request merges. Before it, only the `main` workflow checked the size, after the merge, which is how step 23's second pull request took the Windows binary over its budget unseen (issue #61).

| | |
|---|---|
| **Jobs** | `pr-size-linux` (`x86_64-unknown-linux-musl`) and `pr-size-windows` (`x86_64-pc-windows-msvc`), the targets of the `main` workflow's size jobs. |
| **What it runs** | `cargo xtask size --check --target TARGET --against-main`: the `dist` build as `xtask` makes it (with the build machine's folders taken out of its paths), the binary's home-path check, the size against its budget, and the difference from the size the `main` workflow last recorded for the target (its newest finished run on the main branch that kept a `size-TARGET` record, read through `gh` with the job's token, which has `actions: read` for that). |
| **What it shows** | A table in the job's summary: bytes, mebibytes, the budget, the room left (or how far over), the crates in the build; then the difference from `main`'s record, signed, with that run and commit, and, when the record is of another commit than the pull request's base (`--base`, which the job passes; since the eighth tooling step), a line saying so: the difference then holds what changed between the two. A record it cannot read is said, and fails nothing. |
| **What grew** | Since the ninth tooling step, when the binary grew by more than 100 KB against `main`'s record, the Linux job names the three crates that grew most, with how much (`It grew by 412.3 KB, more than 100 KB: the crates that grew most: \`cineris_tui\` +301.0 KB, …`), in the log and the summary: it reads the build's symbols (a second build, two to three minutes, only then) against the bytes by crate the `main` workflow's Linux size job now records beside its size (`<target>.crates.json`, from `size --record-crates`: the same second build on every push to `main`, about two minutes more of a job that blocks nothing, whose failure is said and fails nothing). The Windows job says that the Linux job names them: an MSVC binary keeps its symbols in a PDB. A record of `main` from before has none, and the job says so. |
| **When it fails** | Over the budget, with the line `the TARGET binary is N bytes (X MB), over its budget of B MB by M bytes`, in the log and in the summary; and when the binary holds a path under a home folder. A red `pr-size` job is the signal: it blocks nothing. |
| **Not required** | It is not one of the four required checks, and branch protection does not name it. |
| **It waits for the required jobs** | `needs: [lint, test]`: it starts only once all four have passed, so it never takes a runner that one of them is waiting for, and a pull request whose tests fail spends nothing on it. A new push cancels it with the rest of the run. It cannot stop another pull request's required jobs from waiting for a runner that it holds; with more pull requests open than the repository's concurrent runners, one of them may wait as long as a size job takes. |
| **What it skips** | A pull request that cannot change the binary skips the build and reports success at once. That is one whose every changed file is documentation (`docs/**` or `*.md`, the generated files under `docs/protocol/` and `docs/configuration/` included, which the code writes and does not read), a list of breaks (`mutations/**`), the developer commands (`xtask/**`) or a workflow (`.github/workflows/**`). The files of `xtask` that build the shipped binary and judge it count: `shipped.rs` (how it is built), `nonblocking.rs` (`size`, the budgets and the verdict), `steps.rs` (how cargo is run), `workspace.rs` (the workspace and target folders) and `rules/home_paths.rs` (the home-path check). So do the workflows that hold the size jobs, `ci.yml` and `main.yml`, and the `changes` action itself (since the eighth tooling step), so that a change to the job is run on its own pull request. Everything else counts, the crates' tests included. The `changes` action decides it, as its `binary` output. |
| **No cache** | As in the `main` workflow's size jobs: a `dist` build shares nothing with the test jobs' caches, and those keep the repository's 10 GB. |
| **What it costs** | Measured on its first pull request, #63 (run 43822660544): `pr-size-windows` 4 min 29 s and `pr-size-linux` 3 min 16 s, 7 min 45 s of runner time for a pull request that can change the binary, most of it the `dist` build (3 min 19 s and 2 min 19 s); a few seconds for one that cannot. Both started 2 s after the last required job ended ([steps/33/ci.md](steps/33/ci.md)). |

`cargo xtask size --against-main` runs the same locally, through `gh`, and `cargo xtask home-paths FILE…` reads any built file for home paths.

### The TypeScript client

Since step 27, `ci.yml` has a second job that blocks nothing, `ts-client`: a client written in TypeScript, which knows the protocol only from the generated declarations (`docs/protocol/ts/cineris-protocol.d.ts`), runs a turn against `cineris serve --stdio`. It is the step's "Done when" item for the declarations, and kept out of the blocking jobs by rule 2 ([steps/27/answered.md](steps/27/answered.md), "The TypeScript client").

| | |
|---|---|
| **The client** | `crates/cineris-cli/tests/system/ts_client/turn.mts`. Node's own modules, and types imported (`import type`) from the declarations and from nowhere else. It starts the binary as `serve --stdio`, says `initialize` as a `user` client that answers approvals, opens a session, sends a prompt, answers each approval with the choice it was given, and reads `session/event` until the turn's `turn.completed` and a `session.idle` after it. It prints the turn, its outcome, the assistant's text and its answers as one line of JSON, sends `shutdown`, and exits 0 once the server has. A JSON-RPC error, a line it cannot read, a missing field or an end before the turn's exits 1 with the reason. |
| **No build step** | Node 22.18 or later (23.6 or later in the 23 line) runs a `.ts` file as it is, stripping its types. So the client uses only syntax that can be stripped, which the type check holds it to (`erasableSyntaxOnly`). |
| **The type check** | Node checks no types. The job runs `tsc` first, with `ts_client/tsconfig.json`: strict, against the declarations as they are, so a protocol change the client no longer fits fails the job. TypeScript 6.0.3 and `@types/node` 22.18.13 come from npm into the runner's temporary folder ([dependencies.md](dependencies.md), "Tools"). The client also reads each field through one function whose key the compiler checks and whose value it checks when the message comes, so a field renamed or removed fails the run as well. |
| **The test** | `ts_client::sys_a_typescript_client_runs_a_turn`, among `cineris-cli`'s system tests. The scripted model writes a protected file, which asks; the client allows it, and the model answers. The test checks the printed outcome (`completed`), the text, the one answer, and the file written. |
| **Where it runs** | With `CINERIS_TS_CLIENT` set it must, and fails without Node 22.18 or later on `PATH`. In CI without the variable, which is every blocking job, it says it was not run and passes, so they never depend on Node. Elsewhere it runs when such a Node is on `PATH`, and otherwise says it was not run. These rules are a unit test beside it, `ts_client::it_runs_where_it_is_asked_for_and_never_in_ci_unasked`, which the blocking jobs run, and `mutations/step-27-ts.breaks` breaks each of them. |
| **When** | On a pull request that changes `crates/cineris-protocol/`, `crates/cineris-rpc/`, `docs/protocol/`, the client or its test (`crates/cineris-cli/tests/system/ts_client*`), or `ci.yml`; on any other pull request it reports success at once. When the workflow is started by hand (`gh workflow run ci --ref <branch>`, which runs the blocking jobs first). And every night, in the nightly's job of the same name, with no cache. |
| **Not required** | Branch protection does not name it. It starts only after the four required jobs have passed (`needs: [lint, test]`), as `pr-size` does. A failure is read as a failure in the step's reports. |
| **Its cache** | It reads the Linux test job's build cache (`rust-cache` with `shared-key: test`, which makes the key the test job's own), and writes none: it builds only the workspace's crates, for `cineris-cli`'s system tests, in cargo's `ci` profile. |
| **What it costs** | Measured on its first run, started by hand on its branch (`ci` run 44007791420): 68 s for the job, after the required jobs. The toolchain 9.5 s; the cache 4 s, restored in full under the test job's key; Node 6 s, downloaded, since the runner's image does not hold 22.18.0; the type check 3 s (npm 1.5 s, `tsc` 1.7 s); `xtask` built in 13 s and the system tests in 24 s; the test 0.18 s. The blocking jobs gain two tests that start nothing: this one, which says it was not run, and the unit test of where it runs. |
| **Locally** | With Node 22.18 or later on `PATH`, `cargo xtask test -p cineris-cli --test system -E 'test(=ts_client::sys_a_typescript_client_runs_a_turn)'` runs it. The type check, with the two packages in a folder of one's own: `npm install --prefix DIR --no-save typescript@6.0.3 @types/node@22.18.13`, then `DIR/node_modules/.bin/tsc -p crates/cineris-cli/tests/system/ts_client --typeRoots DIR/node_modules/@types`. |

### The owner guard

The owner's name, email and home path never appear in the repository (rule 3). Since 2026-11-13 CI checks for them, in the `lint-linux` job, on every pull request and every push to the main branch: `cargo xtask owner-guard --base REV --head REV`.

- **What it looks at:** every line the change adds, the path of every file it adds or changes, and every commit's message and its author's and committer's names and emails. A line it removes is not looked at. For a pull request the change is what its head adds since it left the base branch; for a push, what the push adds (the head commit alone on a first push). The repository's existing content is not scanned.
- **The terms are a secret, `OWNER_TERMS`,** which only the owner sets, so that they are not in a public repository. Its format: one term a line; white space around a term and empty lines are ignored; case is ignored. A term that contains `@` or `/` (an email, a home path) matches anywhere in the text; any other term (a name, a phrase of several words) matches only as a whole word, with no letter, digit or `_` against either end. The owner sets it with `gh secret set OWNER_TERMS < file`, from a file with one term a line.
- **A term is never printed.** A finding names the file and the line, or the commit and the field, and says that it "matches a protected term"; a file whose path matches is named by its number in the diff. GitHub masks a secret's value in a log as well, which is not relied on.
- **Without the secret it passes, with a warning** in the log and on the job's summary page: a pull request from a fork is not given the repository's secrets, and the secret may not have been set yet. A fork's pull request is therefore not checked until it is merged, when the push to the main branch is.
- **Which terms to list.** Each part of the name as a word of its own, as well as the whole name: a phrase is matched across any white space, line breaks included, but not across two lines of a diff. The account names, the email addresses, and the home directory's folder name as a word: a Windows path such as `C:\Users\<name>` has no `/`, would be matched as written, and would miss its escaped forms (`C:\\Users\\…`, `C:/Users/…`). Text is lowered and nothing more: a name written in decomposed Unicode, or `ß` against `SS`, does not match.
- **What it is not.**
  - **It stops a merge, not a leak.** A pull request's diff and commits are public as soon as it is opened, before the guard has run, and a commit stays reachable by its hash after it is rewritten. What stops a term before it leaves a developer's machine is the guard before a push, below.
  - **It catches an accident, not someone set on leaking the terms.** Anyone who can push a branch to this repository can change the workflow or the guard on that branch, and with it read the secret.
  - **The workflow rule checks the line's form, not its place.** Only `ci.yml`'s guard step is given the secret today, and nothing enforces that.
  - **GitHub masks the secret's lines in the whole job's log,** so a term that appears in another step's output shows as `***`, in a public log; the guard fails on the same change.
- **Who commits on the main branch.** A pull request merged on GitHub by rebase has, as its committer on the main branch, the account that merged it. That is the project's public handle, which rule 3 allows, so it must not be among the terms: the guard looks at committers on a push to the main branch as on a pull request, and would fail every merge.
- **Before a push: the git hook** (since the fourth tooling step, after wave 7, when a fix session copied the owner's name from a message into a commit and a doc, and pushed). `xtask/hooks/pre-push` runs `cargo xtask owner-guard --pre-push`, with what git gives a pre-push hook:
  - **The terms** are read from `~/.config/cineris-dev/owner-terms` (the home folder is `HOME`, or `USERPROFILE` on Windows), or from the file `CINERIS_OWNER_TERMS_FILE` names. One term a line, matched as CI matches `OWNER_TERMS`. The owner keeps the file; nobody else needs one.
  - **What it checks** is every commit the push sends that the remote's branches, as the repository last fetched them, do not have: **each commit's own** added lines and paths (a term one commit adds and a later one takes out is still public in the first), its message, author and committer; the names of the refs it writes and sends; and an annotated tag's text. A merge is read against its first parent, and a root commit against nothing, whatever `log.showRoot` says. A push to a URL, which names no remote, is held only against what git says the URL has: another remote's branches may be private. `git replace` refs are not followed: the push sends the object itself.
  - **The terms are read as CI reads them,** but for a byte-order mark at the start of a line, which some editors write and which is taken off (in CI's guard too); a term that holds a character that cannot be seen (a control character, a zero-width one) refuses the push.
  - **A term is never printed:** a finding names the commit and the field, or the file and the line. Every line it prints is held against the terms first, and one that would show a term (git's own error text can name a path under the home folder) is withheld. A path to the terms file is shown as `~/.config/…` or as "the file `CINERIS_OWNER_TERMS_FILE` names", never spelled out.
  - **Without the default file it warns and lets the push go,** as CI does without the secret, so a contributor without it is not blocked; **unless the repository requires the file** (`git config cineris.ownerTermsRequired true`, which the owner sets when installing the hook), when it refuses: a push from a process with another home folder, or with the file moved, is then not waved through. **When there is a file and the check cannot run** it refuses the push and says why: a file the variable names that is not there, a file that cannot be read, is not UTF-8 or holds no term, a file inside the repository (where a commit could take it), what git gave the hook unreadable, or git failing.
  - **Speed:** a warm run takes 0.07 s on the second machine for a push of nothing new, and stays under a second for a branch of a few dozen commits: one `git config`, then five to seven git commands a ref, whatever the number of commits. The first push from a new working tree builds `xtask` first.
  - **cargo** is found as the one that started the push (`CARGO`), on `PATH`, or in `${CARGO_HOME:-~/.cargo}/bin`. Without it the push is refused.
  - **Installing it,** once, by the owner, for every working tree of the repository: `cp xtask/hooks/pre-push "$(git rev-parse --git-common-dir)/hooks/pre-push"` and `git config cineris.ownerTermsRequired true`. The copy is in the repository's own hooks folder, outside every branch, so a branch cannot turn it off by changing its own copy; `core.hooksPath` pointed at `xtask/hooks` would have run each branch's. **Install it once the change that adds `--pre-push` is on the main branch, and rebase the open branches:** a branch made before it has a guard without `--pre-push`, so its push is refused (closed, not open) until it is rebased.
  - **What it does not stop:** the guard it runs is the branch's own `xtask` and `.cargo/config.toml` alias, as CI's is, so a branch that changes them changes the check. It catches an accident, as CI's guard does, not someone set on pushing a term.
  - **Never push with `--no-verify`:** it skips the hook. [AGENTS.md](../AGENTS.md) says so.
  - **By hand:** `cargo xtask owner-guard --unpushed [REMOTE]` checks what a push of `HEAD` would send.
  - **How it is tested:** with made-up terms, in a repository with a remote that the test makes (`xtask/src/owner_guard/local/tests.rs`): a term in an added line that a later commit removes, a path, an author, a committer, a message, a remote ref's name and a tag, each found and named without the term; a clean push and what the remote has already find nothing; a git that fails is an error. And end to end: a real `git push` through the hook script, with a stand-in cargo in a `CARGO_HOME` of the test's own that runs the guard from the test binary: a push with a term is refused, one without the file warns and goes, a clean one goes.
- **How it is tested:** with made-up terms (`xtask/src/owner_guard.rs`): whole words and case, an occurrence inside one that is not a word, a phrase across white space and a line break, an email and a path anywhere, letters beyond ASCII, an added line against a deleted one, a line that looks like a diff header, a path (an empty file's and one with a tab included), a commit's author, its committer and its message, and a message that holds the separators. And on a real repository the test makes: a range, a first push, a base that is not there (a force push: the head commit alone is checked, and the log says so), a binary file and an empty file. Its breaks are in `mutations/owner-guard.breaks`.

### A CI run's test times

Each test job keeps nextest's JUnit report as an artifact for 14 days, whether the tests passed or not: `junit-linux`, and on Windows one for each part, `junit-windows-tiers` and `junit-windows-system` (`junit-windows`, both parts in one, before the split). It has the time of every test, which the log does not.

```text
gh run download <run id> --name junit-windows-system --dir junit
cargo xtask ci --tiers --from junit/junit.xml --os windows --top 20
```

prints the tier totals of that report against the Windows budgets, each tier split by crate, and its twenty slowest tests in each tier. A system tests' report with its start probe's beside it (`start-probe.xml`, as the artifact keeps them) is read as that part, above the probe's start. A whole run's two parts, read as one, are `ci --tiers --compare <run id> --os windows` and `ci-times`, below.

**Two runs compared.** `cargo xtask ci --tiers --compare NEW [--against OLD] [--os linux|windows|both] [--top N]` prints each tier's time above the process start on both sides, with the change and the budget; each crate's change in each tier; and each tier's tests that moved most, with a test on one side only counted whole. Each side is held against its own start cost, so a slower runner alone moves nothing. `NEW` and `OLD` are each a JUnit report, a run id of the `ci` workflow (or of a `try` run of `test`, below), or `main`, the main branch's latest successful `ci` run that kept the report asked for (a run of a documentation change alone succeeds and keeps none, and is passed over; the command prints which run `main` was, and its commit); `OLD` is `main` when it is not given. A run's report is fetched through `gh` (`gh run download`) once, into `target/xtask-runs/<run>/`, and read from there afterwards, for as long as GitHub lists the same upload: a job run again replaces its report (`overwrite`), and the download is kept with the artifact's id, so the new one is fetched. `--os` picks which test job's report: `linux` or `windows`, each held to its own OS's budgets (tier 3 is 60 s on Linux and 90 s on Windows), and `--os both` (since the seventh tooling step) prints Linux's comparison and then Windows', each under its name, from runs that kept both reports. `main` is one run for both (the latest that kept both reports), so the two OSes are of one commit; a report on disk is of one OS and is refused with `both`; and one OS whose report cannot be read is named in its place, without hiding the other's table, and the command fails after the last. Without `--os` it is the OS the command runs on. A run of the `try` workflow is a source too, since the seventh tooling step: a plain `test` run keeps its report in `try-<os>` (as `target/nextest/ci/junit.xml`, wherever the artifact puts the runner's folders), and it is read from there when the run kept no `junit-<os>` (on Windows, neither the one artifact nor both parts). A `try` A/B run is refused, with the commands that read it: it holds two trees' rounds and no one report. **A side that is no measurement fails the command** (since the review of the seventh tooling step): a test that failed on either side (a timeout reads as a test of its limit's length), or a tier with tests on one side and none on the other (a run that did not happen reads as a tier that got faster), is named under the table, and the command exits as a failure. A `try` run, now a source, fails far more often than `main`'s green `ci` runs.

**A step's tables.** `cargo xtask ci-times RUN [--against RUN|main] [--pr N] [--write FILE]` (with `--pr N` and no `--against`, or `--against main`, "main" is `main`'s successful `ci` run at the commit the pull request's run is based on, where its history meets `main`'s (GitHub's compare), since the ninth tooling step: `main`'s latest run, made after other pull requests merged, moved the pull request's figures by theirs; when that commit has no run that kept its reports, `main`'s latest is taken and said; and a matrix job that was skipped before its matrix was made, whose name is left unexpanded (`pr-size-${{ matrix.name }}`), has no row) fetches a run's jobs and both test jobs' reports through `gh` and prints the two tables the steps' `ci.md` files have, in their form: which pull request and which `main`, each job's time under "Main, `sha`" and "The PR at `sha`" (the jobs a merge waits for first; then the ones that block nothing, the pull request's size jobs and `ts-client`, marked `(blocks nothing)`, so that their four minutes are never read as a five-minute job's: since the eighth tooling step, and a test fails when `ci.yml` has a job of neither kind), and each tier's time above the process start against its budget, a column for each OS and run, with each run's start cost. `--write docs/steps/<NN>/ci.md` puts them in the file's section "On the pull request" (since the fifth tooling step): what it writes goes between two HTML-comment marks, and writing again replaces only that, so a note written after the end mark stays; a section of that name it did not write is refused, and nothing is written. `cargo xtask ci-times --ab TRY-RUN [--write FILE]` does the same for each OS's section "Windows, both trees on one runner" and "Linux, both trees on one runner", as the records name them, from a `try` run made with `-f against=…`: each OS's tiers on both sides, the median of the rounds, the tests counted and the change, read from the run's kept reports, written all or none; and it prints the pull request's CI section, each tier's change and its share of the budget by OS, ready to paste. Sessions used to grep these out of `gh run view --log`. A report with a failed test is named with a warning, and `--write` then writes nothing (since the review of the seventh tooling step): a step's record holds the times of runs whose tests passed.

**What moved, by crate and by test** (since the seventh tooling step). After those tables `ci-times --ab` prints, for each OS, each round's tier times (the spread of one side is the noise a change has to stand out of), the rounds left out and why, and then, tier by tier above the one start cost both sides were held to, the crates that moved (the time on each side to the hundredth of a second and the change; those that moved by 0.05 s or more, the ten that moved most named and the rest counted) and the `--top N` tests that moved most (8 when not given; a test on one side only counts whole). It is the comparison `ci --tiers --compare` makes of two CI runs, from the A/B's medians. `--top 0` prints neither the rounds' table nor the detail. It is printed and not written: a `ci.md` keeps the tiers. **The rounds are read as `ab` read them** (the review of the step): a round in which a side's run broke (`ab` keeps that run's report as `<side>-round-<n>-broke.xml`, which is not a round's), in which a test failed on one side only or not the same tests failed on both, or for which a side kept no report, is left out of both sides' medians with the numbers of the others kept, and named; a round in which the same tests failed on both sides, and nothing else went wrong, is compared, and those tests are left out of every round on both sides (before this, a failed round's partial report was counted as a finished one, and a run that broke after tiers 1 and 2 read as a faster tier 3).

**Several sections in one page** (since the seventh tooling step). A step that is measured again after a review's fixes writes each measurement under a name of its own. `--write FILE --section NAME` makes the tables **one section called NAME**, with marks that carry the name (`<!-- written by cargo xtask ci-times, section "NAME": it replaces this up to the end mark -->` and `<!-- end of what cargo xtask ci-times wrote, section "NAME" -->`): writing it again replaces what is between them and nothing else, whichever other sections the page has, wherever the section has been moved to, and whatever its heading has been edited to. An A/B's two OSes are `###` parts of that one section. `--heading TEXT` is the heading a **new** section is added with, when it should not be its name: a dated one, as an older step's page has (`--section fixes --heading "After the review: the fixes' pull request (#56), 2026-11-16"`). Alone, it is the name as well, which is how a page written before the marks existed gets a section of its own. The rules, which refuse every case that could lose a line:

- A section is **added at the end of the page** under `## <heading>`, never inside another, and never after a heading chosen by the tool; move it by hand and the marks go with it.
- The name and the heading are one line, with no white space at either end, no `-->`, and, for the name, no double quote; a heading is the text and not the `## ` before it.
- A **heading of that text already on the page that the tool did not write** (an older page's section by hand, with no marks) is refused, and so is a section under the plain marks of the unnamed form: choose another `--heading`. Nothing is written, in any of the page's sections, when one is refused.
- Marks that are not one opening and one closing, in that order, are refused, naming what was found; a mark or a heading inside a fenced block is text.
- `--section` and `--heading` say where `--write` puts the tables, and mean nothing without it.
- Without either, nothing changes: the sections `On the pull request` and `<OS>, both trees on one runner`, between the plain marks.

**A page that is not there yet** (since the twelfth tooling step). `--write` makes a missing page, and its folder, rather than failing, and gives it the heading the step records' rule asks for, from where it is: `# After step 24: its CI times` for step 24's `ci.md`, `# After tooling-12: its CI times` for a folder named in words, `# Step 24, pr2 trust: its CI times` for a part's record (`docs/steps/24/<group>/pr2-trust.md`), and `# CI times` anywhere else. A page that is there but empty gets the heading too. It says so (`made … with the heading …`): the words after the colon are for the step to make its own. A page that has no such section gets it at the end, as before.

**A report is missing from a table only when the run made none** (its tests did not run), and the command says so; whether it did is read from the run's list of artifacts. An expired report, a download that fails and a question GitHub does not answer are errors that name the run, never a column left out (since the third tooling step). The upload is the one step these tools add to a blocking job. What they add in all (that step, three static checks and their own tests) is measured in [steps/tooling-w5/ci.md](steps/tooling-w5/ci.md).

**A `try` run's result.** `cargo xtask try-result RUN [--os linux|windows|linux-22.04] [--top N] [--tail N]` (since the seventh tooling step) reads a finished run of the `try` workflow in one step: it lists the run's jobs through `gh run view`, downloads each `try-<os>` artifact once (into `target/xtask-runs/<run>/`, as the other commands do) and prints, for each OS the run had, what the run's command found:

- **an A/B run** (`-f against=…`): each tier on both sides as `ci-times --ab` prints it, each round, the crates and tests that moved, the rounds left out and why, and `ab`'s block of the tests that failed on both sides (its line `N TEST(S) FAILED ON BOTH SIDES` and the indented lines after it), which are not counted as failures of the run;
- **a `test` run:** the tier report against that OS's budgets, whatever the verdict, with its crates and slowest tests;
- **a stress run:** the summary the run printed, rebuilt from its report (the seeds from the log's command line);
- **a `perf` run:** each test and its time, and where its own tables are (`ci-log RUN --grep 'perf_'`);
- and then **every failed test** with the first lines of its message, read from the JUnit reports as `stress` reads them (a test that passed on its retry is not one; one that failed in several rounds is listed once, with where). For a job that failed with **no failed test in a report**, the last `--tail N` lines of its log (30 when not given): from the artifact's own, or, for a job that kept no artifact (cancelled, or ended before the upload), from GitHub's log of the job.

**A run whose own status says it is still running, when every one of its jobs has ended,** is read as finished, and says so (since the ninth tooling step): GitHub can mark a run complete some time after its last job. It ends as a failure, naming what failed (`try run 123 failed: `try-windows` ended as failure; `try-windows`: 2 test(s) failed (…)`), and says `nothing failed` when the run did not. A run that is not finished, is of another workflow, or has no job for `--os` is an error that says so. It reads `try`'s artifacts as they are kept ("A run on the runners without a pull request"); it asks GitHub for the run's jobs and downloads, and nothing else. A `mutate` run is not a `try` command, so there is no mutation summary to read.

**A job's log.** `cargo xtask ci-log RUN|JOB [--job NAME] [--all] [--grep RE] [--context N] [--tail N]` (since the sixth tooling step) prints a CI job's log through GitHub's jobs-logs API (`gh api repos/{owner}/{repo}/actions/jobs/{id}/logs`) with its escape codes taken out. `gh run view --log-failed` prints nothing for the nightly workflow's runs, which this reads. A run's id lists its jobs (`actions/runs/{id}/jobs`) and prints the log of each one that failed or timed out, of the jobs whose names contain `--job NAME` (in any case, given again for more), or of all with `--all`; a job's id prints that job's log. Which an id is, is told by asking GitHub: for a run's jobs first, and for a job's log when GitHub says it knows no such run. `--grep RE` prints the matching lines only, each as `N:line` with `--context N` (2 when not given) lines either side as `N-line` and `--` between groups, as `grep -n -C` does; `--tail N` prints the last N lines of what would be printed. The pattern is read by a small matcher of `xtask`'s own (`xtask/src/pattern.rs`: literals, `.`, classes, `\d \w \s`, groups, `|`, `* + ? {n,m}`, `^ $` and a leading `(?i)`), because a regular-expression crate would be built before every CI job for a search through a log; a pattern it cannot read is an error before GitHub is asked. A log keeps its timestamps. A job's log is kept by GitHub for 90 days; an expired one is the error `gh` gives.

**The same report on a local machine.** `cargo xtask ci --tiers [-p CRATE]… [--top N] [-- NEXTEST ARGUMENT…]` builds and runs the tests as the blocking job does, in cargo's `ci` profile, and prints the same report with ten tests a tier. The profile is the point: a plain local build optimises third-party crates and CI's does not, so a test that spends its time in one (a JSON reader, a regular-expression engine) is several times slower in CI than it looks. Steps 12 and 14 each put a Windows tier over its budget that way. A local machine is still faster than a hosted runner, and Windows about twice as slow as Linux there: read the report for which tests the time is in, and for the tier's share of its budget with room to spare. `--like-runner` adds an estimate of what a runner would say (below).

### What a runner would say, from a local run

`cargo xtask ci --tiers --like-runner [--base DIR] [-p CRATE]…` (since the fifth tooling step) runs the tests as `ci --tiers` does, at a hosted runner's four test threads (`--test-threads 4`), and after the report prints, for each OS, each tier's time on this machine, its estimate on a runner, its estimate on a slow runner, and the budget, with the three crates that make most of it; a tier a slow runner would put over its budget says so. `--from REPORT --like-runner` estimates from a report already made.

- **How it estimates.** Each crate's time in each tier, above the start of its processes, times a factor measured for that crate and tier on each OS. A crate the table does not know (a new one) takes its tier's factor. A slow runner is the measured runner times a measured ratio.
- **Why a factor a crate.** The kind of work decides it. Code of our own that is unoptimised in CI's build is two to four times as slow on a runner: `cineris-llm`'s replays 1.7 times on Linux and 3.2 on Windows, `cineris-tui`'s markdown 2.2 and 3.5. Work that waits on the disk can be slower on the reference machine than on a Linux runner, because its Linux flushes to a virtual disk (`cineris-config`'s integration tests 0.07, `cineris-core`'s 0.69). And Windows-only work is not done on a Linux machine at all: `cineris-tools`' system tests run PowerShell on Windows, 84 times their local time. Whole tiers, as measured in the sixth tooling step with Windows' system tests two at a time: on Linux 1.4, 1.0 and 1.4; on Windows 2.6, 1.8 and 6.4 (the fifth's, with three: 4.4, 1.7 and 12.4 on Windows).
- **Optimised crates and generics.** A crate optimised in CI's build (`[profile.ci.package.*]`) is optimised in its own code only: a generic function of it is compiled in the crate that calls it, at that crate's level. `serde_json::to_vec::<Value>` in `cineris-session`, or `from_slice::<T>` in `cineris-llm`, runs unoptimised in CI's build whatever `serde_json`'s entry says. A session's line of 66 MiB took 400 ms to write on the reference machine in that build. The factors count it as the crate's own work.
- **How it was measured, and how to measure it again.** The same tree (the fifth tooling step's branch at `ef5009d`, the job in its two runs) on the reference machine with `cargo xtask ci --tiers --like-runner`, on an otherwise idle machine, and on both runners as the head of an A/B, `try` with `-f command=test -f against=main -f count=5` (run 43691692866: start 3 ms on Linux, 10 ms on Windows), each test the median of its rounds. Then `cargo xtask ci --tiers --from LOCAL.xml --factors-from TRY-RUN` prints the table, by crate and tier, to paste into `xtask/src/like_runner.rs`; it reads the A/B's head, the tree whose job this `xtask` runs (an A/A run's two sides are one tree). The slow runner's ratio is tier 2 of the same tree in its pull request's CI run (43691685559, start 4 and 10 ms) over the measured runner's: 1.44 on Linux, 1.22 on Windows. **Measured again in the sixth tooling step,** after Windows' system tests went to two at a time: the step's branch at `5e2866b` on the reference machine (otherwise idle, though its disk stalled 54% of the run, which makes its file-heavy crates slow there and their factors low) against the branch's side of `try` run 43718003767 (start 4 ms on Linux, 6 ms on Windows). The fifth step's table read Windows' system tier of that tree at 84 s, where the runners read 39 to 71 s. **The first table,** measured on `main` at `9ddbf67` when the job was one run, read Windows' tier 2 of the split job 51% high (37.4 s against 24.8 s): a table is of one layout of the job, and is measured again when the layout changes.
- **How far off it is.** From the local report it was measured on, it gives the runner's figures by construction. From a second quiet run of the same tree on the reference machine, an hour later, it came within 15% on every tier of both OSes: tier 2 8.8 s against the runner's 9.7 s on Linux (9% low) and 21.1 s against 24.8 s on Windows (15% low); tiers 1 and 3 4 to 8% high. The reference machine itself moved more than that between the two runs: its own tier 2 was 14.2 s the first time and 9.7 s the second, mostly the flushing tests of `cineris-session` and `cineris-config` on its virtual disk. The estimate is as steady as the local disk. On another tree it is right where a change does more of what a crate's tests already did, and wrong where it adds another kind of work: a first Windows-only test, or a test that waits on the disk in a crate whose tests did not. Runners themselves differ by a factor of up to two, and the estimate is of two particular ones. Read it for "is this crate's tier near its budget on a runner", and measure a change with `try -f against=main`, which takes about two minutes for one crate ("Running an experiment on the runners").
- **Process starts** (since the ninth tooling step). What a Windows runner charges most for is a process's start, and a local run does not see it: fitted to a 9 ms runner's system tests, a PowerShell 7 start is 0.33 s there, Windows PowerShell 0.28 s, a test binary in a role 0.15 s, git 0.037 s, `cmd.exe` 0.026 s (two system tests at a time anywhere, before the two lanes: 0.44 s, 0.31 s and 0.054 s), where bash on a Linux machine takes a few milliseconds. Step 17 put 34 shell runs into new tests, 11.7 s of Windows' system tier that only a runner's A/B showed. The places that start a process mark it (`cineris_base::hook::started(program)`: nothing in a build without `test-hooks`): the process tree the shell tool and the sandbox start through, the harness's git, the external editor, a pseudo-terminal, the test kit's git, a child's own child and a test rerun in a process of its own, and `cineris-cli`'s tests' start of the binary. A shell asked its version is a start of its own kind (`hook::probed`: the harness asks bash on a Linux machine, and asks the tests' Windows PowerShell nothing), and the sandbox's launcher, marked by the harness where it wraps a command (the launcher's own process is given none of the test's environment), is counted as the program it becomes. And with `CINERIS_TESTKIT_STARTS` naming a directory each process writes `<binary id>\t<test name>\t<program>` lines to a file of its own there, for the test nextest says it runs (`NEXTEST_BINARY_ID`, `NEXTEST_TEST_NAME`, which a test's children inherit). `--like-runner` sets it, and after the report prints each tier's starts by kind and what the Windows runner would charge for them. The Windows estimate of a test that started processes is then its local time less what its starts cost locally, scaled by its crate's factor for work that is not starting a process (`WORK_FACTORS`), plus each start at the runner's price; a POSIX shell run locally is charged as Windows PowerShell, the shell of the tests that are not about which shell. A test that started nothing is all work.
- **Against a base: `--base DIR`.** The tree at `DIR` (a checkout of `main` beside this one, as `ab` takes it: `git worktree add ../main-base origin/main`) is built and run too, its starts written down, and the two estimates compared: each tier's estimated Windows time on both sides, the change, and the tests whose estimate moved most with their starts. **A tier that grew by more than 3 s is a warning**, with the `try` command that measures it on a runner. A base from before the starts were marked writes none down, and the comparison says so: every start then reads as new.
- **Calibrating it.** The prices of the starts are fitted to a runner's run of a tree with its starts written down (`try -f testkit=starts -f command=test`), each system test's time against its starts of each kind and its crate, by least squares; the work factors with `cargo xtask ci --tiers --from LOCAL.xml --starts-from LOCAL-STARTS --factors-from RUN`, where `RUN` is a runner's run of the tree that was run locally (a plain `test` run, or an A/B's head), `LOCAL.xml` the local report and `LOCAL-STARTS` its starts directory (`target/like-runner/starts`). A crate's work factor is its runner time, less what its local starts would be charged there, over its local time less what they cost locally: the estimate of the local run is then the runner's figure for that crate, by construction, wherever the factor is not 0. It prints `WORK_FACTORS` to paste into `xtask/src/like_runner.rs`; the ninth tooling step measured the system tier's, which is where the starts are ([steps/tooling-9/answered.md](steps/tooling-9/answered.md)), and tiers 1 and 2 keep the factors above.
- **How far off it is, and what it is for.** The absolute figure of the system tier is not the runner's: the tests that run on one system only (bash's and sh's on a Linux machine, the sandbox's on Linux, each Windows shell's own on the runner) start different processes on each, and a crate whose Linux-only tests start more than its Windows run costs (`cineris-core`'s, at a factor of 0) is estimated high. On the tree it was measured on, the system tier read 99.6 s against the runner's 72.8 s. What it is for is **the change a branch makes**, which `--base` gives: a test that adds twelve shell starts is about 3.3 s more of Windows' system tier whatever the rest reads, and is warned of. Measure the figure itself with `try -f against=main`.
- **What it does not see:** a start that no mark covers (a test's own `Command::new("git")`, which most set-up git is, and the commands a test builds and starts itself), Windows-only tests (they do not run on a Linux machine: their crate's factor carries them), and a shell that a test names (a local bash stands for Windows PowerShell, where a test of PowerShell 7 pays more, and is asked its version on the side, about 0.33 s, where a local probe of bash is charged nothing). A crate's work factor is never under 1: one measured at 0 would hide every change to its tests' work.

### A run on the runners without a pull request

The `try` workflow (`.github/workflows/try.yml`) runs one `cargo xtask` command on Linux, Windows or both, for any branch that has been pushed. It blocks nothing and opens no issue.

```text
git push origin <branch>
gh workflow run try --ref <branch> -f command=startup -f profile=dist
gh workflow run try --ref <branch> -f os=windows -f command="test -p cineris-tools shell::"
gh workflow run try --ref <branch> -f command="stress -p cineris-testkit" -f count=200 -f load=6 -f cores=2
```

| Input | What it is |
|---|---|
| `os` | `both` (the default), `linux`, `windows`, or `linux-22.04`: Ubuntu 22.04, whose kernel has Landlock ABI 4, the partial case of the sandbox ([architecture/06-permissions-and-sandbox.md](architecture/06-permissions-and-sandbox.md)). It builds without the cache: the cache's key names the OS and not its version, and build scripts built on the newer image do not run on the older one. |
| `command` | `test [-p CRATE]… [--dependents] [WORD]…`, `stress -p CRATE… [ARGS]`, `startup`, `perf [ARGS]` (step 20's), `perf -p CRATE… [NAME]` (a crate's ignored `perf_` tests, below), `size [OPTION]…` (the binary's size, below), `ci [ARGS]` or `mutate BREAK… [--jobs N]` (the breaks of a list, run on the runner, below); or one of the nightly workflow's jobs, as the nightly runs it and with nothing added: `partial-landlock` (on `os=linux-22.04`, the one runner whose kernel has the partial case), `advisories` or `unused`. Words are separated by spaces and may hold letters, digits and `_-:./=,()\|&!~*+@#`; nothing else is passed on. A crate is `-p x`, `--package x`, `-px` or `--package=x`; every other word of `test` goes to nextest, a test's name or an option (`--run-ignored only`). |
| `profile` | `ci` (the default: the blocking jobs' build), `release` or `dist`. For `size` it is the build's profile: `ci`, the default, is the command's own, the shipped `dist` build. |
| `count` | For `test` or `stress`, the repeats of a stress run (`test` becomes `stress`); for `startup`, its runs. |
| `load`, `cores` | For a stress run: busy loops beside it, and the cores to pin it to ("Running the tests many times over"). |
| `filter` | For `test` or `stress`: nextest filter expressions, separated by `;`, each passed to nextest as one `-E` argument, spaces and all. A test any of them matches is run: `-f filter="test(/shell::/) & !test(=slow); package(cineris-agent)"`. |
| `against` | For `test` in the `ci` profile: a ref to compare the branch with, on the same runner ("Two trees on one runner", below). `count` is then the number of rounds, 5 when not given. For `perf -p CRATE…`: the same, of the crates' `perf_` tests and the numbers they print ("Two trees' performance tests on one runner", below), 3 rounds when `count` is not given. |
| `label` | What an A/B's report calls the `against` side (since the tenth tooling step): `try --dispatch` sets it to the ref as typed when it sends `against` as the hash it resolved, so that the report still says `main` and `-f against=main`. Empty: `against` as it is. Passed to `try` only when set, as `--label`: a `commit` from before it runs only with it empty. |
| `commit` | A full commit hash (40 hexadecimal digits, lower case): the branch's side of the run is that commit and not the head of `--ref`, so that a variant needs no branch of its own ("A variant without a branch", below). Empty: the head of the ref. The commit's own `xtask` runs the command, so the workflow passes it `--commit` and `--capture` only when they are set (`capture=no`): a commit from before an input was added runs as long as that input is left at its default (a commit of the step's own branch from before `capture` failed as "unknown flag" until this). |
| `capture` | `yes` (the default: nextest keeps a passing test's output, as in CI) or `no`: a passing test's output is shown in the log (nextest's `--no-capture`, which runs the tests one at a time), for `test` or `stress`; refused with `against`, whose comparison is of runs made as the job makes them (since the seventh tooling step). |
| `testkit` | `none` (the default), `profile` or `starts`: a test-kit switch for the tests, from a list. `starts` (since the ninth tooling step) sets `CINERIS_TESTKIT_STARTS` to a directory each process a test starts is written down in, uploaded with the log ("What a runner would say, from a local run"); not with `against`. `profile` sets `CINERIS_TESTKIT_PROFILE` and a directory each test's time profile is written to, which the run uploads ("Where a test's time goes"). The value reaches the job through `env:`, as the other inputs do. |

- **The tools a command needs are installed for it:** cargo-deny for `ci` (its `check` ends with it) and `advisories`, cargo-machete for `unused`, at the versions the other workflows pin. The workflow's condition reads the command's words with spaces round them, so `test -p x-ci` installs nothing. `command=ci` failed on a missing cargo-deny before the fourth tooling step.
- **What each command becomes** is decided by `cargo xtask try`, under unit tests, not by the workflow's script: `test` is the blocking job's build and run with the tier report (`ci --tiers --top 20`) in the `ci` profile, and `test --cargo-profile P --profile ci` in another; `startup` builds `cineris` in the profile (release or dist, refused for `ci`) and measures it (`startup --profile P`); `perf` and `ci` take their words as they are, except `perf` with a crate (`-p`, in any of cargo's spellings), which is `test --cargo-profile release --profile perf -p CRATE… --run-ignored only -E 'test(/(^|::)perf_/)' --no-capture --test-threads 1` and any words after the crates: the crates' ignored `perf_` tests in a release build (`--profile dist` for a dist build), one at a time, their output shown, and nothing else, in nextest's `perf` profile, which reports a test as slow at 60 s and stops it at 30 minutes (tier 2's limit of 10 s stopped step 18's tree of 100,000 files, a measurement, while it made its files). A word narrows them (nextest takes a test that matches the word and the filter), so `perf -p cineris-index warm` is the one warm-map test; `--filter`, `--load`, `--cores`, `--count` and `--against` are refused with it. `against` runs pass `--keep-going` to `ab`. **With `against`, `perf -p CRATE…` is** `perf-ab --base ../ab-base --label REF --cargo-profile release -p CRATE… [--count N] [NAME]…` (`dist` with `--profile dist`; a count below 2 is 2; a word that is an option is refused, and so are `--load` and `--cores`; `perf` without `-p` is refused with `against`). **`size [OPTION]…` is** `size [OPTION]… [--profile P] [--target x86_64-unknown-linux-musl]`: the options of `cargo xtask size` as they are, the `profile` input as `--profile` (nothing for `ci`), and on a Linux runner the static target the budgets are of when the words name none (a runner's own default would be the gnu build). It is refused before anything is built when a word is `--profile` (the input chooses), `--at` (a runner's checkout is one commit deep: use the `commit` input, which checks out and measures any commit), a `--target` of another system than the runner's, `--breakdown` on Windows, `--check` or `--against-main` in a profile other than `dist`, or an input that does not go with it (`against`, `count`, `load`, `cores`, `filter`, `capture`).
- **`mutate`** (since the seventh tooling step) is `cargo xtask mutate --minutes 0 BREAK… [--jobs N] [--budget TIME] [--again] [--no-compile-check] [--file PATH]`: the breaks of the lists named ("Breaking the code on purpose") on the runner, to the end in one job, as `--minutes 0` says (its time budget is for a machine that others share, and a runner has no one to share with). `--budget TIME` (since the twelfth tooling step) runs the nearest breaks first and stops starting them when the time is spent ("Breaking the code on purpose"), 50 minutes at most on a runner, so that the breaks under way, and a class begun just before the budget ran out, finish within the run step's own limit of 80 minutes and the results are uploaded; a runner's checkout has no history, so `--changed` is not passed, and every break is of the last class there. It builds its copies of the tree from cold, so the job's time limit is 90 minutes for a `mutate` command (the job limits are listed below, under "The command stops before the job does"). At least one break, list or `--file` must be named: the whole of every list is more than one job. `--check`, `--list`, `--report`, `--tests`, `--fix-in` and `--write` run no break and are refused, and so are `--minutes` (`try` gives it), `--count`, `--filter`, `--load`, `--cores` and `--against`, and a profile other than `ci`. The run's results (`target/mutate/results.jsonl`) and the logs of the runs that named no failed test (`target/mutate/logs/`) are kept in the artifact with the log. The exit code is `mutate`'s: 0 when every break did what its list expects, 1 when one did not.
- **`--dependents`** (since the seventh tooling step): a `test`, `stress` or `perf -p` command that has `--dependents` beside `-p CRATE` is resolved by `try` as `cargo xtask test --dependents -p CRATE` resolves it (the same code, `dependents::expand_in`): the crates whose tests can see a change to the ones named are added, and the job runs `ci --tiers -p A -p B …`. The crates it added are printed, first, in the log and the summary. Before this step the word went on to nextest as a test name's neighbour (`ci --tiers -p cineris-config -- --dependents`), which rejected it after the build, and the job failed with no test run. The workspace is read from the checked-out tree, so with `commit` it is that commit's.
- **A word `try` cannot pass on is refused before anything is built,** with the word and the reason, by `try` at the start of the job. Two kinds. A word of `test`, `stress` or `perf -p` that nextest would not take: an option of `ci --tiers` (`--tiers`, `--top`, `--from`, `--compare`, `--like-runner`, `--factors-from`), what `try` sets itself (`--profile`, `-P`, `--cargo-profile`), an input (`--count`, `--load`, `--cores`, `--filter`, `--against`, `--commit`; a stress run's own `--count`, `--load`, `--cores` and `--first-seed` pass), and the options of `ab` (`--keep-going`, `--base`, `--label`); a word after a `--` of the command's own is the test binaries' and is not looked at. And the command that `try` makes from the rest, read by the parser of that command: `ci`, `stress`, `ab`, `perf`, `mutate` take their arguments as `cargo xtask` takes them, and `startup` its profile, `--runs`, `--budget`, `--check` and `--workspace`, so that `ci --nope` or `stress -p x --stress-count 3` is refused here and not by the command after the build. The words nextest itself takes (its options and filters, a test's name) cannot be checked without running it, and go through.
- **`cargo xtask try --dry-run --command "…" [--profile P] [--count N] [--load N] [--cores K] [--filter F] [--against REF] [--commit SHA] [--os OS] [--testkit T]`** does only that, locally: it reads the inputs as the job does, resolves `--dependents` against this tree, prints the notes and `$ cargo xtask …`, the command the job would run, and exits 1 with the job's own message where the job would refuse. Nothing is built. `--os` and `--testkit` are the workflow's inputs that `xtask` is not given; a dry run checks their values and says what they would change (`partial-landlock` off `linux-22.04`; `testkit=profile` with `against`, which the workflow leaves unset). Use it before dispatching: a refused dispatch costs a job's start and the toolchain's install.
- **`cargo xtask try --dispatch BRANCH --command "…" [the same inputs]`** (since the ninth tooling step) checks the inputs as `--dry-run` does, dispatches the workflow on `BRANCH` with them (`gh workflow run try --ref BRANCH -f …`, each input that is set and none at its default), and prints the run it made: `dispatched: run N`. `gh workflow run` says nothing of the run, and a session that listed the branch's runs to find it took an older one when two were dispatched together. The run is the one GitHub lists on the branch after the dispatch and did not before; the list is asked every two seconds, for at most a minute; two runs dispatched at once are both named.
- **`--against REF` is fixed at the dispatch** (since the tenth tooling step).
  The workflow checks `against` out when the job starts, so a run that sat queued for twenty minutes compared the branch with whatever `REF` was by then.
  `try --dispatch` now asks GitHub for the commit `REF` names (`gh api -H 'Accept: application/vnd.github.sha' repos/{owner}/{repo}/commits/REF`, which answers with the hash alone), passes its full hash as the input and `REF` as typed as the `label` input, and prints `against main is <12 digits> (resolved at dispatch); the report calls it main`.
  GitHub is asked, not the local repository: it is what the workflow's checkout would take, a local `main` may be behind it.
  A full hash is passed as it is, and a `REF` GitHub does not know is refused, naming it, before anything is dispatched.
  The workflow's checkout and `try`'s own check of the input take a hash as they take a name. The A/B's report calls the base by the label (`ab --label main`), so that `try-result` and `ci-times --ab` still write `main`; with no label, by the hash's first twelve digits.
  A dispatch with `--commit` of a commit from before this step fails at its start with "unknown flag" for `--label`, as for any input added since the commit: give that commit's `try` a ref it knows, or no `against`.
  `--dry-run` asks GitHub nothing, and says that a dispatch would resolve the ref.
- **A run that sits queued is named** (since the tenth tooling step).
