  After `try --dispatch` finds its run, it looks at the branch's runs of the workflow, and names each one that has sat `queued` (or `in_progress` with no job started) for longer than 10 minutes, `--queued-warn MIN` to change it (`--queued-limit` is the same): `run <id> (try) has sat queued with no jobs for <M> min: GitHub has not given it a runner. Cancel and dispatch it again (gh run cancel <id>), or keep waiting.`
  The run just dispatched is minutes old; an earlier one that waits that long says the new one may wait as long.
  `try-result RUN` says the same of a run that has not finished, below its line that the run is not finished.
  The rule is a function of the run's status, the time it was made, the time now, the jobs started and the limit, unit-tested at fixed times (`runs::sat_queued`).
- **The checks before a pull request, as one run** (since the twelfth tooling step). `cargo xtask try --final --dispatch BRANCH` dispatches `command=final`, and the run is three jobs, whatever `os` says:

  ```text
  cargo xtask try --final --dry-run
  cargo xtask try --final --dispatch <branch> [--against REF] [--count N] [--commit SHA]
  cargo xtask try-result <run id>
  ```

  | Job | Part | Runs |
  |---|---|---|
  | `try-windows` | `ab` | The Windows A/B, `ab --base ../ab-base --label main --top 20 --keep-going [--count N]` |
  | `try-linux-22.04` | `tests` | The tests on the kernel with Landlock ABI 4, `ci --tiers --top 20` |
  | `try-linux` | `size` | `size --check --against-main --target x86_64-unknown-linux-musl` |

  - `--against` is `main` when not given, and resolved to its commit at the dispatch as for any run. `--count` is the A/B's rounds. `--commit` is every job's commit. `--os`, `--profile`, `--filter`, `--load`, `--cores`, `--capture no`, `--testkit` and words after `final` are refused: each part is fixed.
  - The workflow's matrix for `command=final` gives each job its part, which it passes to `try` as `--part`; `try` makes the part's command, and refuses a part that is not its runner's system (the A/B is Windows'). Only the A/B's job checks the base out, and only the size job is given the token that reads `main`'s size record. A test reads `try.yml` and holds its matrix to `try`'s list of parts.
  - The jobs are named as any `try` job is, so `try-result RUN` reads the three as it reads any run, and `--os` picks one. Its limit is 60 minutes, for the A/B's base built without the cache.
  - A dispatch by hand (`gh workflow run try -f command=final`) must give `-f against=main`: the workflow checks out only what `against` names, and the A/B's job refuses to run with none, saying so, where it would have looked for a base that is not there.
  - `try-result` fails a run whose `size --against-main` could not read `main`'s size (`size` itself says `main not read` and passes): the comparison asked for did not happen.
  - `--dry-run` prints each job's command, checked as that job will check it.
- **`try-result` of a run of several jobs** (since the twelfth tooling step) ends with each job's conclusion and the command it ran, from its log, together. A job that ran no tests and passed (a `size` check, a start-up measurement) shows its log's last lines, `--tail N` of them, where it showed nothing.
- **`commit`:** the workflow checks the given commit out in place of the ref's head (`actions/checkout`'s `ref`, empty for none, which is the ref the run was dispatched on), after a step that reads the input from the environment and refuses anything but 40 lower-case hexadecimal digits; `cargo xtask try` checks the form again and that `HEAD` is that commit. The workflow file stays the one of `--ref`. The checked-out commit's own `xtask` runs the command, so **the commit must contain this `try`**: a commit from before the seventh tooling step has no `--commit`, and the job fails at its start with "unknown flag". A commit that no pushed branch reaches cannot be fetched by hash: push it first.
- **The inputs reach the command as arguments, through the environment, never as text in the script.** `cargo xtask rules` refuses any workflow whose `run:` script has an input or a part of the event pasted into it.
- **The output** goes to the log, its last 80 lines to the run's summary page, and the log and any JUnit report are kept as the artifact `try-linux` or `try-windows`, for 14 days.
- **The command stops before the job does** (since the twelfth tooling step). The job's limit is 45 minutes, 60 for an A/B (any `against`: of the tests, of the performance tests, and a final run), 90 for `mutate` and 240 for a job A/B; the run step has a limit of its own, ten minutes under the job's (35, 50, 80 and 230), so that a command that overruns fails as a step and the steps after it still put its output in the summary and keep the artifact: a job stopped at its own limit is cancelled, and those steps do not run. A `mutate --budget` past what its step allows is refused before anything is built.
- **The cache.** The job's id and environment are the blocking test job's, so a run in the `ci` profile restores the main branch's cache and builds only what the branch changed. It writes no cache. A release or dist build builds from scratch: up to 35 minutes on Windows, the run step's limit within the job's 45.
- **A dispatched workflow must be on the main branch** before it can be started on another; a branch made before it was merged has to be rebased first.
- **It replaced `windows-try`** in the third tooling step, which deleted that workflow: `-f os=windows -f command="test -p CRATE FILTER"` is what it ran, and `-f count=N` its repeats. **A test that passes every repeat of one crate may still fail in the blocking job:** the repeats do not have the rest of the workspace's tests running beside them.

### Running an experiment on the runners

A question a local run cannot answer (does it pass on Windows, on the kernel with Landlock 4, is it faster on a hosted runner) is a `try` run of a pushed branch. A branch whose pull request is not open yet is fine; so is a scratch branch of your own (`<branch>-try`, say), deleted afterwards.

- **One OS, one crate, a few tests:** `gh workflow run try --ref <branch> -f os=windows -f command="test -p cineris-base" -f filter="test(/location/); test(=paths::x)"`. Each `;`-separated filter is one `-E`; a test any of them matches runs. A test's name as a word of `command` works too, but a filter is exact.
- **The partial Landlock:** `-f os=linux-22.04 -f command=partial-landlock` is the nightly's job; `-f os=linux-22.04 -f command="test -p cineris-sandbox"` is the crate's tests there, with `backends` turned on ("A crate alone", below).
- **Many times over:** `-f count=200` makes `test` a stress run; `-f load=6 -f cores=2` squeezes it onto two cores beside six busy loops. A race that shows once in a thousand runs locally often shows in a few hundred there.
- **A change's cost, apart from the runner:** `-f against=main -f count=5` builds both refs and runs them in turn on one runner ("Two trees on one runner", below).
- **A change to how the job builds:** `-f command="test --job --setup-head FILE" -f against=main -f count=2` runs the whole job of both refs each round, each with its own setup, and prints each phase's median ("A whole job two ways on one runner", below).
- **Before opening a pull request:** `cargo xtask try --final --dispatch <branch>` runs the Windows A/B, the tests on Linux 22.04 and the size check in one run (above).
- **A variant without a branch of its own** (since the seventh tooling step). Wave 10 made twelve throwaway branches to try variants, because a force-push to one is refused. Commit each variant on one branch instead, each on top of the last (a commit that reverts the previous variant and makes the next), push normally, and dispatch each commit by its hash:

  ```text
  git commit -am "Variant A"; git push origin <branch>
  gh workflow run try --ref <branch> -f os=windows -f command="test -p cineris-llm" -f against=main -f commit=$(git rev-parse HEAD)
  git commit -am "Revert A, make B"; git push origin <branch>
  gh workflow run try --ref <branch> -f os=windows -f command="test -p cineris-llm" -f against=main -f commit=$(git rev-parse HEAD)
  ```

  Every commit reachable from a pushed branch can be fetched by its hash, so the earlier ones stay runnable after the branch has moved on. `--ref` stays the workflow file, so the workflow of the branch's head runs whatever commit is chosen, and `against` still names the other side. The commit's own `xtask` runs the command, and must contain `try`'s `commit` flag: a variant is a commit on top of this step's. With several dispatched on one branch, note which run id each hash got.
- **Which crates a change reaches:** `-f command="test --dependents -p cineris-config"`, resolved as `cargo xtask test --dependents` resolves it, the crates it added named at the top of the log; `cargo xtask try --dry-run --command "test --dependents -p cineris-config"` shows them locally.
- **Breaks on a runner:** `-f os=windows -f command="mutate tooling-7 --jobs 2"` runs the list's breaks there, for a break whose code is built only on Windows or whose tests behave differently there ("Breaking the code on purpose": `mutate` cannot tell a break in code the build does not compile). `cargo xtask ci-log RUN --grep 'caught|NOT CAUGHT|COULD NOT'` reads the verdicts.
- **Is my change slower on the runner, for one crate:** `gh workflow run try --ref <branch> -f os=windows -f command="test -p <crate>" -f against=main -f count=5`, the A/B of that crate's tests alone, five rounds on one runner. For `cineris-llm` it took 2 min 24 s on Windows and 1 min 20 s on Linux from start to end of the job (run 43667663980), the base's build included. `cargo xtask ci-times --ab RUN` prints its tables, the crates and the tests that moved, and `cargo xtask try-result RUN` everything the run found, for each OS, in one step.
- **A passing test's output, on a runner:** `-f capture=no` passes `--no-capture` to nextest, so what a passing test prints (a `Phases` breakdown, a measurement's own table) is in the log, which `cargo xtask ci-log RUN --grep …` reads from a local machine: `gh workflow run try --ref <branch> -f os=windows -f command="test -p cineris-tui" -f filter="test(/first_frame/)" -f capture=no`. nextest then runs the tests one at a time, so the tier report's times are of a serial run and do not compare with the job's; it is refused with `against`. A count makes it a stress run, each repeat's output shown (since the seventh tooling step, for step 23's start-to-first-frame figures).
- **A crate's performance tests, in release, on a runner:** `gh workflow run try --ref <branch> -f os=windows -f command="perf -p cineris-index"` builds the crate's tests in release and runs only its ignored `perf_` tests, one at a time, with their output (`-f command="perf -p cineris-index warm"` for one of them). It is the command step 18's records give by hand (`test -p cineris-index --run-ignored only --no-capture --test-threads 1` with `-f profile=release`). It first fetches the corpus of real code that a measurement of real code reads, and checks it against its pin ("A tree of real code to measure"). `perf` without `-p` is still step 20's terminal measurement. The tests' own tables are in the log; `cargo xtask ci-log RUN --grep 'perf_'` reads them from a local machine.
- **Two trees' performance tests:** `gh workflow run try --ref <branch> -f os=windows -f command="perf -p cineris-index" -f against=main` builds the crate's `perf_` tests of both refs in release, runs them in turn three times each on one runner, and compares the numbers they print ("Two trees' performance tests on one runner", below). `try-result RUN` shows the comparison.
- **The binary's size on a runner:** `gh workflow run try --ref <branch> -f os=both -f command="size --check --against-main"` builds the shipped binary on both systems as the pull-request job does (the musl target on Linux), `-f profile=release` the thin-LTO build in a fraction of the time (no budget, no `main` to compare with), `-f command="size --breakdown --top 40"` the bytes by crate (Linux only), and `-f commit=<hash>` the size of any commit. The cost is the pull-request job's: three to five minutes a system, and twice that with `--breakdown`.
- **What a run found, in one step:** `cargo xtask try-result RUN` (`--os windows` for one runner) prints, for each OS the run had, the A/B's tables and what moved, or the tiers, or the stress summary; every failed test with its message's first lines; and, for a job that failed with no report, the end of its log. It fails when the run did, naming what failed. Run it once the run has finished. The details are in "A CI run's test times".
- **A run's tiers against another's, both OSes:** `cargo xtask ci --tiers --compare RUN --against main --os both` (a `try` run of `test` is a source as a `ci` run is). For an A/B, `ci-times --ab RUN` or `try-result RUN`.
- **Why a run failed, from a local machine:** `cargo xtask ci-log RUN` prints the logs of the run's failed jobs, the nightly's and a `try` run's included, and `--job windows --grep 'FAIL|panicked|timed out'` the lines that matter ("A CI run's test times").
- **What CI would say, before a pull request:** `-f command=ci` runs `cargo xtask ci`, the static checks and the tests; `-f command="ci lint"` the checks alone.
- **Waiting:** with several runs dispatched at once, look at each: `gh run list --workflow try --branch <branch>`.
- **An A/B locally:** `cargo xtask ab --base DIR`, with `DIR` a checkout of the other ref beside this one ("Two trees on one runner", below): one machine, both trees, without a hosted runner.
- **Reading it:** the log's last 80 lines are on the run's summary page; the whole log and any JUnit report are the artifact `try-<os>`. A `test` in the `ci` profile ends with the tier report, whose first line gives the run's start cost: a test's time means something only beside it.
- **The traps sessions fell into:**
  - **Comparing two runs on two runners.** Hosted Windows runners differ in speed by a factor of two. Two runs are compared only on runners of the same speed, told by the start cost the tier report prints, or in one A/B run.
  - **Git Bash as the shell** (before the third tooling step): `try` ran the command from Git Bash on Windows, whose `mkdir` of `target/try` made a Windows test of a file held with no sharing fail that passed in the blocking job. It now runs from PowerShell, as the blocking job does.
  - **A crate's repeats alone are not the workspace.** A test that passes every repeat of one crate may fail in the blocking job, with other crates' tests beside it.
  - **A crate built alone builds what the crate turns on.** `cineris-sandbox` alone compiled its confinement tests out until the fourth tooling step turned `backends` on for it.
  - **A workflow is the branch's.** A dispatched run uses the workflow file of the ref it runs on, so a change to `try.yml` is tried by dispatching on the branch that has it.

### A tree of real code to measure

Step 18's review found its generated tree about ten times lighter than real code, and measured real code from whatever the cargo registry of the machine held: a local registry and a hosted runner's are different crates, so a local number and a runner's for "real code" did not compare. The corpus is one pinned set of real Rust, the same bytes on every machine:

- **What it is:** 20,162 `.rs` files of 670 crates from crates.io (324 MB; the median file is 4.5 KB, six are over 1 MB, 551 have CRLF line endings, one a byte-order mark; the longest path is 119 characters, which fits Windows' limit with the places it is kept at). The list is `corpus/dependencies.toml`: tokio, syn, wasmtime, boa, reqwest, tantivy and thirty more, which between them need the 670. It is what a harness meets, tests, generated tables and bindings included.
- **How it is pinned,** in three layers. `corpus/Cargo.lock` holds the version and SHA-256 checksum of every crate, and cargo checks each download against it. `cargo xtask corpus fetch` has cargo fetch them (`cargo fetch --locked`, in a manifest it makes for the purpose, in a folder outside the repository: no workspace, no code, nothing built), and copies every `.rs` file out of cargo's registry, byte for byte, into a folder named for its hash, each file dated 1 January 2021 so that a fresh fetch is not "modified a moment ago" to an index that reads it. `corpus/PIN` holds the number of files, the bytes and a **hash of the paths, lengths and contents** (64-bit FNV-1a, the hash `mutate` keeps its results by: it is here to see that the folder is what was pinned, which a single changed byte always breaks, and not to resist anyone, which cargo's SHA-256 does). The folder is checked against the pin **after every fetch, before it is put in place**, and **every time a test asks for it** (`cineris_testkit::corpus::real_code`). A folder that is not whole is removed and made again by `fetch`, and refused by a test with the command to run.
- **Line endings** are whatever the crates were published with. Nothing in the path touches them: cargo's registry sources are unpacked from the crate's tarball, not checked out of git, so `autocrlf` and `.gitattributes` do not apply; the corpus is never in the repository; and the copy reads and writes bytes. A test holds that a changed line ending is refused ("a line ending that changed").
- **Where it is kept:** outside the repository and outside `target`, so that `cargo clean` and a new working tree leave it be and one fetch serves every working tree: `$XDG_CACHE_HOME/cineris-dev/corpus/<hash>` (`~/.cache/…` when it is not set) on Linux, `%LOCALAPPDATA%\cineris-dev\corpus\<hash>` on Windows. `CINERIS_CORPUS_DIR` names another folder to keep them in: `try` sets it to a folder in the runner's temporary directory, on the disk the workspace is on, so that a measured tree can hard-link the files (on Windows the user's cache folder is on the system disk). **Its size:** 324 MB of content, 373 MB of disk with 4 KB blocks, in 4,769 folders. A corpus of an earlier pin is not removed by a new one: delete its folder. A folder `.work-<process id>` beside them is a fetch that was killed: delete it.
- **How long it takes,** measured on the reference machine and on the second machine, the dev build of `xtask` (tooling 7): a **cold fetch** with an empty cargo registry and an empty cache, 6.2 s on the reference machine (5.1 s of it cargo downloading the 670 crates, 0.6 s copying, 0.6 s hashing) and, on the second machine with the crates already in its registry, 4.9 s (0.6 s of cargo, 1.5 s copying, 2.8 s hashing, the hash loop being unoptimised there); a fetch on the reference machine while its disk was stalled by other builds, 14.9 s (6.6 s copying). A **warm check**, which reads and hashes the whole corpus: 0.5 s on the reference machine and 2.7 s on the second machine in `xtask`'s dev build, and 0.9 s (884 ms) in a release build of the perf test on the reference machine, with the disk busy. The same bytes came out on both machines: hash `285100c9ac0c85f0`. What a hosted runner takes is in the `try` run's log (`cargo fetch:`, `copied …`, `hashed in …`, `fetched in …` lines), which step records quote.
- **How a test asks for it:** `cineris_testkit::corpus::real_code()` returns the folder after checking it, and fails with the command to run when it is not there or not whole. Nothing blocking asks for it: a `perf_` test is ignored in the blocking checks. `cargo xtask corpus path` prints the folder, for a shell (`CINERIS_PERF_SOURCE=$(cargo xtask corpus path)`); `corpus check` verifies and prints its size and hash. `cineris-index`'s `perf_a_tree_of_real_code` reads it and, since the eighth tooling step, `cineris-tools`' `perf_search_real_code` and `perf_diffing_a_reread_of_real_code` and `cineris-llm`'s `perf_decoding_a_large_tool_call_of_real_code` read it too. `CINERIS_PERF_SOURCE` names another directory for the index's test to read in its place (a measurement of that, not of the corpus: do not compare the two).
- **How `try` has it:** `perf -p CRATE` runs `corpus fetch` before it builds anything, on Linux and Windows, and so does it with `against` ("Two trees' performance tests on one runner"). It is fetched each time and not cached by the workflow: a cold fetch costs seconds where a cache of twenty thousand files would need a key, a pinned action and a restore, and the time is in the run's log (`fetched in … s`). No other `try` command fetches it.
- **To change it:** edit `corpus/dependencies.toml`, run `cargo xtask corpus pin` (`--update` to take the newest versions the list allows, which changes more than the list does), and commit `corpus/`. `pin` writes `Cargo.lock` and `PIN` and keeps the new corpus where it is looked for. A figure measured on the corpus is of its hash: a new pin is a new baseline, and the page that holds the figure says which hash it was.
- **What it is not:** the crates are read as test input, kept outside the repository and never committed, built or shipped. They are under their own licences (the corpus's are almost all MIT or Apache-2.0, some of them with Unicode-3.0, BSD, Zlib or MPL-2.0 terms, and a few offer GPL-2.0 or LGPL beside a permissive one); nothing is copied into the harness. `cargo deny` does not read it: it is not a dependency of anything.
- **What was weighed instead.** A tarball as a release asset: a copy of third-party source on our account, a step to upload and refresh it, and bytes that cargo's checksums do not vouch for. A git repository at a commit: a clone with its history, and the checkout's `autocrlf` setting, which differs between a developer's machine and a hosted Windows runner, changing the bytes it was to fix. The corpus in the repository: 324 MB of other people's code in every clone. The crates' own checksums, cargo's fetch and a hash of the copy need no host, no account and no new dependency.

### Two trees on one runner

Hosted Windows runners differ in speed by a factor of two, so a branch's run and the main branch's land on runners that cannot be compared, and a change's cost to a tier is lost in the difference. `cargo xtask ab --base DIR [--label NAME] [--count N] [-p CRATE]… [-- NEXTEST ARGS]` takes the runner out of it:

- **Both trees are built once,** this one and the one at `DIR`, as the blocking test job builds them, each in a build directory of its own (`<tree>/target`), so that building one never rebuilds the other. **Each is run as its own job runs it:** in two runs, or in one for a tree from before the split ("Each tier is measured under its own load", above).
- **Then they are run in turn on the same machine,** `N` rounds (5 when not given), the first round base then head, the next head then base, so that whatever drifts during the job falls on both alike.
- **Each side's wall time, round by round** (since the tenth tooling step), from the start of the side's job to its end, both runs of it: a table of round, which side ran first, the base's and the head's seconds, and each side's median over the rounds it passed (a side that failed is marked `!` and left out of its median).
  A round in which both sides were slow is the runner's moment; a side slow in every round is its tree.
  It is printed after the rounds, put in the job's summary, and kept in `target/ab/wall-times.json`, which the `try` workflow uploads and `try-result` prints for each OS.
  A run from before this step kept no such file, and `try-result` prints none for it.
- **Each test's time is the median of its runs,** and the two sides are compared as `ci --tiers --compare` compares two CI runs, by tier above the start of the processes, by crate, and the tests that moved most, **with one start cost for both:** the 1st percentile of both sides' tests together. They ran on one machine, and each side's own would differ by the noise of a millisecond, which over the unit tier's thousand tests is a second: the first A/A run of a tree against itself, so held, showed its unit tier 0.8 s slower than itself. Each round's tier totals are printed first: their spread on one side is the noise a difference has to stand out of.
- **On a hosted runner,** `try` runs it with `-f against=REF`: the workflow checks `REF` out and moves it beside the branch's checkout, to `../ab-base` (inside it, the branch's `.cargo/config.toml` would apply to the base's build too, and `ab` refuses a base inside its tree), and `cargo xtask try` turns `test` into `ab --base ../ab-base`. The base builds without the cache, which takes a few minutes more on Windows. Neither checkout keeps the job's token: the base may be any ref's code. Each round's two reports are kept in the head's `target/ab/`, and uploaded with the run's log.
- **A test that fails ends the run,** after both builds, unless `--keep-going` is given (since the sixth tooling step; `try` gives it for every `against` run). With it, a round in which a side's run fails (a test failed, or the run could not be made) is recorded as failed for that side and the run goes on to the next round. The comparison is of the rounds **both** sides finished: a round in which either side failed is left out of both sides' medians and of the rounds table, which keeps the other rounds' numbers. After the comparison the failed rounds are listed, each with the tests its report names (or what the run said when it wrote no report), and the command exits as a failure, so that the job is red and the measurement is still in its summary; with no round finished on both sides it says so and compares nothing. A failed round's report is kept in `target/ab/` with the others. Without `--keep-going` nothing changes: the first failing run is the error.
- **A test that failed on both sides is skipped, not a failure** (since the seventh tooling step, with `--keep-going`). Wave 10 lost three whole A/B runs, twenty minutes each on Windows, to one test that failed on both trees. Now a round in which both sides' runs failed **only by tests failing, and the same tests on both sides**, is compared, without those tests. Such a test is left out of both sides' medians and of the comparison **in every round**, not only the one it failed in: a time taken in a round where it passed is no more a measurement than one taken where it failed. The summary, at the head of the table in the job summary and again at the end of the log, says `N TEST(S) FAILED ON BOTH SIDES`, and names each test with the rounds it failed in and the first line of each side's failure message; the command exits 0 when that is all that went wrong. What it will not take for one:
  - **A test that failed on one side only,** in any round: the other side passed it, did not run it (a filter, a test the tree does not have) or has no report. Until the ninth tooling step that round was failed for that side, and a test that failed every round on one side (a Windows-only test's bug) lost the whole comparison. **Since then the round is compared without it**, as a test both sides failed is: it is left out of every round on both sides, listed under `N TEST(S) FAILED ON ONE SIDE ONLY` with the rounds it failed in and each side's message (`(passed, or not run)` for the side it did not fail on), and **the command still exits as a failure**, naming it (`1 test(s) failed on one side only, and the comparison above is of the others: …`). A test that failed on both sides of one round and one side of another is one-sided. A test that is missing on one side is never read as failed there. `ci-times --ab` and `try-result` read the rounds by the same rule, and name such a test as a failure of the run.
  - **A run that failed otherwise too:** a run counts as failing by its tests only when nextest's exit code says that (100) **and** its report names a failed test, in each of the job's two runs that failed. A build that broke, a run killed by a signal, a report that lists nothing, a failure no report names, or two runs of which one failed by its tests and the other broke, is not that, and the round is failed.
  - **A report that could not be read or put together.**
  - **The job's two runs.** A job is two runs (tiers 1 and 2, then the system tests), and with `--keep-going` the second is made even when the first failed, so that a failing tier-2 test does not leave tier 3 unmeasured; their reports are brought together after. Without it the first failing run still ends the job, as it always did.
  Tested on JUnit reports written for the purpose (`xtask/src/ab.rs`): a test failing on both sides in some rounds, on one side only, missing from one side, with a run that broke too, with no report, and with two failing tests of which only one is shared; the job's two runs with the first failing; and nextest's exit codes. Its breaks are in `mutations/tooling-7.breaks`.

```text
gh workflow run try --ref <branch> -f os=windows -f command=test -f against=main -f count=5
```

What it showed when it was built is in [steps/tooling-3/ci.md](steps/tooling-3/ci.md).

### A whole job two ways on one runner

`ab` builds each tree once and times the tests. A change to how the job builds (a cache, a linker, a profile, a variable in the environment) is in the build, which `ab` does not time. `cargo xtask ab --job` (since the twelfth tooling step) makes every round a whole job on each side, each phase timed:

```text
cargo xtask ab --job --base ../other-checkout --count 4 --setup-head scripts/no-debug.sh
gh workflow run try --ref <branch> -f os=windows -f command="test --job --setup-head scripts/no-debug.sh" -f against=main -f count=2
```

- **Each side's round:** its build of the tests is removed (`target/ci` and `target/tmp`): in every round, or under `--keep-build` in the first two alone, one in each order, so that both sides start cold whatever their build directories held (on a runner the cache restores `main`'s build into the head's tree alone, and a first round that kept it compared a warm head with a cold base). Under `--keep-build` (which needs `--count 4` or more) a cold round's phases are named apart from a warm one's (`build (cold)`, `build (warm)`, and so for the runs, the tiers and `the job`), so that a cold build and a warm one are never in one median or one pair: with one median over both, two rounds gave the mean of one cold pair and one warm one, and four rounds dropped the cold pair altogether; its setup script runs, if it has one; its tests are built as the blocking job builds them; and the job's runs of them are made, tiers 1 and 2 then tier 3. Each is timed, and so is each tier's tests' time summed above their processes' start, from the report. `the job` is the setup, the build and the runs together. The rounds alternate the sides' order, as `ab`'s do, and **their number is even** (`--count N`, 2 to 20, 2 when not given), so that each side goes first as often as the other: three rounds put the base first twice. **Before the first round, untimed, each tree is warmed** (`cargo fetch --locked`, which unpacks the crates' sources, and `cargo nextest --version`), so that what a first run pays for once (the registry's sources unpacked, the toolchain and nextest read into the disk cache) falls on neither side's timed round; what is still the first side's in a round (its own build's files read cold) is shared out by the order.
- **Each way has its own setup.** `--setup-base FILE` and `--setup-head FILE` name scripts in this tree (the head's commit: both setups are part of what was committed), each run in its side's tree by `bash` (Git's own on Windows; a `.ps1` by `pwsh`), with `CINERIS_AB_SIDE` (`base` or `head`) and `CINERIS_AB_ROUND`. A script may write `KEY=VALUE` lines to the file `$CINERIS_AB_ENV`, which are set for that side's build and tests: `echo CARGO_PROFILE_CI_DEBUG=false >> "$CINERIS_AB_ENV"` builds one side without debug info. The base may be the same commit as the head, checked out beside it, for the same code two ways.
- **A failed round is recorded, and the run goes on.** A failed clean, setup or build ends that side's round, and is kept with the time it took and what it said. A failed run of the tests does not: the job's next run is still made and timed, as the blocking job makes it, and the tiers' sums and `the job` of that round count as failed. Either way the other side, and the next rounds, run as planned. A throwaway harness for this measurement in wave 14 failed four times, at about 45 minutes a round. Everything measured is written to `target/ab/job-ab.json` after every side, so that a run that is stopped keeps what it made, and each side's report to `target/ab/<side>-job-<round>.xml`.
- **`--minutes M`** starts no round that the longest round so far would carry past `M` minutes from the start, and says where it stopped. `try` gives it `--minutes 200`, within the run step's own limit of 230 minutes for a job A/B and the job's of 240.
- **What it prints:** a line for each side's round, its phases with a failed one marked `!`; then each phase's median on each side over the rounds it passed in, with how many that was (`110.0 (3/4)`), and **its change paired by round**: the median of head minus base over the rounds the phase passed in on both sides, with how many such rounds and in how many the base went first (`-20.0 (2, 1 base first)`). Each side's own median drops its own failed rounds, so the two medians can be of different rounds, or of rounds one side always began; the paired change cannot. A change whose pairs are not half base-first is marked `*`: it is partly the order's. A run that `--minutes` stopped before it had a whole round in each order fails, and `try-result` names it: every change it printed is of one order. A failed phase is left out of its median and of the paired change. The command fails at the end when a side's round failed, after the medians. `try-result RUN` prints the same from the kept record, and each failed test with its message.
- **On a runner:** `test --job` with `against` is `ab --job --base ../ab-base --label REF --minutes 200 [--count N] [--setup-base FILE] [--setup-head FILE] [--keep-build] [-p CRATE]…`. `--job` and its options without `against` are refused before anything is built.
- **Why `ab --job` and not a `try` command of its own:** it is the same two trees, order, label and base checkout as `ab`, and the same artifact that `try-result` reads, and it runs locally as well as on a runner, where a `try`-only command could be tried nowhere but on a runner.
- Tested as pure functions (`xtask/src/job_ab.rs`): the request, each phase's median over the rounds it passed in, the paired change and its order, a phase no round passed, a failed side shown and failing the run, the rule that starts a round, the record kept and read back, the setup's environment file, and the reports' names. A side's round and the rounds are drivers over a trait of phases and a clock, tested with fake phases: each phase timed, a failed build ending the round, a failed run of the tests not ending it, a failed round recorded and the rounds going on in turn, and the stop before a round past the minutes.

### Two trees' performance tests on one runner

`cargo xtask ab` takes the runner out of a comparison of tests' times. `cargo xtask perf-ab --base DIR [--label NAME] [--count N] [--cargo-profile release|dist] -p CRATE… [NAME]…` (since the eighth tooling step) does the same for the numbers a performance test prints. A figure measured on one hosted runner and the main branch's figure, measured on another, do not compare: runners differ by a factor of two, and the two runs land on different ones.

```text
gh workflow run try --ref <branch> -f os=windows -f command="perf -p cineris-index" -f against=main
gh workflow run try --ref <branch> -f command="perf -p cineris-tools -p cineris-llm real_code" -f against=main -f count=5
cargo xtask try-result RUN
```

- **What it does.** Both trees' ignored `perf_` tests of the crates named are built once each, in a release build as `perf -p CRATE` builds them (`--cargo-profile dist` for a dist build), each in a build directory of its own. Then they are run in turn on this machine, `--count` rounds (3 when not given, 3 to 20: with two, two runs of one tree read `apart` a third of the time, and with three a tenth, so use 5 for a decision): the first round the base then the head, the next the head then the base, so that what drifts during the job falls on both alike. Each run is one `cargo nextest run` in the `perf` profile, one test at a time with its output shown, and a word narrows the tests as it does for `perf -p`. A run that fails ends the command, with its output in the log: a perf test fails by an assertion on what it measured or by a corpus that is not there, and neither is a number to average.
- **How a test's numbers are read.** A test that wants its numbers compared prints each with `cineris_testkit::numbers::report("subject.what", value, "unit")`: the line `perf: subject.what 28.9 ms`. The name is one word of letters, digits and `._-`, starting with what is measured (`search.literal_rare_real_code`); the unit is one word and is only shown. Any other line the test prints is for a person and is not read. A name printed twice in a run is the median of its values, and a name with two units is two measurements. The line is the whole convention: the existing tables and what they measure are as they were. **The tests that print them** (a test that prints none is unaffected, and an A/B shows nothing of it): `cineris-index`'s `perf_a_tree_of_real_code` (`index.cold_index_real_code`, `index.warm_pass_and_map_real_code.nothing_changed` and `.one_file_changed`); `cineris-tools`' `perf_search_real_code` (`search.literal_rare_real_code`, `.literal_common_`, `.regex_`, `.no_match_`) and `perf_diffing_a_reread_of_real_code` (`diff.reread_changed_median_real_code`, `.reread_unchanged_median_`, `.reread_changed_95th_`); `cineris-llm`'s `perf_decoding_a_long_answer_and_a_large_tool_call` (`llm.decode_long_answer_and_large_call`) and `perf_decoding_a_large_tool_call_of_real_code` (`llm.decode_large_call_real_code`). The tests of real code read the pinned corpus ("A tree of real code to measure"), which `try` fetches first for `perf -p` with or without `against`; both trees must pin the same corpus, or the base's test fails with the command to run.
- **What the report says.** For each measurement: each side's median over the rounds with its least and most beside it, the head's median less the base's, with its percentage, and whether the two sides' ranges are `apart` (they do not overlap: the difference is more than this run's own spread) or `within noise` (they do, and the run cannot tell the difference from the machine's). A measurement that only one side printed (a new test, or one the head dropped) says so. Nothing judges which way is better: a time is better lower and a rate higher, and the unit is shown for the reader. The table is in the log, in the job's summary and, as JSON (`target/perf-ab/results.json`), in the run's artifact, from which `cargo xtask try-result RUN` prints it for each OS.
- **What it cannot tell.** With three rounds a range is two or three points: `apart` is a sign and not a test, and a difference near the spread wants more rounds (`count`). The very first run, the base's, reads files the machine may not have cached (the head's first run finds them in the cache, if the tests read the same files): that is one round of three, which each side's median discards. It compares the numbers a test prints, not the time of the test, and not a test that prints none.
- **What it costs.** Two release builds of the crates named, from scratch (the runner's cache is for the `ci` profile), and then the tests' own time six times over for three rounds; the job's limit is 60 minutes for it. A crate whose tests take a minute costs six minutes of runs.
- **How it is tested.** `xtask`'s unit tests (`xtask/src/perf_ab.rs`): the request and what it refuses; the nextest commands that build and run; the numbers read out of an output with noise, carriage returns, broken lines, a repeated name and two units; the order of the sides over the rounds with a runner that fails in the third run; a measurement on one side only; `apart` and `within noise` at the edges (overlap, a faster head, one run a side, a base of nothing); the report's row; the record read back; and in `try_run.rs`, `perf -p … --against` become `perf-ab` with their words, rounds, profile and refusals, and fetch the corpus. In `try_result.rs`, a run's artifact with a record prints the comparison and not the last run's test times. The test kit's `numbers` module has unit tests of the line, and the same line is held on `xtask`'s side. The workflow is not run by a pull request that changes only it ("A run on the runners without a pull request"): the first run of `perf-ab` on a runner is the one dispatched on the branch that adds it. Its breaks are in `mutations/tooling-8-measure.breaks`.

### The binary's size by crate and at a commit

`cargo xtask size` (since the eighth tooling step) takes more than it did:

```text
cargo xtask size [--check] [--target TRIPLE] [--against-main]
                 [--profile release|dist] [--breakdown [--top N]] [--at COMMIT]
```

- **`--profile release`** builds the thin-LTO `release` profile instead of the shipped `dist`, stripped as `dist` is (`release` is not stripped by default, and its size would be mostly its symbol table's: `size` sets `CARGO_PROFILE_RELEASE_STRIP`): a fraction of the build time, and not the shipped size (fat LTO is about 7% smaller), so it has no budget and no `main` to compare with: `--check` and `--against-main` are refused with it. For a look at what a change does to the size when a minute matters more than the byte.
- **`--at COMMIT`** builds the size of another commit: the commit's tree is read from this repository's history with `git archive`, extracted with `tar -m` into `target/size-at/<hash>` and built there with a build directory of its own (the build machine's folders are taken out of its paths as for any `size`), measured and removed. `-m` makes every file as new as the extraction: `git archive` dates each file as its commit, and a build directory that holds artifacts newer than that takes the file for unchanged. A copy with no `.git` cannot do it; neither can a runner's checkout, which is one commit deep (`try` refuses `--at`: its `commit` input checks out any commit and measures it). The record `size` keeps for the workflow is not written for a commit but this tree's.
- **`--breakdown`** prints the binary's bytes by crate. It builds the same code a second time with the profile's `strip` turned off, in a build directory of its own (`target/size-breakdown`, so neither build rebuilds the other; the build costs as much again), reads the symbols with `nm --print-size --demangle` (binutils: on the runners and on a Linux machine) and adds each symbol's size to the crate its name starts with: the type's crate for a method of a trait (`<serde_json::Value as Debug>::fmt` is `serde_json`'s), the generic's own for generic code (`core::ptr::drop_in_place<cineris_core::X>` is the standard library's), and the standard library's `core`, `alloc` and `std` as one row. Symbols of no Rust crate are one row (C, assembly and libc), and data with no name of its own (strings, tables) another. The last row is what is in no symbol: the headers, the unwind tables and padding, as the stripped binary's size less the symbols'. The shares are of the stripped binary, the one that is measured. `--top N` lists the N largest crates (25 when not given) and the rest in one row. The table is printed and added to the job's summary, after the verdict, so that a failure of the breakdown does not hide it.
- **What it cannot tell.** A symbol is the code that is left: what the linker folded into another or threw away is not in it, and two names at one address count once. A generic function is the crate that owns the generic, as for `cargo bloat`, so `std` holds code instantiated for our types. **On Windows it does nothing:** an MSVC binary keeps its symbols in a PDB, which `nm` does not read, and the command says so before it builds anything (`try` refuses it too). The same code is about 1.15 to 1.2 times the Linux binary's size on Windows, so the Linux breakdown is the one to read. On macOS and other targets it is refused for the same reason.
- **How it is tested.** `xtask`'s unit tests (`xtask/src/nonblocking.rs`): the request and its refusals (a profile that has no budget, a commit's name with an option, a space or a revision's syntax in it); a runner's target and the breakdown on Windows; the report of a profile that is not shipped; `nm`'s output read (a symbol in `bss`, with no size or at an address already seen is left out); the crate of each kind of name (a function, a method of a trait, a reference, a slice, a primitive's impl, the standard library, C, an anonymous constant); the sums and their order; the table's rows, shares and remainder; and a real repository that the test makes, with a commit dated in 2001, extracted so that its files are dated now. The builds themselves are not run by the tests: `size` was run on the reference machine, and its first run on the runners is the dispatch of this step's branch. Its breaks are in `mutations/tooling-8-measure.breaks`.

### This branch on top of others

A clean rebase can hide a conflict of meaning: in wave 6 a pull request added a trait method with a default body, a parallel one wrapped the trait without forwarding it, and each passed alone. `cargo xtask combine PR…` checks a branch on top of the pull requests that will merge before it, before either merges:

- **It makes a clone** of the repository under the build directory (`target/combine/tree`) with `git clone --shared`, which reads the repository's objects and writes nothing to it: no branch, no ref, no linked-working-tree entry, no `FETCH_HEAD`. The checkout's working tree, its branch and its repository are not touched, and a test holds that, reading the repository's branch, head, refs, working trees and status before and after.
- **It puts together** the main branch as GitHub has it now, then each pull request's commits since it left the main branch, in the order named, then this branch's commits since it left the main branch: what a rebase merge of each would make. Uncommitted changes are not in it, and it says so.
- **A commit that does not apply stops it,** naming the pull request, the commit and the files in conflict. Otherwise it runs `cargo xtask ci` in the clone, with a build directory of its own (`target/combine/target`), kept between runs so that the next builds only what changed.
- **It cleans up after itself:** the clone is removed when the run ends, pass or fail, and any clone a killed run left is removed when the next one starts. `--keep` leaves it to be looked at; `--no-ci` combines and runs nothing.
- **It asks `gh` whether each pull request is open,** and says so when `gh` cannot answer.
- **In a tree with no `.git`,** the branch is taken from GitHub: push it, then `cargo xtask combine --repo https://github.com/<owner>/<repo> --head <branch> 41 42`.
- **How it is tested:** against a small repository a test makes, standing for GitHub, with two pull requests as `refs/pull/N/head`: two that change different lines combine, and the check sees all three layers; two that change the same line stop at the second, naming it, its commit and the file, and the check is not run; a checkout in a linked working tree, whose `.git` is a file, is combined and its branch, head, refs, status, stashes and the list of working trees are the same after; and a pushed branch is taken from the remote. Each test starts git about forty times: under 0.1 s on Linux, and seconds on a Windows runner, where they are ignored in the blocking checks and the nightly workflow's Windows job runs them (`cargo test -p xtask -- --ignored combine::tests::sys_`).
- **A clone kept with `--keep` reads the repository's objects in place** (`--shared`): a `git gc --prune` in the repository can take objects it needs. Look at it and remove it; do not keep it for long.

### A branch's history without an interactive rebase

`cargo xtask branch` (since the tenth tooling step) makes three changes of history that were made by hand, each in one command with plain arguments and without an interactive rebase.

```text
cargo xtask branch commit-records --branch <branch> --message "Record the second pull request's CI times" docs/steps/<NN>/ci.md
cargo xtask branch fold 1a2b3c4d          # the staged changes, into that commit
cargo xtask branch fold 1a2b3c4d --last   # the branch's last commit, into that one
cargo xtask branch to-end 1a2b3c4d        # that commit, moved to the tip
```

- **`commit-records --branch B --message MSG [--replace] FILE…`** commits the files, as the working tree has them, on top of branch `B`, which no working tree may be using: the use is `ci-times --write docs/steps/<NN>/ci.md` after a pull request's last push, while the working tree is on another branch.
  It is made in an index of its own (`read-tree B`, `hash-object -w`, `update-index`, `write-tree`, `commit-tree -p B`), so the working tree, the index and the branch checked out are not touched.
  The branch is moved with `update-ref` from the commit read at the start, and is left when it moved meanwhile.
  The commit has the repository's identity, as `git commit` would give it.
  A file must be a record, `docs/steps/**/*.md`, `docs/outstanding/*.md` or `mutations/*.breaks`, given from the top of the repository: anything else belongs on its own branch, where its checks run.
  A file must be a regular file with no link on its path: each part of it, from the top of the repository down, is read with `symlink_metadata`, and a link anywhere is refused, wherever it leads (as `git add` will not add a path beyond one), since what would be committed is the link's target.
  **Each file must be built on `B`'s copy**, when `B` has one: it may be committed when `B`'s copy is the one `HEAD` has (the file was made from it), or when every line of `B`'s copy is in the file, in its order, with lines added anywhere (line ends read alike). So the file made by adding a section to `B`'s copy is committed, and so is a second run after the first commit, with the next push's section added. A file that dropped or reordered `B`'s lines is refused, with `B`'s blob and the way on (take `B`'s copy with `git show B:FILE > FILE`, make the change on it, run again), or `--replace` to put the file in place of `B`'s copy knowingly. `B`'s copies are read in one `cat-file --batch`, and only when one is not `HEAD`'s.
  `B` is the branch named: `@{-1}` and `-`, which `git check-ref-format --branch` expands to the branch checked out before, are refused.
  The branch is moved by an `update-ref --stdin` transaction from the commit read at the start.
  It refuses a name that `git check-ref-format --branch` does not take, a branch that is not there, a file that is not in the working tree, a commit that would change nothing, and **a branch a working tree is using** as `git branch -f` refuses one: checked out (in any working tree, linked ones included), or being rebased or bisected there (its `HEAD` detached, the branch named in `rebase-merge/head-name`, `rebase-apply/head-name` or `BISECT_START`), naming the working tree. Each working tree's git directory is read as files, not asked of git.
  It prints the commit and `git push origin B`, and pushes nothing.
- **`fold COMMIT`** folds the staged changes into `COMMIT`, an earlier commit of the current branch, and makes the commits after it again on top; **`fold COMMIT --last`** folds the branch's last commit into it; **`to-end COMMIT`** moves `COMMIT` to the tip (step 19 kept a commit it might drop last by `switch -c` and `cherry-pick -n`).
  Only the current branch is rewritten, and only from `COMMIT` on: `COMMIT` must be on it and not on `--base REF` (`origin/main` when not given), whose commits are published.
  It refuses a merge in the range, changes not committed (`fold` takes the staged ones, and refuses any others), a detached `HEAD`, a branch another working tree is using (checked out there too, with `worktree add --force`, or being rebased or bisected there), and a commit with nothing to fold or nowhere to move.
  The new history is made in objects alone: each commit is made again with `git merge-tree --write-tree --merge-base` (git 2.40 or later) and `git commit-tree`, keeping its author, date and message byte for byte, with the repository's identity as committer. The commits before `COMMIT` are not made again.
  No ref, index or file is touched while it is made, so a commit that does not apply leaves everything as it was: the command names that commit and the files it conflicts in, and that nothing was changed.
  The new tip's tree is checked against the one it must be (the old tip's, or the staged tree for a `fold` of the staged changes) before anything moves. A deletion staged and folded into a commit before the one that adds the file ends elsewhere: the later commit adds it back. The refusal says which tree it would end at and which it must, and to make the change a commit of its own at the end, or fold it into the later commit.
  Then one `git update-ref --stdin` transaction keeps the old tip as `refs/xtask-backup/<branch>/<unix time>` (`-1`, `-2` for a second in the same second) and moves the branch from the tip read at the start: both, or neither when the branch moved meanwhile. The backup is printed with the command that takes the branch back: `git reset --soft <backup>` after a `fold` of the staged changes, which leaves the fix staged as it was (`--keep` would take it out of the index and the files), and `git reset --keep <backup>` after `fold --last` and `to-end`, which leave the tree as it was.
  The index and the working tree already match the new tip, which has the tree they have.
- **What it costs:** a few gits a command (where `HEAD` is, the names looked up in one `cat-file --batch-check`, the range in one `rev-list`, the commits read in one `cat-file --batch`), then two for each commit made again, and one for the move.
  A git starts in about 0.05 s on a hosted Windows runner (tooling 9's fit), so the tests count their gits too.
- **How it is tested** (`xtask/src/branch/tests.rs`): unit tests of what is a record, the requests, what a working tree's git directory says it uses (checked out, rebased, bisected) and the working trees read from a git directory of files, the undo printed for each command, the transaction's text, the refusals of a tree that is not the one it must be and of a copy that is not `HEAD`'s, a commit's raw text, git's batch output, the commits after the target (a merge, a line that breaks), `merge-tree`'s output and the backup's name.
  Three `sys_` tests on real repositories of a few commits, made with one `git fast-import`, that read none of the machine's git configuration.
  One runs on both systems, in 29 gits: records committed to a branch not checked out, with the working tree's `HEAD`, index and files left byte for byte, and staged changes folded into an earlier commit, with the backup.
  Two run on Unix only, where a git costs a millisecond: what they add is git's own logic, the same on every system, and on Windows it would be about 8 s of the system tier.
  The first commits records to a branch while the working tree has other changes, holds that every other ref, the index and the working tree are as they were, a second run with a section added, the refusal's advice followed (a file the branch has and `HEAD` has not, made from the branch's copy with a section added, committed), a file that dropped the branch's lines refused and then committed with `--replace`, and each refusal (the branch checked out here or in a linked working tree, or being rebased in another, a branch not there, a name that is no branch's, `@{-1}`, a file not there, a link to a file outside, a folder that is a link out and one that is a link inside the repository, nothing changed).
  The second moves a commit of four to the end (the tip's tree is the old one, the backup holds the old tip, the first commit and another branch are untouched, the author, date and message kept, the repository's identity as committer), folds the last commit and then staged changes in, refuses a staged deletion whose fold would not end at the staged tree, refuses the branch checked out twice, refuses what it must, and meets a conflict and a merge with every ref, the index and the working tree left as they were.
  Its breaks are in `mutations/tooling-10-runs.breaks`.

### Rebasing onto main

`cargo xtask rebase` (since the twelfth tooling step) fetches and rebases the current branch onto `origin/main`, and settles each conflict that has a mechanical answer, so that a rebase of a parallel wave's branch stops only where a person is needed.

```text
cargo xtask rebase                     # fetch origin, rebase onto origin/main
cargo xtask rebase --onto main --no-fetch
cargo xtask rebase --continue          # after settling what it left, and `git add`
cargo xtask rebase --abort             # the branch as it was
```

Each time git stops, every conflicted file is put in a class and settled by it:

| Class | Files | What is done |
|---|---|---|
| generated | the files `cargo xtask regen` writes (`regen --list`), named file by file (`classify::GENERATED`, held by a test to `regen`'s places): `docs/protocol/schema/` and `ts/`, `docs/configuration/schema.json` and `reference.md`, the reference screens' `.ans` and `.txt` files, the snapshots. Not the pages written by hand beside them (`docs/protocol/README.md`, `docs/architecture/tui-look/README.md`), nor the recorded provider fixtures in `crates/cineris-llm/fixtures/`, which are never rebuilt | the side rebased onto is taken (`git checkout --ours`: either side would do), and that generator runs at the end |
| the open items' index | `docs/outstanding/README.md` | the same, and `cargo xtask outstanding` at the end, which runs after every rebase: git merges the index line by line and never stops on it |
| additions | a `.rs` or `.md` file, not under a `fixtures/` folder (a fixture is kept byte for byte, and is a person's), in which both sides only added lines at one place, or one side added lines just after lines the other replaced with as many or more | both sides' lines, the side rebased onto first (an addition after a replacement goes after it, the replacement kept whole), checked |
| anything else | | left conflicted, as git left it, and named, with the reason |

When every file of a stop is settled, it is added (the names as paths, never as patterns) and the rebase goes on. A commit the command settled to nothing is skipped with `git rebase --skip`, and named: git's own `--continue` would drop it without a word. One a person settled to nothing before `--continue` is dropped by git, unnamed: git keeps `REBASE_HEAD` too when it could not apply a commit at all (a file in the way), so the two cannot be told apart, and the command does not guess. When git will not go on (a file git does not track is in the way of a commit), the command stops with git's own words, which name the files, and skips nothing. When one is left, the rebase stops there: settle the file, `git add` it, and run `cargo xtask rebase --continue`, which carries on and settles the later stops the same way. What a run keeps between a stop and `--continue` (the tip before the rebase and the generators to run) is a file in the working tree's git directory, `xtask-rebase`, removed at the end. At the end it writes the open items' index, runs the generators of the files it took (`--no-regen` names the command instead), and says that what it wrote is not committed. It prints the tip before the rebase, with `git reset --keep <tip>` to go back, whether the end succeeded or not: a generator that fails is named after the report, and the command fails.

**It never drops a side silently.** The merge is the command's own (`xtask/src/rebase/merge.rs`: Myers' diff of lines, with each run of added lines pushed down past the lines it starts with, so that a test appended after a function's last `}` is read as the whole test after it and not as "`}`, the test, its body" before it), and a union is written only when three checks of the text that would be written pass, whatever made it (`xtask/src/rebase/classify.rs`, `keeps_both`):

- each line is there as many times as the two sides' changes come to (ours' count and theirs', less the base's): a lost `}` is found though another `}` is still there to stand in for it. An earlier wave's union by hand kept one closing brace of two;
- each side's added lines are there in their order;
- the lines of the base that both sides kept are there in their order.

Then, in a Rust file, **no line that attaches to what follows it** (one that starts with `#[`, `#!`, `///`, `/**` or `//`: an attribute, a doc comment, a comment) may be the line just before the place, or the last line of the first part: an attribute one side put above `fn two` with the other side's `fn new` put after it would make `new` Windows-only, or `#[ignore]`d, or a `#[tokio::test]`, and every other check would pass. That rule was chosen over a stricter one (the line before must be blank or end in `}`, `;` or `,`), which would refuse the fields both sides add to a `Debug`, after a line ending in `)`. The brackets must balance outside comments, strings and character literals, and rustfmt (the workspace's edition and `rustfmt.toml`) must leave it as it is; every line a Markdown union put side by side must be a table's row with as many cell dividers as its table's header, and prose added on both sides is a person's. **What is left for a person**, each with its reason: two changes of one line; an addition just before lines the other side changed; an addition beside lines the other side deleted, or replaced with fewer (what was added above an item, an attribute such as `#[cfg(windows)]` or `#[test]`, or a doc comment, belongs to that item, and with the item gone it would attach to the next one; a `let` added after lines the other side removed would use what is not there; a `let` added after lines the other side renamed one for one is kept, and the build says what it uses that is gone); a change both sides made alike in a file that also has a union (the count takes it twice; git itself merges such a change when nothing else conflicts); a file one side added or deleted whole; a file that is not text; three texts whose line ends are not one kind (a text that mixes CRLF and LF, or sides that use different ones: the union is written with one kind, and would change the ends of lines neither side touched); and a place where git saw a conflict and this merge does not. The union is written in the line ends the three share, and its last line ends with a line end when either side's does.

It refuses changes not committed, a second start while a rebase is stopped, and `--continue` or `--abort` with none in progress. Each step is one git with plain arguments, so the command is one call, with no loop or `$(git …)` for a shell to refuse.

What a stop looks like, from this step's demonstration on a branch that added a row to a table that `main` had reworded the last row of and added rows after:

```text
Stopped at c3992548 Add a row to the table that main also adds rows to (a throwaway branch):
  united    docs/steps/25/parts/pr3-first-run.md: both sides' additions at 1 place(s); every line of both kept, and checked
Rebased tooling-12-b-demo onto origin/main: every conflict settled.
docs/outstanding/README.md is up to date.
The tip before was c3992548734d: `git reset --keep c3992548734d` takes the branch back to it.
```

**How it is tested** (`xtask/src/rebase/tests.rs`): the merge, the classes and the checks are pure functions of a path and three texts, tested with no process on the shapes the last wave met, kept in `xtask/fixtures/rebase/` (stored byte for byte, one of them with CRLF line ends): rows added at the end of one of AGENTS.md's tables on both sides, fields added to `CoreOptions` and its `Debug` in `crates/cineris-core/src/core.rs` on both sides, and tests appended to one module on both sides, each with the text the repository had; and the refusals (one line changed on both sides, prose, a row of another shape, rows with no table). A union that lost a brace is refused by the count and by the brackets, each alone. Three `sys_` tests run the command on repositories of a few commits made with one `git fast-import`: one, on both systems, settles a table, a Rust module and a generated file in one stop with rustfmt run on the union (about 17 gits and one rustfmt: 0.06 s on the reference machine, about a second on a hosted Windows runner); two, on Unix only, where what they add is git's own logic, stop on a line both sides changed, go on with `--continue` after a person settles it, abort, and refuse a second start and changes not committed.

### Questions for git in one command

`cargo xtask git` (since the twelfth tooling step) answers three questions that were asked of git with `$(git …)`, pipes into `grep` and line numbers worked out by hand, each in one call with plain arguments, read the same way on every system. They only read.

```text
cargo xtask git changed --against origin/main
cargo xtask git commits docs/testing-and-ci.md --since origin/main --max 10
cargo xtask git added --grep 'TODO|FIXME' --against origin/main xtask/
```

- **`changed [--against REF]`**: every file that differs from where the branch left `REF` (`origin/main` when not given; the merge base of `REF` and `HEAD`), committed, staged, changed in the working tree, or new and not ignored: its status (`A`, `M`, `D`, `T`, or `?` for a new file git does not track), its path, and `(not committed)` when the change is not all in `HEAD`.
- **`commits PATH… [--since REF] [--max N]`**: the commits that touched any of the paths, newest first, as `hash date subject`; `--since REF` keeps those after `REF`; at most 30 unless `--max` says otherwise (0 is all).
- **`added --grep RE [--against REF] [PATH…]`**: each line added since where the branch left `REF`, committed or not, the lines of new files included, that matches `RE` (the matcher of `ci-log --grep`, `xtask/src/pattern.rs`), as `PATH:LINE: TEXT`, the line being the file's as it is now, read from each hunk's start in `git diff -U0`.

They are named for the question, under `git` because what they read is git's. A unit test reads a diff's added lines and their numbers (a deleted file, a new file, a line that starts `+++`), and one `sys_` test, on both systems, asks all three of a branch with a commit, a change not committed and a new file (about 16 gits: 0.02 s on the reference machine).

### The public API between two trees

A fix session is asked to list the public signatures it changed, for the step that comes after it, and wrote the list by hand. `cargo xtask api-diff [--against REF] [-p CRATE]… [--format text|markdown]` writes it (since the seventh tooling step): the public items added, removed and changed between a ref (`main` when not given; `origin/main` where there is no `main`, as in a checkout of a branch from the remote) and the working tree, by crate, each with its path and its signature before and after. `-p` is repeatable; without it every workspace crate that has a library is compared, those only one tree has included (the report says so).

```text
Public API, `main` (1f2e3d4c5b) against the working tree
cineris-base: 3 added, 1 removed, 1 changed
  + cineris_base::ResolvedPath::shown  fn shown(&self) -> String
  - cineris_base::ResolvedPath::display  fn display(&self) -> String
  ~ cineris_base::load
      was  fn load(path: &str) -> Config
      now  fn load(path: &str, strict: bool) -> Config
no public API changes: cineris-agent, cineris-config
```

- **How the API is read: rustdoc's JSON output** (`--output-format json`). It is exact: it has the re-exports, the items a macro or a derive makes, what a `cfg` leaves out, and every signature as the compiler understood it. The lighter way, reading the `pub` items with the lexer of `mutate`, was not taken because it misses exactly those (a re-export, a `pub(crate)` that is not public, an `impl` a derive writes, a macro's items) and would report them wrongly in both directions. The JSON needs `-Z unstable-options`, which the pinned stable toolchain accepts with `RUSTC_BOOTSTRAP=1`: no second toolchain to install, and the format is the pinned toolchain's own, where a nightly would write another. `cargo xtask fuzz` needs a nightly because the fuzzer does; this does not.
- **Only the format it was written against is read.** rustdoc's JSON has no stability promise: its field names move from one toolchain to the next. `api_diff/read.rs` reads **format 61**, the one toolchain 1.99.1 writes, and refuses any other number with both numbers named (`SUPPORTED_FORMATS`). **A toolchain bump that moves the number makes `api-diff` fail** until the new format has been looked at, the reader changed and the list extended: the ignored test `sys_the_real_rustdoc_…` of `xtask` runs the real rustdoc on two small crates and says at once (the nightly workflow runs it with the other `sys_the_real` tests). What the reader does not know within its format (a type, a bound or a predicate of a shape it has not seen) is written as the JSON itself, so a change in it still shows.
- **What is listed.** The walk goes from the crate root through public items only, and follows `pub use` (a glob too). An item that is reached by one path is an entry there, with its members: a struct's or union's fields, an enum's variants (each with its fields in its signature and its discriminant), a trait's items (a method with a default says `{ .. }`, a constant with one `= ..`), the methods of its inherent `impl`s (with the `impl`'s bounds when it has any), and `impl`s of traits for it, with the associated types they fix. An item reached by several paths is full at the first (where it is defined, else the shortest) and a `use` entry (`pub use <path>`) at each of the others, so a re-export that is added or removed shows. A struct says when it has private fields, `#[non_exhaustive]` and `#[repr(…)]` are part of the signature, and an `impl !Send` is listed where a type stops being `Send`. What rustdoc adds to every type (`Send`, `Sync`, `From<T> for T`) is left out. A private or `#[doc(hidden)]` item is not in rustdoc's JSON and not here.
- **What "changed" means:** a different kind of item, or a different signature: generics, bounds, arguments and return type, a struct's shape, a variant's fields, a trait's supertraits and items, an `impl`'s bounds. Not its documentation, not its body (a constant's value, a function's code), and not the name of an argument (it is shown, and a rename is no change: a caller does not see it).
- **The `impl`s of a type that was added in the same change are left out of the report** (and those of a type that was removed): a new type with its derives would be a dozen lines of `impl Clone for …` beside it. Its fields, variants and methods are listed.
- **The output is sorted by path and the same each run.** One line for an addition or a removal and three for a change, short enough to paste; `--format markdown` is a table for each crate, for a report. Progress goes to the standard error, the report to the standard output.
- **Nothing of the checkout's own is touched.** As `combine` does, the other tree is a clone under the build directory (`target/api-diff/base`) made with `git clone --shared`, which reads the repository and writes nothing to it: no branch, no ref, no linked-working-tree entry. It is checked out at the ref and reused by the next run, which moves it. A clone that is damaged is made again, never checked out in: git would look in the folders above it and find the checkout's own repository. The working tree is documented where it is, uncommitted changes and new files included. Each tree has a build directory of its own (`target/api-diff/head-target` and `base-target`), kept between runs, so that the next run documents only what changed; they are apart from the ordinary build, so that a build with `RUSTC_BOOTSTRAP=1` (which build scripts may read) never disturbs the ordinary one. One run at a time (a lock). A copy without a `.git` is refused.
- **The API is that of the host system with the crate's default features** (and `cineris-sandbox`'s `backends` on Linux, as `test -p` turns it on): an item behind `cfg(windows)` is in the report of a Windows run only.
- **`--features CRATE/FEATURE,…`** (since the tenth tooling step) turns features on when documenting both trees, so that what a test kit behind one exports is in the report: `api-diff -p cineris-tui --features test-kit`, or `--features cineris-tui/test-kit,cineris-policy/test-support` for several crates (a bare name needs exactly one `-p`). A feature the working tree's crate does not have, or of a crate not compared, is refused before anything is built; one the ref's crate does not have yet is left off there and said, so a kit added by the change shows as added.
- **What it costs** (the reference machine, a debug build of `xtask`, the tree with 22 crates; the first run builds each tree's third-party crates in its own build directory):

  | Run | Time |
  |---|---|
  | One crate (`cineris-base`), build directories empty | 10.5 s: 5.1 s to document each tree |
  | The same, nothing changed | 0.7 s |
  | The 19 crates that have a library, third-party crates built once before for one crate only | 21.8 s: 9.6 s and 9.5 s to document the two trees, the rest the clone, `cargo metadata` and reading 19 JSON files twice |
  | The same, nothing changed | 2.6 s: 0.8 s of cargo for each tree, the rest the reading |

  The second machine took 43 s for the first run on one crate, third-party crates included. A run is dominated by the first build of each tree's third-party crates in its own build directory, which is why they are kept; after it, a change in a crate costs what rustdoc takes to document that crate and those built on it. Under two minutes cold was the bar, and it is met by a wide margin, so the exact way was taken.

- **How it is tested:** `api_diff/read/tests.rs` reads a rustdoc JSON file recorded from the real rustdoc (`xtask/fixtures/api-diff/toy.json`, the crate in `toy.rs.txt` beside it, with what the reader does not use taken out) and holds the whole listing it gives (every kind of item and type, the re-exports of a private module, a glob, derives, a trait with defaults, `extern "C"` with a variadic argument), and JSON written for the purpose for the rules one at a time: the format that is refused, private items, what is not public, argument names, auto-trait impls and `impl !Send`, two methods of one name in two `impl`s, the path an item is given, and which type an `impl` belongs to. `api_diff/diff/tests.rs` holds the comparison and both outputs. No test runs a toolchain except one, `sys_the_real_rustdoc_reports_what_was_added_removed_and_changed`: a repository made for it with a crate at a commit and, in the working tree, a signature changed, a field, a default method and a derive added, a function removed and a crate that was not there. It documents two trees with the real rustdoc, so it is ignored in the blocking checks (0.3 s on the reference machine once built, a few seconds from cold) and the nightly workflow runs it. Two tests start git and are ignored on Windows, as `combine`'s are (the ref's default and fallback; the clone that moves between commits and leaves the repository as it was).

### Where the five minutes go

Everything in this section was measured on GitHub-hosted runners on 2026-11-12, in step 2. Both runner types report 4 cores. Times are from the job and step timestamps the API reports, to the second, and from timers in the measurement scripts.

**Hosted runners vary.** The same clean build took 27, 29, 42 and 45 s on four Linux runners and 60, 75, 77 and 82 s on four Windows runners on the same afternoon. Read every number here as "about", and compare two settings only when they were measured in the same job on the same machine, which is how the comparisons below were made unless they say otherwise.

#### The skeleton, as merged in step 2

The workspace has 21 crates with no code to speak of, 22 test binaries and five small third-party crates. Wall time per OS is the slower of that OS's two jobs.

| Case | Linux | Windows |
|---|---|---|
| Cold cache: the pull request's own runs, which have no cache to read | 20 to 29 s | 37 to 39 s. Before cargo's and rustup's files were moved off `C:` (below): 47 to 59 s, and 74 s once |
| Warm cache: a scratch branch running the same jobs with cache writing on, second run | 20 s | 42 s, measured before that move |
| Documentation-only pull request | 16 s | 14 s |

All four cases are far inside 5 minutes, and cold and warm are the same within noise, because there is almost nothing to cache yet. The warm case can only be measured on the real workflow once the main branch has written its first caches, which happens when step 2 is merged; the first pull request after that is the check.

The job breakdown, for the last cold run of step 2's pull request:

| Phase | `lint-linux` | `test-linux` | `lint-windows` | `test-windows` |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Start the job, check out, ask what changed | 2 s | 4 s | 9 s | 9 s |
| Install the pinned toolchain | 8 s | 14 s | 9 s | 8 s |
| Look for a cache (there is none yet) | 0 s | 1 s | 2 s | 3 s |
| Install cargo-deny or cargo-nextest | 1 s | 1 s | not needed | 2 s |
| Build `xtask`, then run the job's command | 5 s | 6 s | 11 s | 14 s |
| of which the command's own steps | fmt 0.1 s, rules 0.1 s, clippy 1.9 s, deny 0.3 s | build tests 2.7 s, run tests 0.3 s | clippy 3.8 s | build tests 4.2 s, run tests 0.6 s |
| Finish the job | 1 s | 1 s | 3 s | 1 s |
| **Job total** | **20 s** | **29 s** | **37 s** | **39 s** |

The steps are rounded to the second, so a column does not add up exactly to its total. The toolchain install in `test-linux` took 14 s in this run and 8 to 9 s in the others.

Most of a skeleton job is fixed cost: starting the job, checking out, installing the pinned toolchain and looking for a cache. That fixed cost is about 15 s on Linux and 20 to 25 s on Windows, and it is the floor under every future run.

#### After each later step

From step 3 on, each step records its CI times, and what its first runs showed, in a file of its own: `steps/<step>/ci.md`, beginning with [steps/03/ci.md](steps/03/ci.md). The folders of [steps/](steps/README.md) sort in step order. A step adds nothing here.

#### The planned third-party set

The skeleton cannot show what a cold cache will cost once the dependencies arrive, which is the risk this page was written around. So step 2 also built a throwaway program that depends on the whole third-party set planned for the first release (the async runtime, the HTTP and TLS stack, serde, the terminal library, the search libraries, the MCP SDK, the highlighter, the script engine, the test-kit libraries, and the Windows-only and Linux-only crates: 283 packages in its lockfile) on both runners. The program was on a scratch branch and is not in the repository. It uses each library enough to link it.

| Measurement | Linux | Windows |
|---|---|---|
| Fetch the sources (download and unpack) | 2 to 3 s | 26 to 37 s |
| Clean build, dependencies at `opt-level = 1` (the `dev` profile) | 129 s | 259 s |
| Clean build, dependencies at `opt-level = 0` (the `ci` profile) | 27 to 45 s | 60 to 82 s |
| Relink the one binary after touching its source | 0.4 to 0.5 s | 1.2 to 1.9 s |
