| Build directory | 0.5 to 0.6 GB | 0.65 to 0.72 GB |
| The cache `rust-cache` stores for it | 209 MB | 220 MB |
| Restore that cache | 3 to 4 s | 26 s |
| Unpack the sources again after a restore (the cache holds the `.crate` files, not the unpacked trees) | 2 s | 18 s |
| Build after a restore | under 1 s | 5 s |

What follows from it:

- **Optimised dependencies do not fit a cold Windows run.** With the `dev` profile's `opt-level = 1`, Windows spends about 290 s fetching and building dependencies before it compiles a line of ours. Unoptimised, it spends about 110 s. That is why the test jobs build in a profile of their own, `ci`. The `dev` profile is unchanged, because on a local machine the dependencies are built once and kept.
- **Line tables are nearly free.** In one job per OS, with dependencies unoptimised: no debug info anywhere, 29 to 30 s on Linux and 75 to 82 s on Windows; line tables for the top crate only, 29 s and 75 s; line tables for every crate, which is more than `ci` asks for, 32 s and 83 s. Relink times did not change. So `ci` kept line tables for our own crates, until the CI-budgets fix measured them again with the workspace grown: 19.7 s of a cold Windows build, and `ci` has none since ("Tests build in cargo's `ci` profile", above).
- **The faster linker does not pay on Windows.** With `rust-lld` in place of the default linker, in the same job: a clean build took 274 s against 259 s, and a relink 1.3 s against 1.5 to 1.7 s. For the skeleton's 22 test binaries, 2.9 s against 3.8 s. Linking is not where the time goes, so the default linker stays. (Mitigation 7 below is closed.)
- **The script engine builds on Windows.** QuickJS compiled with the MSVC toolchain on the hosted runner and ran a script. Step 28 still confirms it in the real crate.
- **A warm Windows run paid about 45 s before it built anything of ours:** 26 s to restore the cache and 18 s to unpack the sources. The second figure is what the next section cuts to 9 s.

#### Where cargo's files live on a Windows runner

A hosted Windows runner has two disks. The workspace is checked out on `D:`, and cargo's home directory, where crate sources are downloaded and unpacked, is on `C:` by default. Unpacking a few hundred crates is tens of thousands of small files, and that is slow on `C:`. Measured in one job, two rounds each, with the planned set (283 packages):

| Where `CARGO_HOME` is | Download and unpack | Unpack only (what a warm run does) | Set-up cost |
|---|---|---|---|
| `C:`, the default | 26 to 30 s | 23 to 27 s | none |
| `C:`, in a folder excluded from antivirus scanning | 24 to 25 s | 24 to 33 s | 0.1 s |
| The workspace's disk, `D:` | 10 s | 9 s | none |
| A Dev Drive (a ReFS volume in a virtual disk on `D:`, trusted) | 8 to 9 s | 7 to 8 s | 9 s to create, and 1.5 s to copy the checkout onto it |

- **The Windows jobs set `CARGO_HOME` and `RUSTUP_HOME` to a folder on the workspace's disk.** It costs nothing and saves 15 to 20 s on every Windows run that has dependencies to unpack, warm or cold.
- **A Dev Drive is not used.** The owner asked for it to be measured, with the workspace, `CARGO_HOME` and the build directory on it. In one job, alternating the default disks and the Dev Drive twice: a clean build of the planned set took 75 and 76 s on the default disks and 76 and 77 s on the Dev Drive; a relink 1.6 to 1.7 s on both; the skeleton's 22 test binaries 7.9 and 8.0 s against 8.0 and 9.2 s; restoring a cache archive 3.1 and 3.0 s against 2.7 and 3.0 s. Compiling and linking are not held back by the file system here. The one thing the Dev Drive speeds up is unpacking sources, and the plain `D:` disk gets all but about 2 s of that without the 10 s of set-up. Net, the Dev Drive is slower.
- Real-time antivirus scanning is already off on the hosted image (`RealTimeProtectionEnabled: False`), which is why excluding a folder changes nothing and why a Dev Drive's "performance mode" has little to give.

#### After a lockfile change: `rust-cache` alone, or with `sccache`

The owner asked whether a per-crate compile cache (`sccache` with the GitHub Actions cache as its store) would shorten the run after a lockfile change. The premise was that a lockfile change throws the dependency cache away. It does not: `rust-cache` restores the newest cache for the previous lockfile, and cargo rebuilds the crates that changed and the crates that depend on them. The measurement, with the planned set in the `ci` profile, each cell one job (so different machines; differences of a few seconds are noise):

| Run | Linux, `rust-cache` | Linux, with `sccache` | Windows, `rust-cache` | Windows, with `sccache` |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Everything cold | 45 s | 61 s | 88 s | 88 s |
| One small crate added that nothing else depends on: first push | 1.8 s | 2.2 s | 18.2 s | 19.8 s |
| The same: second push | 1.8 s | 1.9 s | 18.0 s | 19.2 s |
| A patch bump of `bytes`, which 19 crates of the set are rebuilt for: first push | 14.9 s | 17.3 s | 39.4 s | 40.1 s |
| The same: second push | 11.2 s | 8.0 s | 36.3 s | 28.4 s |

The times are the build step, which on Windows includes about 18 s of unpacking sources on `C:` (these runs were made before `CARGO_HOME` was moved). "First push" is the first run of a pull request that changes the lockfile; it reads the main branch's cache and, like every pull request, cannot write it. "Second push" is a later run of the same pull request.

- **`sccache` is not used.** On the first push after a lockfile change it has nothing to offer: the crates that must be rebuilt have new inputs, so its cache has never seen them. It was 0.4 to 2.4 s slower there. It helps only from the second push of a pull request that bumps a crate much of the graph depends on, by about 3 s on Linux and 8 s on Windows.
- What it would cost: a third-party action in the blocking workflow, a network request per compiled crate, cache entries written from pull requests (against the rule that only the main branch writes caches), and a slower cold build on the Linux runner that measured it.
- The warm case was not slowed by it: with every dependency fresh, cargo starts no compiler and the wrapper is not called.

#### Projection for the first release

Not measured: put together from the measured parts above, for a pull request once the planned dependencies are in. The parts for our own code are estimates, because that code does not exist yet.

| Phase | Linux, warm | Linux, cold | Windows, warm | Windows, cold | Basis |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Start the job, check out, install the toolchain and the tools | 0:15 | 0:15 | 0:25 | 0:25 | Measured |
| Restore the cache | 0:04 | 0:00 | 0:26 | 0:00 | Measured |
| Download or unpack the sources | 0:02 | 0:03 | 0:09 | 0:10 | Measured, with `CARGO_HOME` on the workspace's disk |
| Build third-party dependencies, `ci` profile | 0:00 | 0:27 to 0:45 | 0:05 | 1:00 to 1:22 | Measured |
| Build workspace crates and test binaries | 0:50 | 0:50 | 1:30 | 1:30 | Estimate, unchanged from the first draft |
| Run tiers 1 to 3 | 0:45 | 0:45 | 1:15 | 1:15 | Estimate, unchanged; the tier budgets cap it |
| **Total** | **about 2:00** | **about 2:20 to 2:40** | **about 3:50** | **about 4:20 to 4:45** | |

With the `dev` profile's optimised dependencies the cold Windows column would be about 7:40. In the `ci` profile it is inside 5 minutes, with little room, and it rests on two estimates. The steps that add the first heavy dependencies (5, 6 and 14) should look at the job summary's timings when they land, and take mitigation 8 below before the estimate is tested the hard way.

**The risk is still the cold-cache Windows run,** and it is now a rare one: a toolchain bump, or expired caches. The mitigations, in the order they are applied, with what step 2 found:

1. Keep the dependency tree small. This is why every dependency has a measured cost and an owner crate.
2. Only the main branch writes the cache, and a scheduled job on the main branch keeps it warm. A pull request that does not touch the lockfile never builds dependencies. **In place.**
3. A lockfile change restores the previous lockfile's cache and rebuilds only what changed. **In place** (it is how `rust-cache` works), and measured above.
4. Build dependencies unoptimised in CI. **In place:** the `ci` profile. It is the largest single saving found.
5. Group dependency updates into one weekly pull request, and bump the toolchain in a pull request of its own, so cold runs are rare and expected.
6. One integration-test binary per crate, to cut link steps. **Checked by `cargo xtask rules`.**
7. The faster linker on Windows. **Measured: no gain**, in step 2 and again in the eleventh tooling step on the real workspace (a rebuild after a change to `cineris-base`, 71.6 s with `rust-lld` against 72.2 s). Not used.
8. If the Windows job is still over budget on a warm cache: split it in two with nextest's partitioning, building once and sharing the test archive.
9. If a cold-cache Windows run is still over 5 minutes after all of this, that case is reported to the owner for a decision. The limit is not relaxed quietly.
10. Cache the workspace's own build on Windows, saved by the main branch and dated against its commit ("The workspace's own build, cached"). **In place** since the eleventh tooling step.

**What grows the time, and the rule for each.**

| Growth | Rule |
|---|---|
| More tests | The per-test limits and tier budgets under "Test tiers", which CI enforces. The final review step lists the slowest tests. |
| More crates | Each new crate needs one of the reasons in [architecture/01-workspace.md](architecture/01-workspace.md). |
| More dependencies | The dependency policy, and the crate-count check below. |
| The script engine's C code | Built once and cached. Its measured build time is in decision 0005. |
| More Windows jobs | Windows runners are slower to start a job than Linux ones, and when more than about four Windows jobs of this repository were running at once, further ones waited minutes for a runner. Queue time is outside a job's 5 minutes but inside what a person waits. Keep the blocking workflow at two Windows jobs. |

### Not blocking

`main` and `nightly` exist in their first form since step 3 of the [build plan](build-plan.md), with the checks that need no product code. Later steps add their tiers to them. `release` and `live` arrive with the steps that need them. The blocking workflow also runs on every push to the main branch and twice a week on a schedule: those runs are what write the caches and keep them from expiring.

| Workflow | When | What it runs now | What later steps add |
|---|---|---|---|
| `main` (`.github/workflows/main.yml`) | After each merge to the main branch, and by hand | `cargo xtask size --check` for Linux x86-64 and Windows x86-64: a build of the published profile (`dist`, with the build machine's folders taken out of its paths and the binary then read for any path under a home folder, since step 33's size fix: decision [0036](decisions/0036-the-shipped-binary-is-optimised-for-size.md)), the binary's size checked against its budget ([performance-budgets.md](performance-budgets.md)), and the number of crates in the build. Each run keeps its record as an artifact, and shows it on its summary page. | Tier 4 performance checks on both OSes with stored baselines (step 20), and a comparison of the size and the crate count with the run before. |
| `nightly` (`.github/workflows/nightly.yml`) | Every night at 03:17 UTC, and by hand | `cargo xtask advisories` (`cargo deny check advisories`, of the lockfile of every package the repository builds: the workspace's and, since step 30, `fuzz/`'s; the corpus's lockfile, which pins test input nothing builds, and `mutate`'s fixture's are left out, named in `xtask`); `cargo xtask unused` (cargo-machete); `cargo xtask size --check` for every release target, each on a runner of its own architecture: `x86_64-unknown-linux-musl`, `aarch64-unknown-linux-musl`, `x86_64-pc-windows-msvc`; and, since step 4, tiers 1 and 2 on Linux with fifty times the property-test cases ("Property tests", above); and, since step 14, coverage-guided fuzzing of the stream decoders, and since the fixes of step 18's review of the outline extractors, and since step 27 of the RPC server's decoder, for two minutes a target (`cargo xtask fuzz --seconds 120`); and `cargo xtask mutate` against the real cargo and nextest, on the small workspace kept for that ("Breaking the code on purpose"); and, since the tenth tooling step, `cargo xtask mutate --check --tests` (the `break-names` job), which builds the tests every break runs and checks each `caught-by` against them; and, since the second tooling step, the timeout report against a test that hangs on purpose and the race pause, on Linux and in the Windows release job ("When a test runs out of time"); in that Windows job, `combine`'s tests and, since the CI-budgets fix, `cargo xtask git-tests-on-windows`: the git-heavy tests of the tools run locally or on Linux CI, which the blocking job runs on Linux alone ("A system test that starts more than about 20 processes", above); and, since the third, `cargo xtask partial-landlock` on Ubuntu 22.04: `cineris-sandbox`'s tests on a kernel with Landlock ABI 4, the partial case, which enforces with the signal gap named and which no other runner or machine of ours has. It fails when the tests fail, and when the run did not show the partial case (the signal test's line naming the gap), so that an image whose kernel moves on cannot turn it into a copy of the blocking job. `try` runs on the same image with `-f os=linux-22.04`. And, since step 27, a turn run by the TypeScript client of the RPC protocol, its types checked first (the `ts-client` job: "The TypeScript client", above). | Tier 6: a fuzz target for each later decoder, the long-session memory test. Tier 5 where a key is configured. |
| `release` | On a version tag | Not built yet. | Builds, checks and publishes binaries with checksums. |
| `live` | By hand | Not built yet. | Tier 5 and fixture recording. Uses a protected environment for secrets. |

- **The jobs call `cargo xtask`,** like the blocking ones, so the same commands run locally.
- **Fuzzing is two things.** The first is the nightly property run. Step 5 built the first decoder, the SSE parser, and fuzzes it with a property: the pieces of the format in any order, with bytes that are not text among them, cut at random, must never panic and must give the same events however they are cut. Pull requests run 400 such inputs and the nightly run 20,000. Step 14 added the same for the Anthropic decoder, from the pieces of real streams with some of them damaged. That is random testing: it does not follow coverage.
- **The second, since step 14, is coverage-guided fuzzing,** in the `fuzz` job of the nightly workflow.
  - **The targets** are a package of their own, `fuzz/`, outside the workspace, with its own lockfile: the fuzzer needs a nightly compiler and a C++ compiler for its runtime, and neither belongs in the blocking checks or in what ships. There is one target for the SSE parser and one for each protocol's decoder: `anthropic_decoder` and, since step 31, `openai_chat_decoder`, whose first input byte chooses the kind of server (reasoning under a named member, reasoning in tags inside the text, a model that is asked for a speed tier or one that is not). Since the fixes of the independent review of step 18, `extractors` runs every outline extractor of `cineris-index` on the input as a file, decoded as a pass decodes one: no input panics or passes a cap (entries, a name's, a head's or a doc's length). Since step 27, `rpc_decoder` reads the input as the bytes a client sends the RPC server, as a connection reads them: lines split by the framing's decoder (with a limit of 4 KiB, below the fuzzer's longest input, so that a line too long is reached too), each line read as the server reads it, a request's operation by `Op::from_wire`, and an answer's result as the answer to each kind of request the server puts (`ServerResponse::from_wire`). It reads lines with the server's own code, `cineris-rpc`'s `wire.rs`, which the package compiles from that file by `#[path]` rather than depend on `cineris-rpc`: that would build the whole core into the fuzzer, the HTTP and TLS stack among it (a cold build of the package took 51 s on the reference machine with it, against 30 s). The file uses `cineris-base`, `cineris-protocol`, `serde` and `serde_json` alone, and says so at its head.
  - **What a target checks** is written as an ordinary function (`fuzz/src/lib.rs`), which a test on the stable toolchain also calls. The first two bytes of an input decide where the rest is cut, so the fuzzer explores the cuts as well as the content. For the parser and each decoder: no input panics, and the same bytes give the same result however they are cut. For the decoder also: a response ends at most once and nothing follows its end; a block ends only after it started; and every block the decoder stores is one JSON object, so that it can be sent back. The RPC target's input is the stream as it is, so that a recorded line is an input; where it is cut comes from a hash of it. Its properties: no input panics; the same bytes are read the same however they are cut; a line that is not a message, or is too long, gets an error the server can write, and a client reads that error back; a JSON object with an id the server can read gets it under that id; and an operation, an answer, a request and a response, once read, are the same once written again and read. A float may come back a unit or two in the last place off: serde_json, built without its `float_roundtrip` feature as the harness builds it, reads a float only that closely (none of 20 million random floats came back further), so the target takes floats four units apart for the same, and nothing else.
  - **The corpus** each run starts from is the recorded fixtures, each once whole and once in small pieces; the extractors' is their sample files (`crates/cineris-index/fixtures/outlines`), each once; the RPC decoder's is files of lines a client sends (`crates/cineris-rpc/fixtures/lines`), each once: every request of the protocol's golden transcript, which holds every operation; the answers to each kind of request the server puts; a notification; a batch; one line of each kind the server refuses; and a line past the target's limit. Nothing the fuzzer finds is kept between runs yet.
  - **Running it.** `cargo xtask fuzz [--seconds N] [TARGET…]`, which needs a nightly toolchain and `cargo-fuzz`. An input that fails is kept under `fuzz/artifacts/`, and as an artifact of the nightly run, and `cargo +nightly fuzz run <target> <file>` runs it again.
  - **The nightly compiler is pinned to a date,** in `fuzz/rust-toolchain.toml` and nowhere else, so that a new nightly cannot break the job without a change here. `cargo xtask fuzz` and the workflow both run inside `fuzz/`, where that file decides the toolchain. **To move it forward:** change the date in that file to a recent nightly, start the nightly workflow by hand on the branch (`gh workflow run nightly.yml --ref <branch>`), and merge when its `fuzz` job passes. Do it when cargo-fuzz or the fuzzer's runtime is updated, or every few months: the sanitizer and the coverage instrumentation are part of the compiler, and an old nightly gets none of their fixes.
  - **The package's lockfile is its own,** and the nightly advisory check, which reads the workspace's lockfile, does not see it. What it adds to the workspace's packages is the fuzzer's runtime and the few crates that brings.
- **No build cache.** Nobody waits for these runs, and the blocking workflow's caches keep the repository's whole 10 GB.
- **One Windows job each.** Hosted Windows runners queue when a repository starts many of them ("What grows the time", above).

**A failure opens or updates an issue.** It does not block merging, and it is not ignored. Each workflow ends with a `report` job that runs only when another job failed, **in a run on the main branch** (scheduled, or dispatched there): a run of the workflow on a branch reports nothing, so that it cannot add to the main branch's issue (since the third tooling step, whose dispatched nightly runs added two comments to #40). The nightly's `partial-landlock` job is not among the jobs it reports until the fixes to step 26 make a test of the protected-paths watch pass on Ubuntu 22.04 (the comment on the `report` job names it). It looks for an open issue titled "The `<workflow>` workflow failed", with the label `ci-failure`. If there is none it opens one; if there is one it adds a comment. Either way the text names the run, the jobs that did not succeed, the trigger and the commit. A person closes the issue when the cause is fixed, and the next failure opens a new one. The steps are in `.github/actions/report-failure`.

**Only the `report` job can write, and only issues.** Every workflow's token is read-only at the top level. A workflow that runs on pull requests can write nothing and uses no secret. In a workflow that does not, the job named `report` may have `issues: write`, and nothing else may have any write permission: the job that holds a token that can write runs no code from the build, and the jobs that build and run code hold a token that cannot write. `cargo xtask rules` checks all of it.

**Testing the report.** Both workflows take a `force_failure` input when started by hand, which makes the first job fail at once. Since the report is for runs on the main branch only, `gh workflow run nightly.yml --ref main -f force_failure=true` exercises the whole path without waiting for a real failure; on a branch the forced failure shows the job failing and reports nothing. Close the issue it opens. Step 3 did this for both workflows from a scratch branch, before the report was held to the main branch: each forced run opened its issue within seconds of failing, and a second failure of `nightly` was added to the open issue as a comment.

## Lints and formatting

- `rustfmt` with a small checked-in configuration.
- Clippy with warnings as errors, a workspace lint table, and three project-specific rules through `clippy.toml`:
  - blocking calls (`std::fs`, `std::process`, `std::thread::sleep`) are disallowed. The lists are on in every crate. Code that is meant to block, because it runs on the blocking pool or the whole crate is synchronous, opts out for a module or the crate with an `allow` that gives the reason;
  - `unwrap` and `expect` are disallowed outside tests, except where an attribute gives the invariant;
  - `println!` and `eprintln!` are disallowed outside `cineris-cli` and `xtask`, so nothing can write to standard output by accident.
- An `allow` or `expect` attribute must carry a `reason`. `todo!`, `unimplemented!`, `dbg!` and `std::process::exit` are warnings, and so errors in CI.
- `unsafe` is denied by default and allowed per module in `cineris-sandbox` and `cineris-term`, where the OS interfaces need it, and in one Windows-only module of `cineris-base`, `private::windows`, which creates files with an access list of their own and reads a file's reparse tag ([decision 0029](decisions/0029-owner-only-files-on-windows.md)), and in one Windows-only module of `cineris-rpc`, `socket::windows`, which makes the named pipe with its security and checks each client's token ([decision 0037](decisions/0037-the-named-pipe-is-served-from-cineris-rpc-in-one-windows-only-module-that-may-use-unsafe.md)). Every block has a safety comment. A new place needs a decision record.
- Public items are documented (`missing_docs`).
- Clippy runs on Linux and on Windows, so that code compiled only for Windows is linted.
- **A build of the binary without the script engine is checked** (`check`'s `no-scripts` step, since step 28's close; [decision 0005](decisions/0005-extension-mechanism.md)). Clippy lints the workspace's build, which has the engine. The step first lists `cineris-cli`'s dependency graph with its `scripts` feature off and its other default features on, as its manifest names them, for every target (`cargo tree --target all -e normal,build -p cineris-cli --no-default-features`), and fails when `rquickjs` is in it all the same: a crate between the binary and the core that turned the core's `scripts` on, on one system or on all, would otherwise let the check pass on a build with the engine. Then it lints that build as the `clippy` step lints the workspace, warnings denied (`cargo clippy --locked -p cineris-cli --no-default-features -- -D warnings`). The code of `cineris-core` that only the engine's build uses is marked `expect(dead_code)` there, each item with why. It runs no test: the words `cineris ext add` says without the engine are held by a test of the build with it (`ext::tests::ext_add_without_the_script_engine_says_the_build_has_none`), and the session's wiring of such a build by the ordinary build's tests ([architecture/12-extensions.md](architecture/12-extensions.md), "A build without the script engine"). It runs in `lint-linux`. Nothing in CI builds it for Windows; it can be checked by hand from Linux with `cargo xwin clippy --target x86_64-pc-windows-msvc -p cineris-cli --no-default-features --locked -- -D warnings` (or `cargo xwin check …`), and on Windows with `cargo xtask check no-scripts`. On the reference machine it takes 0.1 to 0.2 s once built, 0.8 s after a change to the core, and about 7 s with the workspace's crates linted from nothing and the third-party ones kept.

### One crate's lints

`cargo xtask check` lints the whole workspace in one build, and cargo builds each crate there with every feature any member asks of it. A plain `cargo clippy -p cineris-cli` builds only what the CLI asks for, and fails on code the workspace's check passes: `cineris-tui` reads its `clock` field only under its `test-kit` feature, which its own dev-dependency on itself turns on in a workspace build and nothing turns on in a build of the CLI alone, so the field is "never read" there. `cargo xtask clippy -p CRATE…` (since the twelfth tooling step) lints the crates named as the check does:

```text
cargo xtask clippy -p cineris-cli
cargo xtask clippy -p cineris-tui -p cineris-core -- -W clippy::pedantic
```

- **The features are the workspace build's,** worked out from the manifests: each member's `default`, every feature a member's dependency of any kind names on another member, and what each of those turns on, in the crate and in its workspace dependencies, to a fixed point. A `dep?/feature` counts as `dep/feature`, and a dependency under a `[target.…]` table counts on every system: more than the workspace may turn on, never less.
- **Cargo takes `CRATE/FEATURE` only for a crate named and its direct dependencies** ("the package does not contain these features" for any other). Those are passed as `--features`; the others the crates' build reaches are named in a note, and left off. A lint in one of those crates that differs from the check's is found by naming that crate with `-p` too.
- The rest is the check's: `--all-targets --locked`, and `-D warnings` after any arguments given after `--`. Tested on manifests written for the purpose (`xtask/src/clippy.rs`).
- `cargo xtask apply`'s `@@ check: clippy -p CRATE…` runs it, when its words are crates alone; with other words it is cargo's own `clippy --all-targets`, as before.

**The repo rules** are the checks no off-the-shelf tool does. `cargo xtask rules` runs them, and a test runs them again on both OSes:

- no file contains an absolute home-directory path (every file is scanned, binary files included);
- **no file holds a name from before the rename to Cineris** (since the rename, [decision 0038](decisions/0038-the-project-is-named-cineris.md)), in its text or its path, outside the kept files and lines that `cargo xtask rename --kept` lists ([CONTRIBUTING.md](../CONTRIBUTING.md), "Renaming the project");
- the crate dependency rules of [architecture/01-workspace.md](architecture/01-workspace.md), read from `cargo metadata`, and the manifest conventions (the `cineris-` prefix, `publish = false`, shared lints, no doc tests, one integration-test binary);
- `cineris-protocol` depends on serde and serde_json and nothing else, and on schemars only as an optional dependency behind its `schema` feature;
- `docs/dependencies.md` agrees with the manifests;
- workflow actions are pinned by commit hash; every workflow's token is read-only; a workflow that runs on pull requests uses no secret and can write nothing; and the only write permission anywhere is `issues: write` on the `report` job of a workflow that does not run on pull requests ("Not blocking", above); and no `run:` script has a workflow input or a part of the event (`${{ inputs.… }}`, `${{ github.event.… }}`, `${{ github.head_ref }}`) pasted into its text: those reach a script through `env:`; and no unquoted value holds `: ` or ends with `:`, which YAML reads as a mapping, so that GitHub reads the whole file as no workflow (the third tooling step's `run: … --ignored combine::` did that to the nightly workflow, and only its dispatch showed it);
- **a wrapped trait has no default bodies** (since the third tooling step): `Tool`, `Launcher`, `Policy` and, since the fourth, `ModelClient`, which tests wrap to check what a request builds to: the traits listed in `WRAPPED` (`xtask/src/rules/rust_source.rs`). Each is wrapped by a type that holds another implementation and forwards to it, and a method added later with a default body is silently not forwarded by a wrapper written before it: the default runs in its place. Step 26's wrapper of `Tool` did that with `Tool::preview`, which a parallel pull request added, and a clean rebase hid it. With no defaults, adding a method fails to build until every implementation writes it; the rule keeps a default from coming back. A trait that is not where the list says is an error, not a pass. A trait that gains a wrapper joins the list;
- **a program is not started by a bare name** outside tests (since the third tooling step): `Command::new("git")`, and the same through the project's own starters (`CommandLine::new`, `Shell::new`, `brief::run`, `Pty::spawn`), whatever follows the first argument. The name is a literal, borrowed or made into a `String`, an `OsStr` or a path (`"git".to_owned()`, `String::from("git")`, `OsStr::new("git")`), or a constant or a `let` of the same file that holds one. A name that comes from another module, or is built at run time, is not seen. A bare name is looked for in the `PATH`'s relative entries, and on Windows in the working directory, so a file of that name in the workspace would run in its place (step 13a's review, R4). The harness finds a program with `cineris_base::program` and starts it by the path found. Code under `#[cfg(test)]` (a module, or a module in a file of its own) and `#[test]`, files under a `tests` folder, `xtask` (the developer's own tools, as cargo finds them) and the test kit are not checked. One call is allowed with a comment on its line or the line before: `// program by name: <the reason>`. When the rule was written, nothing on the main branch broke it. Only the files whose text calls a starter are lexed for it, and only those that may declare a test module in a file of its own for that: the rules step took 0.6 s on the reference machine with the two rules of the Rust source and without them;
- a crate's `test-hooks` feature (`cineris-tools`, `cineris-stats`, and since the seventh tooling step `cineris-base`, whose points are in [A point the library stops at](#a-point-the-library-stops-at)), and since the ninth `cineris-policy`'s `test-support` ([An engine, with sane defaults](#an-engine-with-sane-defaults)) and `cineris-tui`'s `test-kit` ("Testing the TUI"), is turned on by a dev-dependency and in no other way: not by a normal or build dependency, not by `default` or another feature of the crate that has it, and not by a feature of another crate that passes it on ("A seeded order of events", above). The test kit turns on `cineris-base`'s, and a crate's dev-dependency on itself, which asks for its own feature for its own tests, is no edge of the crate table;
- **every link inside the repository leads somewhere:** a relative link in a Markdown file names a file or a directory that is there; a link with a `#fragment` names a heading the page has, by the name GitHub gives it (the text in lower case, without punctuation, a `-` for each space, and `-1`, `-2` for a second and third heading of the same name); and a `docs/….md` path written in prose, in configuration or in a comment of the code names a file that is there. In Rust source only what follows `//` on a line is read, on a line of its own or after code: a string there is as likely to be a test's made-up file. Links to other sites are not followed;
- **the step records and the open items are where the others expect them** ([steps/README.md](steps/README.md), [outstanding/README.md](outstanding/README.md)): a folder of `docs/steps/` is a step's number in two digits with its letter, or a name in lower-case words joined by hyphens; it holds `answered.md` and `ci.md`, and the records of a step's parts (`<group>/<part>.md`, in hyphenated words, beginning with a heading, and linked, itself or its group's folder, from the step's `answered.md` or `ci.md`, since the tenth tooling step), and nothing else; each record begins with the heading its kind has, naming the step the folder is for; a file of `docs/outstanding/` is named in hyphenated words and holds the item's sentence on its first line, its fields (`- Area:`, `- Owner:`, and a `- Flaky:` for each flaky test it names) and the table's header and one row; and **the index in `docs/outstanding/README.md` is what `cargo xtask outstanding` writes from the items** (since the tenth tooling step; before it, from the fifth, the rule held a hand-written index to list every item exactly once), so that a review can see whether a finding is known without reading every file, and two pull requests that each add an item touch different files. Git merges the index line by line (`merge=union`), and whatever that gets wrong this rule finds.
- **no word of the maintainers' private setup in public text** (since the twelfth tooling step; [Public text](#public-text)): every Markdown file and workflow, step records included, is read for the words listed in `xtask/public-words.txt`;
- **`.worktreeinclude` names only files git ignores** (since the twelfth tooling step): an agent tool copies the files it lists into each new worktree, so a tracked file listed there would be copied over the checked-out one, and an untracked file not ignored would show as new in every worktree. Each entry is read as git reads a pattern (with no `/` it names a file at any depth, and it names what is under a folder), and must name no file git tracks; and it must be ignored by the root's `.gitignore`, read for its anchored and `*` patterns, its folder-only patterns and `!`, with a file under an ignored folder ignored whatever a `!` says, as git does. A `**` is not read. In a copy of the tree with no repository the rule is not run, and the files `.gitignore` names are not read as public text;
- **a pty test sends a key through `send_key`** (since the twelfth tooling step; [Testing the TUI](#keys-through-send_key)): in test code, a call of a sender whose literals, bindings or arrays of bytes hold an escape sequence for a key fails.

**The rules read the files git tracks** (since the third tooling step): what a pull request holds. A scratch folder in a working tree, with a build of its own full of absolute paths, once failed them with 3,963 findings. A new file is read once it is added, and a link may lead to one before. In a copy of the tree with no `.git`, every file is read except those under `.git` and any `target` directory.

`cargo xtask links` runs the rules about text alone: the links, the step records, public text and `.worktreeinclude`. They read text files and ask cargo nothing, so they answer at once: under a tenth of a second for the 137 pages there are.

`cargo xtask check` also has a `generated` step: the protocol's JSON Schema and TypeScript declarations in `docs/protocol/` must be what the types in `cineris-protocol` generate. A test makes the same comparison on both OSes. `cargo xtask schema` regenerates them.

**The configuration's generated files** are `docs/configuration/schema.json` and `docs/configuration/reference.md`, generated from the settings declared in `cineris-config`. They are checked by a test in that crate, on both OSes, and not by the `generated` step: `xtask` is built before every CI job does anything, and checking them there would make it build the configuration crate and its TOML parser each time. `cargo xtask gen` writes them, by running that test in its writing mode.

### Public text

The docs, the step records and the workflows are public text: anyone reads them, and nothing in them depends on how the maintainers happen to run their sessions. The maintainers' own machines, scripts and way of working are in their private notes; the docs say the general thing ("the reference machine", "locally", "the maintainers", "a CI wait", the plain `cargo xtask` command). Sessions that work from those notes wrote the private words back in after the docs were made public-facing, so since the twelfth tooling step `cargo xtask rules` checks for them.

- **The words** are in a data file, [`xtask/public-words.txt`](../xtask/public-words.txt): each phrase, matched in any case or (a `cased` line) in exactly its case, and the public alternative the failure names. A phrase that begins or ends with a letter or a digit matches only as a whole word there, with an `s` after it taken too (a plural), except for an `exact` line, whose plural is an ordinary verb ("each test locks its folder"). A paragraph is read as one line, its runs of white space as one space and each line's leading `>` (a quote) or `#` (a workflow's comment) left out, so that a phrase broken across two lines is found. A change to the list is one line.
- **What is read:** every Markdown file git tracks and every workflow. A step record is public text: the folders written before the rule are named on `history` lines of the same file and keep their words, and every later record is held to it.
- **A product use** of the same word (the budgets' Desktop column, the class of the second machine) is a longer phrase on an `allow` line, with its reason: a hit inside it passes. `AGENTS.md`, which every agent session loads, takes no `allow`: it names none of the words at all.
- **What a failure says:** the file and line, the word, and what to say instead:

  ```text
  docs/measurements/README.md:21: `PHRASE` is a word of the maintainers' private setup, not of the product. Say ALTERNATIVE instead. If this is the product's own use of the word, add the longer phrase as an `allow` line to xtask/public-words.txt.
  ```

- **Where the list came from:** what pull request #106, the public docs pass, took out of the docs and did not put back. Words of the product too ("worktree", "WSL", "sub-agent", the names of other agents) are not on it; Rust comments are not read.
- **It is not the owner guard** ([The owner guard](#the-owner-guard)), which looks for the owner's own details from a file outside the repository. These words are not secret, only not the product's.
- **What it costs:** it is part of `links` and `rules`, reading the Markdown those already read: a few milliseconds in the blocking lint job.

### Adding a repo rule

What the rules of the twelfth tooling step and the rename's label check ran into, for the next rule:

- **Where it goes.** A module of `xtask/src/rules/`, a function from texts to `Violation`s, so that its tests need no repository. It is called from `rules/mod.rs`: from `file_rules` for a rule of one file alone, from `check_repository` for one that reads the Rust sources together, or from `check_docs` for one that reads only text, so that `cargo xtask links` runs it too and asks cargo nothing.
- **It must not match its own docs.** The page that describes a rule is read by the rules. Say what it looks for without writing a match: name the data file that lists the words, or describe them ("the second machine as a kind"), and give a failure's example with a placeholder. A rule that reads Rust is scoped so that its own module, whose tests hold the very text it fails on, is outside what it reads (`crates/`, not `xtask/`), or names the file it skips.
- **It must allow its record.** The step's record describes the rule, and is read by it when the rule reads records. Word the record so that it passes, as above, or use the rule's own way out (an `allow` line, a marked line) with its reason. Run `cargo xtask links` after writing the record, not only after the code.
- **Its breaks.** A list of its own, `mutations/<step>.breaks`, whose name passes the rename's check (`cargo xtask rename --check`: a name with a word from before the rename is refused like any other path). `cargo xtask mutate --check` says whether each break applies: a `find` that ends in a comma does not (rustfmt moves it), and an `in:` that names two items must name the module too (`children::reaped`). Then `cargo xtask mutate <step>`. A break that survives because a second guard holds what it breaks is `expect: survives`, with the second guard named in its `rule`.
- **What it costs** the lint job is measured and written down: only files that can match are read or lexed (a cheap text test first), and the rule says so in its section.

### Rebuilding the generated files

`cargo xtask regen` (since the sixth tooling step) is for after a rebase that met another branch's changes to a generated file, where a merge of the text is not the file the merged sources produce (step 18 settled such conflicts by hand in the snapshots, the screens and the schema). Settle the sources, take either side of each generated file, run it. One step for each generator, all of them run even when one fails, each timed:

| Step | Writes | By |
|---|---|---|
| `outstanding` | `docs/outstanding/README.md` | `outstanding`, from the open items' files (since the tenth tooling step) |
| `protocol` | `docs/protocol/` | `schema`'s writer |
| `configuration` | `docs/configuration/` | the test of `cineris-config` that checks them, with `CINERIS_UPDATE_GENERATED=1` |
| `screens` | the reference screens and their text copies in `docs/architecture/tui-look/` | `tui-screens --write` (before the snapshots: one of their tests holds the text copies to the screens, and since the sixth tooling step `--write` also puts a text copy right that is out of step with its screen alone, as a merge that took one side of each file leaves it) |
| `snapshots` | the snapshots of `cineris-tui`, `cineris-llm`, `cineris-index` and `cineris-config`, and the requests of the hand-written provider fixtures | the tiers 1 and 2 tests of those four crates, with `CINERIS_UPDATE_SNAPSHOTS=1` |

It then lists the files that differ from `HEAD` in those places (a change to a snapshot is a change in what users see: it is read like code), and runs `links`, which writes nothing and checks what `regen` cannot write. **Left out, with `regen --list` saying so:** the tables of `docs/steps/<step>/ci.md`, which are a CI run's measurements (`ci-times RUN --write`, which needs `gh` and a run id); the toolbox and the recorded provider fixtures, which are a paid API's answers (`record`); the golden transcript, which is not edited to make a test pass; and `Cargo.lock` and the break lists. **A test holds the lists to the code:** every `CINERIS_UPDATE_*` variable found in the code is run by one step or named as left to another command, and every place a step writes is a place the repository has, so a writing mode added later cannot be forgotten. On a tree that is right it changes nothing: measured on the reference machine, 46 s, of which the snapshots are 24 s, the screens 15 s and the configuration 6 s.

### The copy of `xtask` that still runs

In the middle of a change to `xtask` itself `cargo xtask replace` cannot run, because cargo builds `xtask` first and the build fails: the command that would edit the code that does not build is the one that cannot be used. Every `xtask` command that ends well keeps the binary that ran it in `target/xtask-last-good/xtask` (`xtask.exe`), beside `xtask.stamp`: the binary's size and modification time (which say whether it is the one already kept, so a run that changes nothing copies nothing), when it was copied, and the commit with a note when `xtask/` held changes that were not committed. A runner keeps none (`GITHUB_ACTIONS` is set): nobody uses its build again.

- **`./x replace …` and `./x links` build first, and run the copy when the build fails.** A cargo that is not there is not a failed build: that is reported as it is. On Windows `cargo xtask` cannot fall back; run `target\xtask-last-good\xtask.exe replace …`.
- **The copy says it is old,** on standard error, on every run: when it was built, how long ago, from which commit, and which files of `xtask/` are newer than it (or that none are, and the build is broken for another reason).
- **It offers `replace` and `links` only.** Every other command is a check or a run whose answer depends on the code as it is, and a copy would answer for the code as it was.
- **It never overwrites the copy in place:** a new one is written beside it and renamed over it, and where the system will not replace a program that is running (Windows) the old one is moved aside first, which it does allow. The copy does not refresh itself.

## Dependency hygiene

- **Licences.** `cargo deny` allows: MIT, Apache-2.0 (with or without the LLVM exception), BSD-2-Clause, BSD-3-Clause, ISC, Zlib, 0BSD, BSL-1.0, CC0-1.0, Unlicense, Unicode-3.0, Unicode-DFS-2016, and CDLA-Permissive-2.0 (the certificate data). Everything else fails the check. The planned dependency set was checked against this list when these docs were written: all of it passes. The one copyleft licence seen anywhere in the candidates (MPL-2.0, through one of the scripting engines that was not chosen) is not in the planned set.
- **Sources.** crates.io only. No git dependencies.
- **Bans.** OpenSSL bindings, native-TLS, and anything that needs CMake or NASM. Duplicate versions of the same crate are warnings that the weekly update pull request is expected to clear.
- **Advisories.** Checked nightly and on every lockfile change. A new advisory against an unchanged lockfile must not block unrelated pull requests, so it is not in the blocking set.
- **Unused dependencies.** Checked nightly, with cargo-machete. It reads the sources and needs no build, so it is a heuristic: a dependency used only in a way it cannot see is declared in the manifest, under `[package.metadata.cargo-machete]`, with the reason. **For now it looks at third-party crates only.** The skeleton declares the edges between workspace crates before the code that uses them exists, so the workspace's own crates are on an ignore list in the root `Cargo.toml`. A name comes off the list when the crates that depend on it have code, and the list goes once the first release's crates are built.
- **The dependency list.** `docs/dependencies.md` names every direct dependency, its owner crate, its purpose and its measured cost. A tier 0 check fails when it and the manifests disagree.
- **Crate count.** The number of crates in a release build is recorded on the main branch by the `main` workflow (every crate cargo compiles for the binary, ours included, with build dependencies and without development ones). An increase of more than five in one change needs a line in the pull request saying why.
- **Updates.** One grouped pull request a week.
- **The lockfile is committed.**

## Local development loop

The same budgets apply on a local machine.

| Action | Budget | How | Measured for the skeleton, step 2 |
|---|---|---|---|
| `cargo check` after an edit in a leaf crate | Under 2 s | Small crates; heavy dependencies in leaf crates. | 0.04 s |
| Rebuild and relink the binary after an edit | Under 5 s | A tiny binary crate. Measured floor for relinking the full dependency set: 0.4 s on the reference machine, 0.5 s on a Linux runner, 1.2 to 1.9 s on a Windows runner. | 0.1 s |
| No-op build | Under 0.5 s | | 0.03 s |
| Tiers 1 and 2 | Under 30 s | `cargo xtask test --profile fast`. | 0.2 s |
| The static checks | | `cargo xtask check`. | 0.3 s |
| Everything that blocks a pull request | Under 3 min | `cargo xtask ci` runs exactly what CI runs. | 0.7 s warm; 3.6 s from an empty build directory on 4 cores |

The measurements are from the reference machine the earlier spikes used, with warm build directories unless it says otherwise. The crates are empty, so these are floors: what the tooling itself costs before there is any code.

The commands, all through `cargo xtask` (or `./x`):

| Command | Runs |
|---|---|
| `check` | The static checks: `fmt`, `rules`, `breaks`, `generated`, `clippy`, `no-scripts`, `deny`. All of them run even when one fails, and the time of each is printed. |
| `test` | All tests. Arguments go to nextest: `--profile fast` for tiers 1 and 2, `-p cineris-agent` for one crate, and then only that crate is built and run. `--dependents -p CRATE` adds every workspace crate whose tests can see a change to it: built on it through normal and build dependencies, to any depth, or using such a crate as a dev-dependency (a dev-dependency is not passed on). Run it after a change of behaviour. |
| `ci` | What the blocking jobs run: `check`, then the tests in the `ci` profiles, then the tier budgets. |
| `ci --tiers` | The tests as CI builds them, and each tier's time and slowest tests ("A CI run's test times"); `--like-runner` estimates what a hosted runner would say ("What a runner would say, from a local run"). |
| `ci --tiers --compare` | Two runs' tiers side by side ("A CI run's test times"). |
| `ci-times` | A run's job and tier tables, for a step's `ci.md`, written into it with `--write`; `--ab` for a `try` A/B run's, and the pull request's CI section. |
| `corpus` | The pinned corpus of real code that `perf_` tests measure: `fetch` it, `check` or print its `path`, `pin` a new one ("A tree of real code to measure"). |
| `try` | What the `try` workflow runs ("A run on the runners without a pull request", "Running an experiment on the runners"). |
| `ab` | Two trees' tests run in turn on one machine and compared ("Two trees on one runner"). |
| `combine` | This branch on top of open pull requests, checked by `ci` in a clone ("This branch on top of others"). |
| `stress` | The tests of the crates named, many times over, each time under the next seed, optionally squeezed onto a few cores beside busy loops ("Running the tests many times over"). |
| `mutate` | The breaks of `mutations/*.breaks`, on copies of the tree, two or `--jobs N` at a time ("Breaking the code on purpose"). |
| `replace` | An edit of Rust code found by its tokens, or with `--text` of any file by its exact text; `--script FILE` makes many edits across many files, all of them or none ([CONTRIBUTING.md](../CONTRIBUTING.md), "Editing from a script"). When `xtask` does not build, `./x replace` and `./x links` run the last binary that ran a command to its end, kept in `target/xtask-last-good/` ("The copy of `xtask` that still runs", below). |
| `scratch-test` | One throwaway test file run against a crate's integration tests, in a copy of the tree; in a cargo profile of its own and with its ignored tests, for a measurement ("A throwaway test"). |
| `api-diff` | The public items added, removed and changed between a ref and the working tree, with their signatures ("The public API between two trees"). |
| `owner-guard` | The owner guard: CI's form, `--pre-push` for the git hook, `--unpushed` by hand ("The owner guard"). |
| `decide` | The permission engine's decision on a command line ("Asking the permission engine"). |
| `links` | The part of `rules` that is about the docs: links inside the repository, and where the step records are. |
| `schema` | Regenerates the protocol's JSON Schema and TypeScript declarations in `docs/protocol/`. `schema --check` only compares. |
| `gen` | Regenerates every generated file: what `schema` writes, and the configuration schema and reference in `docs/configuration/`. |
| `regen` | Rebuilds every generated file the tree can rebuild, with no network and no key: what `gen` writes, the reference screens and every snapshot; then lists what differs from `HEAD` and runs `links` ("Rebuilding the generated files", above). `--list` says what it covers and what it leaves out. |
| `size`, `unused`, `advisories` | What the non-blocking workflows run: the `dist` build with its size and crate count (`--check` fails over the budget, `--target` names the target), cargo-machete, and `cargo deny check advisories` of the workspace's lockfile and of each package built outside it (`fuzz/`). |
